Ege Journal of Education 2015 (16) 1: 1-12 Psychometric Properties of the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale (ÇGPSÖ-12): Validity and Reliability Study 1 Ege Journal of Education 2015 (16) 1: 1-12 Psychometric Properties of the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale (ÇGPSÖ-12): Validity and Reliability Study Gökmen ARSLAN1 Date of Submission: 26.11.2014 Date of Acceptance: 16.06.2015 The aim of this study is to examine the psychometric properties of the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale. A total of 256 students studying in middle and high schools in the city center of Isparta participated in the study. The ages of the participants ranged from 11 to 16 years. The mean age was 13.05 (SD = .97). Of the participants, 44% were female and 56% were male students. The results of the exploratory and confirmatory factor analyses showed that the scale consists of a single factor that accounts for 51.28% of the total variance. In terms of criterion validity, relationships with positive and negative affect and self-efficacy were examined, and it was found that there was a positive and significant relationship between the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale and self-efficacy and positive affect, while there was a negative and significant relationship with negative affect. As part of the reliability study, the internal consistency coefficient of the scale was calculated. The Cronbach’s alpha coefficient was found to be .91. These findings indicate that the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale can be used to assess psychological resilience in children and adolescents in our country.
Keywords: Psychological resilience, adolescence, reliability, validity.

The Cronbach alpha coefficient of CYRM was .91.
Ege Education Journal 2015 (16) 1: 1-12 Psychometric Properties of the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale (ÇGPSÖ-12): Validity and Reliability Study 3 Introduction The period of adolescence is generally addressed in the literature as a transitional phase from childhood to adulthood (Steinberg, 2007). The changes experienced by individuals during this period bring about certain adaptation problems, while also providing opportunities for new experiences. In this process, individuals may be faced with various experiences such as abuse, neglect, or divorce, which can negatively affect their developmental processes and well-being (Santrock, 2012). The healthy development of an individual despite these difficulties is referred to as psychological resilience in the literature (Sameroff, 2005).
Psychological resilience, one of the key concepts of positive psychology— which focuses on individuals’ positive experiences, emotions, personality traits, and strengths—has been shown in the literature to exhibit considerable heterogeneity both conceptually and methodologically (Nasvytienė, La zdauskas, and Leonavičienė, 2012). Psychological resilience, defined as a dynamic process that encompasses positive adaptation in the face of adversity, involves two important conditions. The first is exposure to a significant threat or serious distress, and the second is the ability to achieve positive adaptation despite facing major developmental threats and traumas (Luthar, 2005; Luthar, Cicchetti, and Becker, 2000; Masten, 2001; Masten and Coatsworth, 1998). Here, positive outcomes emerge despite threats or risks to the individual’s development or adaptation (Masten, 2001). Although it is difficult to speak of a single definition, based on the definitions provided, psychological resilience can be said to refer to an individual’s capacity to adapt and continue their developmental process in the face of adverse experiences.
The multidimensional and complex structure of the concept of psychological resilience presents significant challenges in evaluating the construct and in developing measurement tools for its assessment (Liebenberg, Ungar, and LeBlanc, 2013). It can be said that this situation necessitates the use of different measurement tools to identify the individual, familial, social, and cultural qualities of resilient individuals. In addition, examining the characteristics of resilient individuals holds a crucial place in terms of preventive and intervention studies. Therefore, it appears that there is a need for new measurement tools to assess psychological resilience in children and adolescents, as indicated in the literature. Furthermore, when the literature is reviewed, it is evident that there are only a limited number of measurement tools available in our country for assessing psychological resilience in children and adolescents. For example, the Psychological Resilience and Adolescent Development Scale, adapted by Gizir and Aydın (2006), was developed to identify protective factors related to psychological resilience. This scale, which consists of internal and external protective factors, includes 56 items.
Apart from this study, no other measurement tool aimed at assessing resilience in children and adolescents could be found in the literature.
In conclusion, the aim of this study is to examine the psychometric properties of the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale. The limited number of measurement tools available in our country for assessing psychological resilience in children and adolescents creates the need for new studies. Additionally, the fact that existing measurement tools contain a large number of items can pose limitations for researchers working with large samples. Therefore, it can be said that new measurement tools related to psychological resilience will fill an important gap in the literature.
Method Study Group The research data were obtained from 256 students attending various high schools and middle schools in the city center of Isparta. The participants’ ages ranged from 11 to 16 years, with a mean age of 13.05 (SD = .97). Of the participants, 44% were female and 56% were male students. After obtaining the necessary permissions, data collection instruments were administered in randomly selected classrooms at randomly chosen schools. Data Collection Instruments Personal Information Form: This form was prepared and administered by the researcher to determine demographic variables such as gender and age.
Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale: The original form of the scale, developed based on data collected from eleven different countries, consists of 28 items, three subscales, and eight subdimensions. The measurement tool was developed using both quantitative and qualitative methods from a socio-ecological perspective (Liebenberg, Ungar, and Van de Vijver, 2012). The short form study of the scale was conducted by Liebenberg, Ungar, and LeBlanc (2013), and as a result of two different studies, a structure consisting of 12 items was obtained. The factor loadings of the scale range between .39 and .88, and its internal consistency coefficient was found to be .84. The measurement tool uses a five-point Likert structure, ranging from “Describes me completely (5)” to “Does not describe me at all (1).” Higher scores indicate a higher level of resilience.
Self-Efficacy Scale: The Self-Efficacy Scale for Children was developed by Muris (2001) to measure the social, academic, and emotional self-efficacy of adolescents aged 12–19.
The Turkish adaptation of the scale was carried out by Telef (2011). When examining the internal consistency coefficients of the Self-Efficacy Scale for Children, the coefficient was calculated as .86 for the overall scale, .84 for academic self-efficacy, .64 for social self-efficacy, and .78 for emotional self-efficacy. The test-retest reliability coefficients of the scale ranged from .75 to .89. The General Self-Efficacy Scale was used for criterion-related validity.
The Pearson product-moment correlation between the scores obtained from the two scales was found to be .57.
The Self-Efficacy Scale for Children is a five-point Likert-type scale (1 = not at all, 5 = very well), with a maximum possible score of 105 and a minimum score of 21.
A high score on the scale indicates a high level of self-efficacy in children, while a low score indicates a low level of self-efficacy in children. Positive and Negative Affect Scale: The Positive and Negative Affect Scale was developed by Watson, Clark, and Tellegen to assess individuals' positive and negative emotions.
The validity and reliability study in our country was conducted by Gençöz (2000).
The scale consists of 10 positive and 10 negative affect items, each of which is rated on a scale from 1 to 5.
In the adaptation study of the scale, Cronbach’s alpha internal consistency coefficient was found to be .83 for Negative Affect and .86 for Positive Affect. The obtained positive and negative affect scores can be used together or separately.
Data Analysis Process Within the scope of language validity for the measurement instrument, the original English form of the 12-item scale, with permission from the researcher who developed the scale, was first translated into Turkish by the researchers, and then back into English by two faculty members from the field of educational sciences. A third faculty member from the field, proficient in both languages, compared the translations and finalized the scale. The measurement instruments were administered at the beginning of the 2014–2015 academic year to volunteers among students from various middle and high schools. Initially, the necessary permissions to conduct the research were obtained from the provincial directorate of national education. Subsequently, the implementation process began in randomly selected schools. Prior to administration, participants were briefly informed about the purpose of the research and the measurement instruments. Data collection took approximately one week and the administration lasted 30 minutes. After transferring the collected data to the computer environment, the necessary assumptions for the analyses were examined. First, incorrect and missing data were reviewed with the help of frequency distributions, and the necessary corrections were made for the erroneous data.
Missing values can be tolerated if they are below 5% and randomly distributed (Acuna and Rodriguez, 2004). Missing values were examined, and 68 datasets with more than 5% missing data were excluded. For missing values below 5%, values were imputed using the arithmetic mean (series mean) to avoid conditions that might affect the normal distribution status of the dataset or the analysis results (Seçer, 2013). In the next step, outliers and the assumption of multivariate normality in the dataset were examined using Mahalanobis distance values, and 12 datasets were removed due to being extreme outliers. Additionally, skewness and kurtosis coefficients were checked for the assumption of univariate normality. It was found that the skewness and kurtosis values met the normality assumption within the range of +1 and -1 (Can, 2013; Morgan, Leech, Gloeckner, and Barrett, 2004). The results of confirmatory factor analysis for the measurement instrument were evaluated within the framework of fit indices, including chi-square (χ2), χ2/df ratio, goodness of fit index (GFI), normed fit index (NFI), comparative fit index (CFI), standardized root mean square residuals (SRMR), incremental fit index (IFI), and root mean square error of approximation (RMSEA). A χ2/df ratio below 3 indicates an acceptable fit, while RMSEA and SRMR values equal to or less than 0.05 indicate a perfect fit, and values of 0.08 or below are considered acceptable. GFI, CFI, TLI, NFI, and RFI take values between 0 and 1; values of 0.95 and above indicate a perfect fit, while values between 0.90 and 0.94 indicate acceptable fit (Hoe, 2008; Kline, 2011; Raykov and Marcoulides, 2006; Şimşek, 2007). The suitability of the model formed in this study was evaluated within the framework of the fit indices mentioned above. The validity and reliability study of the measurement instrument was tested using SPSS 22 and AMOS 22 software at a significance level of .05.
Findings The validity and reliability study of the measurement instrument consists of two stages. After obtaining the necessary permissions from the author, the measurement instrument was translated into Turkish. Subsequently, the factor structure of the instrument was examined using exploratory factor analysis (EFA) and confirmatory factor analysis (CFA).
Ege Education Journal 2015 (16) 1: 1-12 Psychometric Properties of the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale (ÇGPSÖ-12): Validity and Reliability Study 7 Findings Regarding Validity Before conducting factor analysis, partial correlations between items and the suitability of the correlation matrix for factor analysis were examined using the Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin (KMO) coefficient and the Bartlett test. The results indicated that the Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin value was .92, and the result of the Bartlett test (χ2 = 1533.522, df = 66, p < .000) was found to be significant. Accordingly, it was accepted that the data were derived from a multivariate normal distribution and that the partial correlations between items and the correlation matrix were suitable for factor analysis. To examine the construct validity and factor structure of the scale, exploratory factor analysis was conducted, and principal components analysis was used as the factoring technique. In determining the construct validity of the scale, factors with eigenvalues above 1.00 according to Kaiser normalization were considered as criteria.

It is observed that the factor loadings of the twelve items of the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale range between .54 and .81. This can be considered important, as it indicates a high representational power of the items in the scale. The findings regarding the item-total correlations and factor loadings of the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale are presented in Table 1.
Figure 1.
Standardized parameter values for the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale When the CFA results for the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale (CYPRS) are examined, it is found that the t-values and χ2 value are significant at the .01 level. When the χ2 and degrees of freedom values for the fit indices are compared, it is seen that the χ2/df (105.498/52) ratio is 2.03. Examination of the RMSEA value reveals that it has a fit index of .060 (90%, .045-.080). Looking at the other fit indices, GFI is .94, NFI is .94, CFI is .97, and IFI is .97. Regarding the standardized RMR value, the fit index is .039. As a result, the confirmatory factor analysis indicates that the 12-item model demonstrates an adequate level of fit.
The standardized parameter values for the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale (CYPRS) obtained as a result of the CFA are presented in Figure 1.
Finally, the criterion validity of the measurement instrument was examined in relation to positive and negative affect and self-efficacy. Significant correlations were found: .57 with positive affect, -.39 with negative affect, and .45 with self-efficacy for the CYPRS. In light of these results, it can be said that the scale demonstrates criterion validity.
Findings Regarding Reliability Within the scope of the reliability study, the Cronbach’s alpha reliability coefficient was calculated for the entire scale. The internal consistency coefficient for the first sample was found to be .91. These values can be considered quite good for a measurement instrument consisting of twelve items. Additionally, as shown in Table 1, the item-total correlation values of the scale range between .45 and .79. The obtained values indicate that this scale is a reliable measurement tool for assessing psychological resilience in children and adolescents.
Conclusion, Discussion, and Recommendations In this study, the Turkish adaptation of the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale, whose validity and reliability study was conducted by Liebenberg, Ungar, and LeBlanc (2013), was carried out. As part of the validity study, the factor structure was examined. The analysis results showed that the 12 items of the scale were gathered under a single factor. Criterion validity was established through correlations with positive and negative affect and self-efficacy. In the reliability study, the internal consistency level was examined. The findings obtained in this study indicate that the scale can be used as a reliable and valid measurement instrument for children and adolescents in our country as well. The findings related to the exploratory factor analysis revealed that the scale has a structure explaining 51.28% of the total variance, with item factor loadings ranging from .54 to .81. In the original form of the scale, it was found that the factor load values of the items ranged between .39 and .88 (Liebenberg, Ungar, and LeBlanc, 2013).
In unidimensional measurement instruments, a variance of 30% or above is considered acceptable. Additionally, factor loadings of .45 or higher are regarded as good (Büyüköztürk, 2010). The findings obtained in this study indicated that the scale has items with high factor loadings, demonstrating strong representation of the construct. For criterion validity, positive and negative affect, as well as self-efficacy, were used. Significant correlations were found: .57 with positive affect, -.39 with negative affect, and .45 with self-efficacy on the Child and Youth Psychological Resilience Scale (CYPRS).
Studies have reported that individuals with high self-efficacy tend to have higher levels of psychological resilience (Terzi, 2006; Keye and Pidgeon, 2013), and that resilient individuals possess more positive emotions (Ong, Zautra, and Reid, 2010; Tugade and Fredrickson, 2004). In this context, these findings can be said to support the criterion validity of the scale. The findings related to the reliability study of the scale showed that the internal consistency coefficients calculated for the subscales and for the overall scale were at an adequate level. In the first study, the internal consistency coefficient was calculated as .91. In the study by Liebenberg, Ungar, and LeBlanc (2013), the internal consistency coefficient of the scale was reported as .84. The internal consistency coefficients obtained in the two studies conducted within the scope of the present research are consistent with the results of the original study.
In conclusion, the results obtained indicate that the measurement instrument can be used to determine psychological resilience in children and adolescents in our country. The small number of items in the instrument can be considered an advantage for both researchers and participants in practical applications of future studies. Additionally, it is thought that the instrument will make a significant contribution to school psychological counselors in preventive and intervention studies to be developed for students experiencing adverse events during the educational process. Finally, the validity and reliability study of the scale was conducted on a normal population.
This situation can be considered a limitation. Therefore, it is believed that conducting studies with risk groups would also be beneficial.

Ege Education Journal 2017(18) 1: 408-429 Digital Literacy Scale: Adaptation Study into Turkish 408 Digital Literacy Scale: Adaptation Study into Turkish 1 Nazire Burçin HAMUTOĞLU 2, Özlem CANAN GÜNGÖREN 3, Gülden KAYA UYANIK4, Duygu GÜR ERDOĞAN5 Date Received: 15.11.2016 Date Accepted: 07.05.2017 In this study, the adaptation of the “Digital Literacy Scale,” developed by Ng in 2012 and consisting of 17 items, into Turkish was carried out. The original scale has a four-factor structure. These factors are named as attitude, technical, cognitive, and social. The adaptation process into Turkish began with linguistic equivalence; three experts who were highly proficient in both English and Turkish translated the scale from English to Turkish, and another three experts translated it from Turkish to English. Based on the experts’ opinions, a Turkish trial form was created, and both the English and Turkish versions were administered to 37 academics proficient in both languages, with two weeks between each administration. The correlation coefficients between the two forms were found to be significant. After establishing linguistic equivalence, EFA, CFA, and reliability analyses were conducted. Data obtained from 185 students were used for EFA, and data from a separate group of 210 students were used for CFA. The first-order CFA showed that the sample size was sufficient for factor analysis, and the second-order CFA demonstrated that digital literacy is a significant predictor of the latent variables. The reliability of the scale was assessed using internal consistency coefficients (Cronbach's Alpha) and test-retest methods. As a result of the findings, it was observed that the Turkish form of the scale has a valid and reliable structure.
Keywords: digital literacy, scale, reliability, validity.

Dr., Sakarya University, dgur@sakarya.edu.tr Ege Education Journal 2017(18) 1: 408-429 Digital Literacy Scale: Adaptation Study into Turkish 409 Adapting Digital Literacy Scale into Turkish Submitted on 15.11.2016 Accepted on 07.05.2017 Abstract In this study, it is aimed to adapt the “Digital Literacy Scale,” developed by Ng (2012) and consisting of 17 items, into Turkish.
Ege Education Journal 2017(18) 1: 408-429 Digital Literacy Scale: Turkish Adaptation Study 410 Introduction Today, many new technologies are emerging every day. Among the foremost of these are wireless internet technologies and mobile technologies. Especially with internet technologies, numerous websites that facilitate daily life and enable individuals to achieve various goals have become an integral part of our lives. Recently, the widespread use of personal internet access in education, health, business, and the private sector has become notable.
It is observed that individuals use the internet for a variety of purposes such as using social networks, joining specific groups, reading news, expressing their opinions, listening to music, watching movies, and similar activities.
However, not all of these web pages possess the same degree of reliability.
However, it is also possible to encounter different user profiles serving various purposes while browsing the internet.
Individuals browsing these web pages may encounter online theft, fraud, misguidance, cyberbullying, sharing of personal information, exploitation by malicious software, etc.
They may be exposed to undesirable behaviors (Telecommunications Communication Presidency, TIB, 2012).
Similarly, the reliability of the source of a news article we read is important in determining our attitude and stance on that subject.
Like everyone else, individuals—especially adolescents and those with limited internet experience—need to be cautious when interacting with the user profiles they encounter online, the information they obtain from the websites they browse, and the people they meet on social networks. This situation necessitates that individuals possess digital literacy skills.
In addition to efforts aimed at increasing safe internet usage through different technologies and acquiring skills such as being able to determine the accuracy of the information obtained, digital literacy skills are also considered important for individuals in solving the problems they encounter.
Otherwise, in today's world where information spreads very rapidly, misinformation with low reliability, unverifiable accuracy, and intended for provocation can lead to misleading outcomes for individuals and society. Çubukçu and Bayzan (2013) state that the greatest factor in the emergence of internet risks is the production and sharing of false, exploitative, and misleading information. In this regard, discussing the importance of individuals' digital literacy skills becomes inevitable (Ainley, Schulz, and Fraillon, 2016; Mohammadyari and Singh, 2015).
Ege Education Journal 2017(18) 1: 408–429 Digital Literacy Scale: Adaptation to Turkish 411 Digital literacy is one of the essential dimensions that constitute the most important pillar of digital citizenship (Ribble, 2011). There are efforts to define the skills that digital environment users should possess (Burnett and McKinley, 1998; Zins, 2000). Digital literacy requires possessing the skills to use various technologies correctly, to access, produce, and share accurate information, and to utilize technology in learning and teaching processes. Çubukçu and Bayzan (2013) consider the significance of digital literacy skills in that the FATIH Project will hold an important place in the school education process, and that the introduction of the Media Literacy course as an elective in schools is regarded as an opportunity.
In the Digital Literacy report prepared for the European Union regarding digital environments, it is stated that “students develop their creativity and self-expression skills” (Tornero, 2004).
Considering the technological developments experienced, it is observed that there is a need for a measurement tool to determine the level of digital literacy skills in our country. To serve this purpose, it is planned to adapt the digital literacy scale developed by Ng (2012) into our language and culture. For 21st-century individuals, digital literacy skills such as accessing reliable information, safe internet use, and conscious internet use are considered important.
In the literature, there are studies related to conducting reading and writing using digital technologies as well as studies that highlight the importance of digital literacy in higher education (Özbay and Özdemir, 2014; Prior, Mazanov, Meacheam, Heaslip, and Hanson, 2016; Radovanović, Hogan, and Lalić, 2015; Tüzel and Tok, 2013). Sönmez and Gül (2014) emphasize that in order for individuals to solve the digital problems they encounter in the digital age, they need to possess cognitive skills of digital literacy. Çubukçu and Bayzan (2013) state that digital literacy has begun to become even more important than conventional literacy. In light of these studies, the skills of teachers, who are responsible for imparting digital literacy abilities in education, gain significant importance.
Statistics on prospective teachers’ access to the internet and their purposes for using it in Türkiye indicate that the vast majority of prospective teachers (86.9%) use the internet (Akkoyunlu and Yılmaz, 2005; Atav, Akkoyunlu, and Sağlam, 2006). Despite this intensive use of the internet, the absence of a measurement tool to reveal prospective teachers’ digital literacy skills makes this study significant. In this study, the adaptation of the Digital Literacy Scale into Turkish is considered important, as it will serve not only to determine the digital literacy skills of prospective teachers but also to provide a basis for activities aimed at developing these skills.
Method Study Group The validity and reliability studies of the Digital Literacy Scale were conducted during the Spring semester of the 2015-2016 academic year with students from different departments of the Faculty of Education at Sakarya University. Although the sample of the study consisted of four different working groups, a total of 485 individuals participated. The first group consisted of 37 academics—20 (54%) women and 17 (46%) men—and linguistic equivalence studies were conducted with this group. The second group comprised 185 people—108 (58.4%) women and 77 (41.6%) men—and data from this group were used for exploratory factor analysis and internal consistency studies. The third group consisted of 210 people—118 (56.2%) women and 92 (43.8%) men—and confirmatory factor analysis results were obtained from this group. The fourth group included 53 people—20 (37.7%) women and 33 (62.3%) men—and test-retest analyses of the scale were conducted using data obtained from this group.
Data Collection Tool: Digital Literacy Scale The Digital Literacy Scale (DLS) was developed by Ng (2012) and consists of 17 items and 4 factors (attitude, technical, cognitive, and social). The original scale was administered to prospective teachers aged between 18 and 30 studying at a university in Australia. The scale does not contain any reverse-scored items and uses a 5-point Likert scale ranging from Strongly Agree (5) to Strongly Disagree (1). Procedure The process of adapting the Digital Literacy Scale into Turkish began with obtaining permission to adapt the scale from its developer, Ng, in the original language. Once the necessary permissions were obtained, the translation process from the source language, English, to the target language, Turkish, was initiated. The most important step in adapting a measurement tool from one society to another is the translation process from the source language to the target language (Geisinger, 1994). After it was confirmed that both versions were sufficiently similar, the final draft of the scale became ready for implementation. The scale was made adequate for language validity. Linguistic equivalence studies were carried out using the bilingual design method with 37 academicians. For construct validity, the collected data were examined using data preprocessing methods (Oğuzlar, 2003). The construct validity study of the DLS was conducted using exploratory factor analysis (EFA) and confirmatory factor analysis (CFA) methods. Certain hypothetical criteria are required for factor analysis studies. For this purpose, in EFA, assumptions such as normality, linearity, removal of outliers, and identification of missing values were examined, while in CFA, all required hypothetical criteria were investigated along with the adequacy levels of goodness-of-fit indices. It was observed that the data collected for the adaptation study of the DLS met the requisite adequacy for construct validity, and thus, a construct validity study was conducted. The reliability level of the scale was examined using Cronbach's alpha internal consistency and test-retest methods. All statistical analyses used in the adaptation studies of the DLS were conducted using the SPSS 20.0 and LISREL 8.7 software packages.
Findings Linguistic Equivalence The linguistic equivalence study of the Digital Literacy Scale (DLS) was conducted with 37 participants. According to the findings, the correlation coefficients between the scores of the Turkish and original English forms were found to be .89 for the whole scale, .80 for the Attitude subscale, .93 for the Technical subscale, .73 for the Cognitive subscale, and .73 for the Social subscale. All relationships are significant at the 0.01 significance level. The findings regarding the correlations of the items in the Turkish and English forms of the DLS are presented in Table 1.
Table 1.
Linguistic Equivalence Findings of the DLS by Item Item No r Item No r Item No r Item No r DLS 1 .42* DLS 6 .50* DLS 11 .81* DLS 16 .74* DLS 2 .59* DLS 7 .72* DLS 12 .62* DLS 17 .55* DLS 3 .49* DLS 8 .75* DLS 13 .78* DLS 4 .77* DLS 9 .62* DLS 14 .55* DLS 5 .61* DLS 10 .67* DLS 15 .50* *p < .01 Ege Education Journal 2017(18) 1: 408-429 Digital Literacy Scale: Adaptation Study into Turkish 414 According to Table 1, the correlation coefficient for all items in both the English and Turkish forms was found to be above .30 and statistically significant.
Based on the findings, it can be said that the Turkish and English forms of the scale are linguistically equivalent.
Construct Validity Exploratory Factor Analysis (EFA) and Confirmatory Factor Analysis (CFA) were conducted to examine the factor structure of the Turkish form of the DLS. In addition, the convergent and discriminant validity of the scale were also investigated.
Exploratory Factor Analysis (EFA) Before conducting exploratory factor analysis, it is necessary to determine whether the data set is suitable for factor analysis. The procedures to determine whether the sample is appropriate for data analysis are the implementation of the Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin (KMO) and Bartlett’s Sphericity tests. The KMO can take values ranging from 0 to 1, and KMO values above 0.5 are considered acceptable (Field, 2009). Moreover, KMO values between 0.5 and 0.7 are considered moderate, values between 0.7 and 0.8 are considered good, values between 0.8 and 0.9 are considered very good, and values over 0.9 are regarded as excellent (Field, 2009). In this study, the KMO sample adequacy coefficient was found to be .91, and the Bartlett’s Sphericity test value was 1549.40 (p<.01, df=136). According to these results, it can be said that the data are in excellent conformity for factor analysis. In EFA, the principal components method and varimax rotation technique were used. The principal components method is a multivariate statistical technique that enables identification, classification, dimensionality reduction, and interpretation, and aims to remove the dependence structure (Jolliffe, 2002). Accordingly, as a result of the EFA, a four-factor structure was obtained that accounts for 65.78% of the total variance and exactly matches the items in the original form (Figure 1).
Figure 1.
Eigenvalue Scree Plot Ege Education Journal 2017(18) 1: 408-429 Digital Literacy Scale: Adaptation Study into Turkish 415 When Figure 1 is examined, it is observed that the eigenvalues of four dimensions are above one. The identified factors were named “Attitude,” “Technical,” “Cognitive,” and “Social,” as in the original scale. Since all item factor loadings in the scale were above .30, there was no need to remove any items from the scale. Information regarding the factor loadings of the scale and the variance ratios they explain is presented in Table 2.

The factor loadings of the items in the Attitude subscale range from .31 to .83. The Technical subscale consists of 6 items and explains 44.04% of the total variance. The factor loadings of the items in the Technical subscale range from .67 to .80. The Cognitive subscale consists of 2 items and explains 6% of the total variance. The factor loadings for the two items in the Cognitive subscale are .40 and .67. The Social subscale also consists of 2 items and explains 5.22% of the total variance. The factor loadings for the 2 items in the Social subscale are .51 and .92, respectively.
The four-factor structure obtained from the DLS can be evaluated as four separate scales, and it is also possible to obtain a total score related to digital literacy from the scale.
Confirmatory Factor Analysis (CFA) In this study, CFA was conducted to determine whether the factor structure of the original form of the DLS could be confirmed in the Turkish sample. In this context, to evaluate the adequacy of the model tested in CFA, the Chi-Square Goodness of Fit Test, Goodness of Fit Index (GFI), Adjusted Goodness of Fit Index (AGFI), Comparative Fit Index (CFI), Normed Fit Index (NFI), Non-Normed Fit Index (NNFI), Root Mean Square Error of Approximation (RMSEA), and Standardized Root Mean Square Residual (SRMR) were examined. The criteria to be considered regarding the fit indices are generally as follows: for GFI, CFI, and NFI, a value of 0.90 indicates acceptable fit, while a value of 0.95 indicates perfect fit.
(Bentler, 1980; Bentler and Bonett, 1980; Marsh, Hau, Artelt, Baumert, and Peschar, 2006).
For NNFI, a value of .95 is considered an acceptable fit criterion and a value of .97 is considered an excellent fit criterion.
For AGFI, a value of .85 indicates acceptable fit, and a value of .90 indicates excellent fit (Schermelleh-Engel and Moosbrugger, 2003).
For RMSEA, a value of .08 indicates acceptable fit and .05 indicates excellent fit (Brown and Cudeck, 1993; Byrne and Campbell, 1999).
For SRMR, a value of .05 is considered an excellent fit and a value of .10 is considered an acceptable fit criterion (Schermelleh-Engel and Moosbrugger, 2003). The fit indices of the model obtained in the CFA were examined, and it was found that the minimum chi-square value (χ2=268.45, df=113, p=0.00) was significant.
The fit index values were found to be RMSEA = .071, GFI = .93, AGFI = .91, CFI = .98, NFI = .96, NNFI = .97, and SRMR = .05. The excellent and acceptable fit criteria for the examined fit indices indicate that the four-factor model obtained from the CFA is compatible and that the factor structure of the scale in its original form was confirmed in the Turkish sample. The factor loadings for the four-dimensional model obtained from the first-level CFA are presented in Figure 2.
Figure 2. Path Diagram Obtained from the First-Order CFA for the DLS and Correlation Values Between Dimensions As can be seen in Figure 2, the factor loadings for the "Attitude" subscale range from .54 to .75, for the "Technical" subscale from .63 to .79, for the "Cognitive" subscale from .60 to .69, and for the "Social" subscale from .61 to .77. In addition, Figure 2 displays the correlation values between the dimensions. Accordingly, the relationships between the dimensions range from .65 to .97.
The t values obtained as a result of the first-order CFA are presented in Table 3.

The fact that the calculated t values are greater than 1.96 indicates significance at the .05 level; if they are greater than 2.58, it indicates significance at the .01 level (Jöreskog and Sörbom, 1993; Kline, 2011). Accordingly, it was determined that all t values obtained in the first-order CFA are significant at the .01 level.
According to the results of the first-order CFA, the number of participants in the study group is sufficient for factor analysis, and there are no items that need to be removed from the model.
In addition to the first-order CFA conducted, a second-order CFA was applied to determine the extent to which the Attitude, Technical, Cognitive, and Social subscales, defined as subdimensions, fit the latent variable of “Digital Literacy” conceptualized as a higher-order construct. In the second-order CFA, the minimum χ2 value (χ2=286.60, df=115, p=.00) was found to be significant, and the fit index values were RMSEA=.074, GFI=.96, AGFI=.92, CFI=.97, NFI=.96, NNFI=.97, and SRMR=.048. Taking into account the criteria for excellent and acceptable fit for these indices, it is seen that the fit indices obtained from the second-order CFA are sufficient.
Accordingly, it can be said that digital literacy can be measured with a four-factor structure named Attitude, Technical, Cognitive, and Social.
The criteria for perfect and acceptable fit regarding the fit indices examined in the study, as well as the fit indices obtained from the first and second order CFA, are presented in Table 4.

Therefore, it can be said that the Attitude, Technical, Cognitive, and Social subscales are significant predictors of the latent variable of digital literacy. Convergent and discriminant validity To determine the construct validity of whether the Digital Literacy Scale measures a four-factor structure, convergent and discriminant validity were examined. For convergent validity, the Average Variance Extracted (AVE) values for each factor were analyzed. The AVE values for each factor were found to be 0.62, 0.58, 0.52, and 0.61, respectively. It is stated that all of these values being above .50 provides evidence for convergent validity (Bagozzi and Yi, 1988).
The discriminant validity was assessed by examining whether the square roots of the AVE values of the scales were greater than both the correlations between the constructs and the value of 0.70 (Fornell and Larcker, 1981), and it was found that the scale possesses discriminant validity (Table 5).
Table 5.
Discriminant Validity Values Attitude Technical Cognitive Social Attitude 0.78485 Reliability The reliability of the DLS was calculated using internal consistency (Cronbach's Alpha) and test-retest methods. The results obtained from the reliability studies are presented in Table 6.
Table 6.
The reliability coefficients of the DLS calculated using the internal consistency method are as follows: Subscales Cronbach Alpha Test-Retest Attitude .88 .89 Technical .89 .90 Cognitive .70 .87 Social .72 .79 Entire scale .93 .98 In the study, the internal consistency coefficient obtained from 185 students was calculated as .93 for the entire scale. The internal consistency coefficients for the subscales were found to be .88 for the Attitude subscale, .89 for the Technical subscale, .70 for the Cognitive subscale, and .72 for the Social subscale.
In order to determine the test-retest reliability of the Digital Literacy Scale, the scale was administered to 53 students from the departments of Computer Education and Technology Teaching twice, with a three-week interval, and the correlations between the two administrations were calculated. The reliability coefficients calculated using the test-retest method were .98 for the entire scale, .89 for the Attitude subscale, .90 for the Technical subscale, .87 for the Cognitive subscale, and .79 for the Social subscale. Scales with a reliability coefficient of .70 or above are considered reliable (Büyüköztürk, 2010; Pallant, 2005; Tezbaşaran, 1996). Accordingly, it can be stated that the reliability coefficients of the overall scale as well as its Attitude, Technical, Cognitive, and Social subscales are at a sufficient level.
Evaluation of Scores Obtained from the DLS The DLS consists of 17 items. A 5-point Likert-type rating is used in the scale, ranging from Strongly Agree (5) to Strongly Disagree (1). There are no reverse-scored items on the scale.
The scale consists of four dimensions: attitude, technical, cognitive, and social. Since the Attitude subscale contains 7 items, the minimum score that can be obtained from this dimension is 7, and the maximum is 35. The Technical subscale consists of 6 items; therefore, the minimum possible score for this dimension is 6, and the maximum is 30. The Cognitive subscale contains 2 items; thus, the minimum score is 2 and the maximum is 10. Similarly, the Social subscale also consists of 2 items, and the minimum and maximum possible scores are 2 and 10, respectively.
The fact that the DLS provides sufficient fit indices in both the first and second order CFAs indicates that it is possible to work with scores obtained from the subscales, as well as to obtain an overall score for digital literacy from the scale. Higher scores obtained from the subscales and the overall scale of the DLS indicate higher digital literacy.
Ege Education Journal 2017(18) 1: 408-429 Digital Literacy Scale: Turkish Adaptation Study 422 Discussion and Conclusion With the digital age and new technologies, the concept of digital literacy has come to the forefront, and it is considered important for individuals to be digitally literate.
In this context, the study aimed to adapt the 17-item “Digital Literacy Scale” developed by Ng (2012) into Turkish.
The original scale, which has a four-factor structure, consists of attitude, technical, cognitive, and social dimensions.
The scale uses a 5-point Likert-type rating, ranging from Strongly Agree (5) to Strongly Disagree (1), and does not include any reverse-scored items.
In the study, linguistic equivalence was initially established: the scale was translated from English to Turkish by three experts fluent in both languages, and from Turkish to English by another three experts. Based on the suggestions of these experts, a Turkish trial form was created and both forms were administered to 37 academics fluent in both English and Turkish at an interval of two weeks. The correlation coefficients between the two forms were found to be .89 for the entire scale, .80 for the Attitude subscale, .93 for the Technical subscale, .73 for the Cognitive subscale, .73 for the Social subscale, and above .30 and significant for all items present in both forms. Once it was determined that the Turkish and English forms of the scale were linguistically equivalent, the construct validity of the scale was examined using EFA and CFA. For the EFA, the suitability of data obtained from 185 students for factor analysis was assessed using the KMO sample adequacy coefficient (.91) and Barlett's Sphericity test value (1549.40, p<.001, df=136), which were found to indicate excellent suitability. The EFA, using the principal components method and varimax rotation, yielded a four-factor structure that explained 65.78% of the total variance and matched the items of the original form exactly. The Attitude subscale consists of 7 items, the Technical subscale consists of 6 items, the Cognitive subscale consists of 2 items, and the Social subscale consists of 2 items. The Attitude subscale accounts for 10.52% of the total variance, the Technical subscale accounts for 44.04%, the Cognitive subscale for 6%, and the Social subscale for 5.22%. The factor loadings range from .31 to .83 for Attitude, .67 to .80 for Technical, .40 and .67 for Cognitive, and .51 and .92 for Social subscales. The CFA was conducted with data from 210 students to determine whether the original factor structure of the DLS would be confirmed in the Turkish sample. To assess the adequacy of the model tested in the CFA, fit indices were examined and found as follows: χ2=268.45 (df=113, p=0.00), RMSEA=.071, GFI=.93, AGFI=.91, CFI=.98, NFI=.96, NNFI=.97, and SRMR=.05. These results indicate that the original factor structure of the scale was confirmed in the Turkish sample. Regarding the four-dimensional model obtained from the first-order CFA, factor loadings ranged from .54 to .75 for the “Attitude” subscale, .63 to .79 for the “Technical” subscale, .60 to .69 for the “Cognitive” subscale, and .61 to .77 for the “Social” subscale. The t values ranged from 8.70 to 15.44 and were significant at the .01 level. The number of participants in the research group was found to be sufficient for factor analysis in the first-order CFA. The second-order CFA was conducted to determine to what extent the subscales fit under the higher-order “Digital Literacy” latent variable. As a result of the analysis, the fit indices were χ2=286.60 (df=115, p=0.00), RMSEA=.074, GFI=.96, AGFI=.92, CFI=.97, NFI=.96, NNFI=.97, and SRMR=.048, all of which were at an adequate level. Accordingly, it can be said that digital literacy can be measured as a four-factor structure named Attitude, Technical, Cognitive, and Social. The fit indices obtained from the first and second-order CFA indicate that the construct validity of the Turkish form of the DLS has been established. In addition, all t values were found to be significant at the .01 level; the Attitude, Technical, Cognitive, and Social subscales were determined to be significant predictors of the latent Digital Literacy variable. The fact that the DLS provides sufficient fit indices in both first and second-order CFA shows that, in addition to allowing for calculations based on the scores from the subscales, a total score for digital literacy can also be obtained from the scale overall. High scores on the subscales and the overall scale indicate a high level of digital literacy. In the study, convergent and discriminant validity of the scale were examined, and the findings showed that the convergent and discriminant validity of the scale were within an appropriate range. The reliability of the DLS was assessed using the internal consistency coefficient (Cronbach Alpha) and the test-retest methods. The internal consistency coefficient calculated from 185 students was found to be .93 for the entire scale, .88 for the Attitude subscale, .89 for the Technical subscale, .70 for the Cognitive subscale, and .72 for the Social subscale. For test-retest reliability, the correlations between two administrations conducted three weeks apart among 53 students in the computer education and instructional technology department were calculated as .98 for the entire scale, .89 for the Attitude subscale, .90 for the Technical subscale, .87 for the Cognitive subscale, and .79 for the Social subscale. The results indicate that the DLS has adequate reliability coefficients for the entire scale and its subscales. The findings obtained in the study provide evidence for the validity and reliability of the DLS, which was developed by Ng (2012) and adapted into Turkish.
Due to the lack of sufficient measurement tools for assessing digital literacy skills and the increasing importance of digital literacy skills today, the DLS is a scale that can be used in future research. For upcoming studies, it is recommended that the DLS—which has adequate evidence of validity and reliability—be administered by ensuring measurement invariance across different samples. In this way, the findings obtained from different samples regarding digital literacy skills, which hold significant importance today, will provide support for advancing this area.

To demonstrate the adequacy of the model tested by CFA, the fit indices were examined and found as follows: χ2=268.45 (N=113, p=0.00), RMSEA=.071, GFI=.93, AGFI=.91, CFI=.98, NFI=.96, NNFI=.97, and SRMR=.05.

Ankara University Journal of Environmental Sciences 4(2), 1-32 (2012) Observed and Projected Climate Change, Drought, and Desertification in Turkey Murat TÜRKEŞ Statistical Department, Affiliated with Middle East Technical University Abstract: The main purpose of this article is to provide a scientific assessment of observed and projected climatic changes, drought, and desertification in Turkey, along with global climate change, drought, and desertification.
As a result of the rapid increase in the accumulation of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere due to various human activities since the industrial revolution, the most significant consequence of the intensified greenhouse effect is the creation of an additional positive radiative forcing on the Earth's energy balance, causing the global climate to become warmer and more variable.
On the other hand, whether on a global or regional scale, climate change leads to significant changes in the frequency, intensity, spatial distribution, duration, and timing of extreme weather and climate events. Precipitation amounts, which exhibit high spatial and temporal variability, showed significant increasing and decreasing trends across various regions of the world during the period from 1900 to 2005. For example, while significant increasing trends were observed in the eastern parts of North and South America, northern Europe, and the central and northern parts of Asia, marked drying trends occurred in the Sahel, the Mediterranean Basin—including Turkey—a portion of South Asia, and southern Africa. In addition to these findings, increases have also been observed in severe precipitation events (such as extreme high and extreme low precipitation) in many regions of the world and in Turkey.
In addition to precipitation, new findings based on analyses of long-term climatological and meteorological observation series indicate that, since the 1950s, significant changes have also occurred in some extremes, particularly in daily extreme air temperatures (e.g., highest and lowest temperatures, tropical and summer days, etc.), the number of frost days, and the frequency and duration of heatwaves. Such changes have generally manifested themselves across the Eastern Mediterranean and Turkey—especially since the 1990s—as a marked decrease in the number of frost and snowy days, an increase in the number of hot days and nights, and a rise in both the nightly minimum and daytime maximum average air temperatures.
Keywords: Intensified greenhouse effect; Climate Change; Global warming; Greenhouse gas emission scenarios; Climate model projections.
Observed and Projected Climate Change, Drought, and Desertification in Turkey Climate change can be defined as statistically significant changes in the mean state of the climate or in its variability, persisting for decades or longer (Türkeş, 2008ab). Climate change may arise from natural internal processes, external forcing factors, or from continuous anthropogenic (human-induced) changes in atmospheric composition or land use. Another important concept to be aware of in this context is climate variability. Climate variability refers to changes in the mean state of the climate and in other statistics such as standard deviations and the occurrence of extreme events, across all time and spatial scales (Türkeş, 2010, 2012a). Climate variability may result from natural internal processes within the climate system (internal variability) or from changes in natural external forcing factors (external variability) (Türkeş, 2010, 2011a). The global climate is a highly complex system consisting of five primary components—atmosphere, hydrosphere, cryosphere, lithosphere, and biosphere—which interact with one another, and it is generally referred to as the Physical Climate System or simply the Climate System (Türkeş, 2010, 2012a). The atmospheric component of the climate system is the subsystem that most characterizes and controls the climate.
The climate system evolves over time due to its own internal dynamics, as well as changes (forcings) arising from various external factors and processes that affect the climate, and human-induced forcings (e.g., radiative forcing).
The climate prevailing in any part of the Earth is the result of complex interactions among various fundamental components (subsystems) of the climate system (Türkeş, 2011a, 2012a). External forcings and factors include changes that interact with and are affected by the subsystems of the physical climate system, such as natural events—including volcanic eruptions, changes in solar activity, and alterations in the astronomical relations between the Earth and the Sun—as well as human-induced changes in the composition of the atmosphere (Erinç, 1969; Erlat, 2010; Le Treut et al., 2007; Türkeş, 2010, 2011a). Greenhouse gases and aerosols emitted into the atmosphere as a result of human activities, although their impact duration may vary, are the main external forcings and factors that can cause climate changes (Türkeş, 2008ab, 2011a, 2012a). In other words, changes caused by external forcings and factors develop under the control and influence of natural events outside the climate system as well as anthropogenic forcings and factors. The potential ‘external’ causes of climate change mainly include plate movements in the Earth's solid crust (lithosphere), changes in solar activity, and variations in the astronomical relationships between the Earth and the Sun. These astronomical relationships, also known as Milankovitch cycles, comprise a series of periodic changes and can provide significant evidence for explaining long-term climate changes (Erlat, 2010; Türkeş, 2013).
The aim of this study is to provide a broad and multidisciplinary scientific synthesis of the observed climate change, drought, and desertification in the world and in Turkey, as well as the projected future climate change and variability according to climate model projections run based on various greenhouse gas emission scenarios. This synthesis is based on a large number of published peer-reviewed articles and papers, as well as original analyses and evaluations conducted specifically for this study.
2.
Causes of Global Climate Change 2.1.
Natural Climate Change and Variability The global climate has exhibited a tendency to change at all spatial and temporal scales since the beginning of the Earth's 4.6-billion-year geological history (Türkeş, 2013). Over millions of years, the climate has oscillated between very hot conditions—where average annual surface temperatures in polar regions exceeded 10°C—and glacial periods or epochs when ice sheets (glacial shields or continental glaciers) covered most of the mid-latitude continents (see.
Figure 6, 11).
According to some assumptions, during certain cold periods in the past, the entire surface of the Earth was covered with ice (Snowball Earth Hypothesis). In the segment of the approximately 545-million-year time series from the Paleozoic (the Primary Era) to the present that is closer to today, it is observed that fluctuations with lower amplitude have occurred. In the approximately last 10,000-year Holocene epoch in which we currently live, there has not been a single year that is exactly the same as the preceding year, with year-to-year and decadal or even longer time scale variations developing (Erlat, 2010; Türkeş, 2010, 2012; etc.).
Figure 1.
Simplified joint representation of the Milankovitch cycles: schematic depiction of changes in the shape of the Earth's orbit (E), axial tilt (T), and climatic precession (P) (Jansen et al.).
According to Türkeş (2010, cited in 2007). Some of the changes mentioned here are caused by various forcings.
In connection with its own evolution, the total radiant energy emitted by the Sun from its photosphere layer at all wavelengths—reaching a unit area perpendicular to the Sun's rays at the top of the atmosphere per unit time (total solar irradiance or solar constant, W/m²)—has increased by approximately 30% over the Earth's 4.6 billion-year history due to changes in the average Sun-Earth distance. Although the magnitude is not precisely known, variations in total solar irradiance over shorter timescales are of a comparable amplitude. Low-frequency changes in the characteristics of the Earth's orbit alter the amount of solar energy received at any given location on the Earth's surface during a specific season. The most significant fluctuations in this context are observed within the range of 10,000 to 100,000 years.
Single volcanic eruptions lead to a general cooling in the years following the eruption (Erlat, 2010; Türkeş, 2010, 2013). Furthermore, if volcanic activities intensify during a specific decade or century, they can be responsible for a low-frequency forcing.
On longer time scales, for example, as a result of the convergence and collision of an oceanic plate with a continental plate and/or two oceanic plates—primarily due to plate tectonics—large-scale mountain formation zones (e.g., the Andes in South America) and island arcs (e.g.,
Increased volcanic activity associated with the Pacific can lead to the development of a strong cooling trend that may persist for long periods ranging from thousands to millions of years (Türkeş, 2010).
Milankovitch Cycles When the role of the atmosphere is neglected, insolation (the radiant solar energy received per unit area per unit time, W/m²) at a specific location and time on the Earth's surface is a function of the Sun-Earth distance and the cosine of the solar zenith angle (Türkeş, 2013).
These two variables can be calculated by utilizing the time of day, latitude, and the characteristics of the Earth's orbit.
In climatology, the astronomical relationships between the Earth and the Sun are referred to as Milankovitch cycles. Astronomical relationships are determined primarily by three orbital parameters, known as the state of the Earth’s orbit around the Sun: Axial Tilt (T), Eccentricity (E), and Precession (P) (Figure 1). Briefly, tilt (T) is defined as a measure of the inclination (more tilted or steeper) of the ecliptic plane relative to the equatorial plane; eccentricity (E) is defined as a measure of the shape of the Earth's orbit around the Sun [e.g., more elliptical (less round) or less elliptical (more circular)]; whereas climatic precession (P) refers to the changes in the Sun-Earth distance at the summer solstice (solstice), or in other words, the ‘perihelion timing’. Therefore, the main astronomical relationships that can lead to changes in global climate include changes in the shape of the Earth’s orbit around the Sun (orbital forcing), as well as changes in the axial tilt and precession (timing of perihelion) of the Earth (Figure 1).
Figure 2.
Changes in the monthly average atmospheric CO2 accumulation measured at the Mauna Loa (Hawaii) Observatory during the period January 1958 – May 2013 were reanalyzed and plotted [updated with original monthly average data from January 2009 to May 2013 from the data source “Anonymous (2013)” cited in Türkeş (2012a)]. A third-degree polynomial regression curve was fitted to the interannual variations and seasonal cycles in the series of monthly average CO2 accumulation.
2.2.
Anthropogenic Climate Change and Global Warming The increase in human-induced greenhouse gas accumulation in the atmosphere, observed since the industrial revolution, continues unabated. The significance of CO2 becomes clearer when considering the magnitude and rate of increase of its accumulation in the atmosphere, its atmospheric lifetime ranging from 50 to 200 years, and its ability to absorb a large proportion of outgoing longwave (infrared) terrestrial radiation (OLR). According to the Mauna Loa measurements conducted since 1958 (Keeling and Whorf, 2009; Anonymous, 2013), the accumulation of CO2 in the Earth's atmosphere is increasing very rapidly. When examining the monthly average CO2 time series, it is observed that the annual average atmospheric CO2 concentration, which was around 280 ppmv (parts per million by volume or one molecule in a million units) in the pre-industrial period and about 315 ppmv in 1958, reached approximately 394 ppmv in 2012 (Figure 2). The monthly average CO2 value for May 2013 was recorded as 400 ppmv. The current level of atmospheric CO2 accumulation is far above the changes in the natural CO2 accumulation over the past approximately 700,000 years, which varied between about 180 and 300 ppmv. These increases in greenhouse gas accumulations weaken the cooling efficiency of the Earth by outgoing longwave infrared radiation, resulting in a positive radiative forcing that tends to warm the planet further. Therefore, the positive contribution to the Earth's/atmosphere's combined energy balance is called the intensified greenhouse effect (Türkeş, 2008ab).
This, therefore, means an intensification of the natural greenhouse effect—which has been operating for hundreds of millions of years thanks to the natural greenhouse gases (water vapor, CO2, CH4, N2O, and O3) in the Earth's atmosphere.
(a) Global Mean Surface Air Temperature Temperature anomaly (°C) (b) Northern Hemisphere Mean Surface Air Temperature Temperature anomaly (°C) (c) Southern Hemisphere Mean Surface Air Temperature Temperature anomaly (°C) Figure 3. Changes in annual mean surface temperature anomalies for the global (a), Northern Hemisphere (b), and Southern Hemisphere, calculated as deviations from the 1961–1990 annual means for the period 1850–2011 (Türkeş, 2012a).
The change graphs were drawn using the monthly temperature data from CRUTEM3, updated by CRU/UEA and HadCentre/MetOffice (data source: Anonymous, 2012). The interannual variability in the temperature series was smoothed using a 13-point low-pass Binomial filter (▬▬).
Global warming can be defined as “the increase in temperatures observed on the Earth's surface and in the lower layers of the atmosphere as a result of the intensification of the natural greenhouse effect, primarily due to the rapid rise in the accumulations of greenhouse gases released into the atmosphere since the industrial revolution—especially from various human activities such as the burning of fossil fuels, deforestation, agricultural activities, and industrial processes—with the additional contribution of urbanization” (Türkeş, 2008ab, 2012a). As can be understood from this definition, the greenhouse gases that lead to human-induced climate change and global warming mostly originate from the burning of fossil fuels (energy production and transformation), industry (energy-related; chemical processes and cement production, etc.).
energy Observed and Projected Climate Change, Drought, and Desertification in Turkey (other than energy), transportation, land use change, waste management, and agricultural activities (energy-related; stubble burning, rice cultivation, livestock, and fertilization, etc.).
They originate from activities other than energy.
3.
Observed Climate Changes in the World and in Turkey 3.1.
Observed Climate Change and Variability in the World Primarily as a result of significant changes in the composition of the atmosphere due to human activities, the warming of surface temperatures that began in the late 19th century became even more pronounced in the early 1980s, with almost every year breaking global temperature records by being warmer than the previous one (IPCC, 2001, 2007; Türkeş, 2001, 2008ab, 2012a). Recent global assessments have also shown that warming in the climate system has intensified (IPCC, 2007). The magnitude of the updated 100-year (1906–2005) linear warming trend for global mean surface temperatures has reached 0.74°C (0.74 ± 0.18°C) (Solomon et al., 2007). The linear warming trend over the last 50-year period has been approximately twice that of the past 100 years (0.13°C per decade). Furthermore, during the same period, the increase in mean minimum (nighttime lowest) air temperatures—approximately 0.2°C per decade—has been about twice that of the increase in mean maximum (daytime highest) air temperatures. In addition to the upward trend observed in global mean surface temperatures, the 1990s and 2000s have included the warmest years on record in instrumental observations worldwide, with 1998 being the warmest year (Türkeş, 2001, 2003a, 2008ab).
Figure 3 was drawn based on the monthly temperature data of the Climate Research Unit Third Temperature Dataset (CRUTEM3), which was jointly developed and updated by the well-known British climate research centers—the Climate Research Unit of the University of East Anglia (CRU/UEA) and the UK Met Office Hadley Centre (HadCentre/MetOffice) (Brohan et al., 2006; Jones et al., 2012). According to our new time series analyses using these data, globally, the 2000s constitute the warmest decade in the instrumentally recorded observations between 1850 and 2011; 1998, with an anomaly of +0.548 °C, stands out as the warmest year (Figure 3a). The second and third highest global temperature records were set in 2005 with +0.482 °C and in 2010 with +0.478 °C, respectively. When we compare this updated evaluation based on new data with earlier global assessments and the results of our own previous evaluation studies, we see that, with only very minor numerical differences, it displays characteristics very similar to those found in earlier years’ assessments. For instance, even though the order of the warmest ten years and the second and third warmest years has changed, 1998 still retains the record as the warmest year. Similar warming trends and high temperature records are observed in the annual mean surface temperatures of both the Northern and Southern Hemispheres as well (Figure 3b, 3c). Globally, the second warmest year—2005—was the warmest year in the Northern Hemisphere, with a significant annual temperature increase of 0.626 °C above the 1961-1990 mean (Figure 3b). In the Southern Hemisphere, the warmest year, as in the global average, was 2005, with an increase of 0.485 °C (Figure 3c).
According to recent assessments based on new findings from paleoclimate studies (IPCC, 2007), the warming observed over the past half-century is unusual when compared to at least the previous 1,300 years.
The effect of the urban heat island is primarily local, and its impact on temperature values (less than 0.006°C per decade over land) is considered negligible.
Figure 4 shows the geographical distribution patterns of linear temperature trends in global mean annual surface (a) and tropospheric (b) temperature values obtained from surface and satellite observations for the period 1979–2005.
Gray areas on the maps correspond to regions with missing data.
Accordingly, in addition to the pronounced warming trends observed in the Earth's surface air temperatures, air temperatures within the lowest 8 kilometers of the atmosphere—corresponding primarily to the lower and middle troposphere—have also exhibited a significant upward trend during the past approximately 30-year period (Figure 4). On the other hand, while satellite-based tropospheric records show more spatially homogeneous warming, it is noteworthy that the strong warming trends in surface temperatures are more closely associated with land masses—in other words, they are predominantly observed over land areas.

Geographical distribution patterns of linear temperature trends in global mean annual surface (a) sea and air temperatures and tropospheric (b) air temperatures, as determined from ground and satellite observations for the period 1979–2005 (IPCC, 2007).
Figure 5. Geographical distribution patterns (%) of observed trends in annual precipitation amounts recorded over the Earth's land masses during the period 1901–2005 (central map), along with long-term regional variations and trends (time series graphs on the sides) (Trenberth et al., 2007).
As in many regions of the world and in most countries neighboring Europe and the Mediterranean Basin, many cities in Turkey have also exhibited a statistically and climatologically significant warming trend, especially in the nighttime minimum air temperatures during the spring and summer seasons (Türkeş et al., 2002; Türkeş and Sümer, 2004).
In the 20th century, as mid-latitude and polar snow cover, polar land and sea ice, and mid-latitude mountain glaciers melted and decreased in area and volume, global mean sea level rose by approximately 0.17 m (ranging between 0.12 and 0.22 m) according to tide gauge and sea level measurements, and the heat content of the oceans increased (IPCC, 2001, 2007).
Sea level The main uncertainty encountered in determining sea level rise in Turkey is the impact of vertical terrestrial crustal movements (epeirogenic movements) on tide and sea level measurements. While precipitation has increased by approximately 0.5% to 1% per decade in the middle and high latitude regions of the Northern Hemisphere, it has decreased by about 3% per decade in a significant portion of the subtropical continental zones, including the Mediterranean Basin (Figure 5). In Turkey, as we will see in the following section, there is a marked decreasing trend— in other words, ‘aridification’— observed especially in total winter precipitation in regions dominated by the Mediterranean precipitation regime.
3.2.
Observed Climate Change and Variability in Turkey As can be seen, in addition to its many impacts and adverse consequences, climate change—whether on a global or regional scale—causes changes in the frequency, intensity, spatial distribution, duration, and timing of extreme weather and climate events. For instance, precipitation exhibits high spatial and temporal variability, and during the period 1900–2005, trends of both decreases (aridification) and increases in precipitation amounts have been observed across very large regions and continents.
Accordingly, significant increasing trends are observed in the eastern parts of North and South America, the central and northern regions of northern Europe and Asia, while significant aridification or decreasing trends are seen in the Sahel, the Mediterranean Basin including Turkey, a portion of South Asia, and southern Africa (e.g.
(IPCC, 2001, 2007; Türkeş, 1996, 1998a, 1999, 2011a, 2012ab; Türkeş and Erlat, 2003, 2005; Türkeş and Tatlı, 2010, 2011a, etc.). In addition to these findings, severe precipitation events (extremely high and extremely low precipitations, etc.) in many regions of the world and in Turkey
increases are also observed (e.g.
IPCC, 2001, 2007; Türkeş and Tatlı, 2009; Türkeş et al., 2009ab, 2011; Yozgatlıgil et al., 2010; Tatlı and Türkeş, 2011ab; Öztürk et al., 2012; Altınsoy et al., 2012; Sen et al., 2012, etc.
In addition to precipitation, new findings derived from the analyses of long-term climatological and meteorological observations indicate that, since the 1950s, significant changes have also occurred in some extremes, especially daily extreme air temperatures (e.g., highest and lowest temperatures, tropical and summer days, etc.), the number of frost days, and the frequency and duration of heatwaves. Such changes—particularly since the 1990s in the Eastern Mediterranean and Turkey—manifest as a marked decline in the number of frost and snowy days (Erlat and Türkeş, 2008, 2012; Kartum et al., 2011), an increase in the numbers of hot days and nights (Erlat and Türkeş, 2013), and a rise in both the minimum night-time and the maximum daytime air temperatures (Türkeş et al., 2002; Türkeş and Sümer, 2004; Kuglitsch et al., 2010, etc.), in other words, through the increased frequency and intensity of heatwaves. In this section, monthly mean air temperature data (monthly mean of daily averages), monthly mean maximum (highest) and monthly mean minimum (lowest) air temperature data (°C), as well as monthly total precipitation (mm or kg/m2) data recorded at the climatological and meteorological stations of the Turkish State Meteorological Service (MGM) for the period 1950–2010, were used. Through the statistical and climatological time series analyses of these data, long-term trends and variations in Turkey’s precipitation and air temperature series were re-examined. To determine trends in station data, the Mann-Kendall (M-K) rank correlation coefficient method was applied to the long-term time series (Sneyers, 1990; Türkeş et al., 2002).
Taking into account conditions and rules such as the homogeneity and continuity of the data, and that monthly missing data at a given station should not exceed 5% of the total data for that station (Türkeş, 1996, 1998a, 1999), data from 138 stations were used for the statistical analysis and significance testing of trends in monthly mean air temperature and total precipitation. To determine the trends in the series of mean maximum and mean minimum air temperatures, time series from 70 stations in Turkey with the longest temperature records—as identified by Türkeş et al. (1996, 2002) through detailed homogeneity and randomness analyses—were used. Although analyses were conducted for both seasonal and annual series of all data, only the annual and seasonal M-K results for mean air temperature and precipitation totals, and the annual M-K results for mean maximum and mean minimum air temperatures are presented here.

3.2.1.
Observed Changes and Trends in Air Temperatures When examining the long-term trends in Turkey’s seasonal mean air temperatures, both increasing and decreasing trends are observed in winter, with some being statistically significant (Figure 6.1a). According to the results of the Mann-Kendall test, statistically significant warming trends generally prevail in the Mediterranean Region. Cooling trends, though only a few are statistically significant, are observed in the Black Sea Region as well as in the interior and western regions. Spring mean air temperatures show an increasing trend in most parts of Turkey, with the exception of a few stations (Figure 6.1a). The observed warming trends, particularly in the Marmara, Aegean, Mediterranean, Central Anatolia, and Southeastern Anatolia regions, are statistically significant. Increases in temperature trends are noteworthy and statistically significant at the 1% significance level, especially in areas where urbanization is rapid and widespread and consequently the urban heat island effect is strong, such as the Istanbul area, in coastal stations of the Aegean and Mediterranean regions, and in stations of Southeastern Anatolia (Figure 6.1b).
Observed and Projected Climate Change, Drought, and Desertification in Turkey Figure 6.2. According to the significance test of the Mann-Kendall (M-K) rank correlation coefficient, spatial distribution patterns of long-term trends in average air temperature series for the summer (c) and autumn (d) seasons in Turkey.
The map description is the same as in Figure 6.1.
The most significant difference between this new study, the results of which are presented here, and previous air temperature trend studies conducted for Turkey is that the warming trends are increasingly pronounced during the summer and autumn seasons (Figures 6.2c, 6.2d). In the summer season, the observed strong warming trends are statistically significant at the 1% significance level in the vast majority of stations (Figure 6.2c). This is an important finding that highlights the intensification of regional climate change signals and warrants attention. According to the time series analyses, the observed warming trend accelerated in nearly all stations—regardless of the level of urbanization—beginning in the 1980s, and, with a significant jump, transformed into a notably warm period over approximately the last 20 years (the time series graphs are not presented here). The shift to a period characterized by warmer conditions compared to the long-term average (a climate change signal) occurred in the mid-1980s at some stations and in the early 1990s at others. Autumn mean air temperatures also mostly exhibit a warming trend (Figure 6.2d). While the cooling trends observed at a few stations are statistically significant at only one station, the observed warming trends in the Aegean, Mediterranean, and Central Anatolia regions are, for the most part, statistically significant at the 1% significance level.
Murat Türkeş Figure 7. According to the significance test of the Mann-Kendall rank correlation coefficient, spatial distribution patterns of long-term trends in annual mean (a), annual mean maximum (b), and annual mean minimum (c) air temperatures in Turkey.
The map description is the same as in Figure 6.1. As with the seasonal air temperatures (not all results of the seasonal analysis are presented here), annual mean, annual mean maximum, and annual mean minimum air temperatures also show that warming has been increasingly strengthening in Turkey, in line with the results of previous observed temperature trend studies (Figure 7a, 7b, 7c). With the exception of a few stations characterized by randomly distributed decreasing trends, in the vast majority of stations there is a clear warming trend in annual mean, annual mean maximum, and annual mean minimum air temperatures. The observed warming trend is statistically significant at most stations.
Weak warming and cooling trends are generally distributed across the Black Sea Region and the northern parts of the Central and East Anatolia regions.
Statistically significant warming signals display a highly distinct spatial consistency pattern across Turkey (Figure 7a, 7b, 7c). All these results, among others, demonstrate that global warming—one of the most pronounced and relatively easily detectable consequences of anthropogenic global climate change—has been effective in Turkey.
