3.2.2.
Observed Changes and Trends in Total Precipitation Climate zones ranging from very arid to semi-humid are vulnerable to the impacts of strong climatic changes. Short-term regional fluctuations and long-term variations in precipitation are well-known characteristics of arid and semi-arid lands. For instance, starting from the 1960s, precipitation amounts in Africa’s Sahara and Sahel regions have decreased significantly (Figure 5). Although similar dry periods occurred during the last geological epoch (Quaternary) and in historical times, it has been noted that this most recent dry period in the Sahara tends to be more indicative of continental-scale drought. Long-term decreasing trends in precipitation and distinctly arid conditions, particularly since the early 1970s, have also affected large parts of the subtropical belt—including much of the Mediterranean Basin, with Turkey as part of this region (Figure 5).
Murat Türkeş Figure 8.2. According to the significance testing of the M-K rank correlation coefficient, spatial distribution patterns of long-term trends in total precipitation amounts during winter (a), spring (b), summer (c), and autumn (d) seasons in Turkey.
The map description is identical to that of Figure 7.1.
In fact, when a comparison is made, it is seen that the observed seasonal and annual precipitation trends in Turkey are not as strong as those observed in air temperatures. As in many regions of the world, changes in precipitation occur more in the form of significant alterations in the frequencies and magnitudes of dry and wet (precipitation) periods, along with various patterns of variability and fluctuation, rather than long-term trends (e.g.,
Tatlı and Türkeş, 2011a; Trenberth et al., 2007; Trigo et al., 2006; Türkeş, 1996, 1998, 2003, 2011a; Türkeş and Erlat, 2003, 2005; Türkeş and Tatlı, 2009; Türkeş et al., 2009ab, etc.
The spatial variability of precipitation changes is also strong. Among the regions in Turkey, the Aegean, Mediterranean, Marmara, and Southeastern Anatolia regions have been most affected by the aforementioned aridification trend. When changes in winter and annual precipitation over the past 40 years are considered, the most severe and widespread drought events in Turkey occurred during the periods of 1971–1974, 1983–1984, 1989–1990, and 2007–2008, as well as in the years 1996 and 2001 (e.g.,
Türkeş, 1996, 1998ab, 1999, 2003b, 2008abc; Türkeş and Erlat, 2003, 2005; Türkeş and Tatlı, 2009; Türkeş et al., 2009ab, etc.).
It has been observed that the drought events and water scarcity affecting many regions of Turkey have reached a critical point not only in terms of agriculture and energy production, but also from the perspective of water resources management, which includes irrigation, drinking water, and other hydrological systems and activities.
Observed and Projected Climate Change, Drought, and Desertification in Turkey When examining the long-term trends and changes in precipitation in Turkey, it is generally observed that there is a marked decreasing trend (aridification) in total winter and spring precipitation, particularly in the Marmara, Aegean, Mediterranean, and Southeastern Anatolia regions—where the Mediterranean precipitation regime dominates—as well as in the interior and southern sections of the Central and Eastern Anatolia regions (Figures 8.1a, 8.1b). Some of the aridification trends observed during the winter season, especially in the Aegean, Mediterranean, and Southeastern Anatolia regions, are statistically significant (Figure 8.1a). This result is generally consistent with previous studies on precipitation trends and changes in Turkey. In other words, the aridification trend observed in Turkey, especially in the western, southern, and continental interior-southern regions during winter, continues despite the presence of above-average (wetter) conditions that have prevailed in these regions over approximately the past two years (2008/2009-2009/2010).
The aridification trend observed in Turkey began affecting some stations in the early 1970s and others in the early 1980s, and has continued to be effective until recent years at certain stations along the Black Sea (e.g., Rize station in the Eastern Black Sea region), some stations on the Mediterranean coast (e.g., Alanya), and some stations in the continental Southeastern Anatolia Region (e.g., Mardin) (the time series graphs are not presented here).
Trends in annual total precipitation amounts Figure 9.
According to the significance test of the M-K rank correlation coefficient, the spatial distribution patterns of long-term trends in annual total precipitation amounts in Turkey.

In summer, similar to the results of previous studies, both increasing and decreasing trends predominate, with several being statistically significant (Figure 8.2c).
In autumn, however, distinct from previous studies, it is observed that the increasing trends have strengthened and the number of stations exhibiting an increasing trend has grown.
In autumn, an increase in precipitation is dominant across Turkey, except for an area covering the southeastern corner.
The observed increasing trends are statistically significant—mostly at the 1% significance level—in Central Anatolia, the Western Black Sea subregion, Southern Marmara, and Northern Aegean subregions (Figure 8.2d).
The emergence of these increasing trends may have been influenced by the wetter-than-average conditions observed in recent years, associated with the negative phase of the North Atlantic Oscillation (NAO)—a large-scale atmospheric pressure fluctuation defined by the subtropical high pressure over the Azores region and the mid-latitude low pressure over Greenland and Iceland.
As for the annual total precipitation in Turkey, a decreasing trend is observed in the western and southern regions dominated by the Mediterranean precipitation regime, which largely reflects the trends and changes seen in winter and autumn precipitation (Figure 9).
On the other hand, there is a prevailing increasing trend in annual total precipitation in the Tekirdağ and Istanbul areas of Thrace, as well as in the Black Sea Region and the northern and eastern parts of the Central and Eastern Anatolia regions.
Only a few of the observed increasing and decreasing trends are statistically significant.
3.2.3.
Changes Observed in Drought and Humidity Conditions In studies on climate change and variability, in addition to the methods and approaches used to examine changes and trends in total precipitation and the number of rainy days (precipitation probabilities), various drought indices and methods are also employed to identify, characterize, and monitor drought events. Some of these drought indices are based on precipitation series and relate to meteorological droughts, while others are intended to define hydrological or agricultural droughts and water deficits in urban water supply systems. Among these, the Standardized Precipitation Index (SPI), Normalized Precipitation Anomaly Index (NPAI), Palmer Drought Severity Index (PDSI), and deciles are currently the most widely used drought indices around the world. Using the Modified Standardized Precipitation Index (MSPI) method (Türkeş and Tatlı, 2009), it is noteworthy that, according to the drought severity and affected arid regions calculated for December 2008, prolonged and severe drought events, especially in the western and southern regions of Turkey dominated by the Mediterranean precipitation regime and in continental interior regions neighboring these areas to the north, were clearly identified between November/December 2006 and December 2008 (Figure 10) (Türkeş and Tatlı, 2010). The 360-month MSPI values for December 2008 (Figure 10d) also indicate, as has long been observed in the annual and winter season total precipitation and rainy day series (e.g.,
Türkeş, 1996, 1998a, 1999; Türkeş et al., 2009b, etc.
In the past 30 years, there has generally been a prevailing trend of aridification in Turkey’s western and southern regions, where the Mediterranean precipitation regime is dominant.
Since this calculation is based on the 30-year moving averages of the MSPI calculated backward from 2008, it is noteworthy that these new precipitation trends, based on updated data, are consistent with the statistically significant decreasing (aridification) trends observed especially in the winter and annual total precipitation series in the western and southern regions of Turkey, where the subtropical Mediterranean climate and precipitation regime generally prevail (Figure 8.1 and Figure 9).
In general, the significant decreasing trends observed in the annual and especially winter precipitation of the Eastern Mediterranean Basin and Turkey may be linked to the reduced frequency of frontal mid-latitude and Mediterranean low-pressure systems—particularly in winter—and to the increased occurrence of high-pressure conditions in this region. According to recent studies on the atmospheric causes of variability and changes in precipitation in Turkey (Türkeş and Erlat, 2003, 2005), a substantial part of the severe and widespread winter droughts in Turkey also corresponds to the strong positive index phases of the North Atlantic Oscillation (NAO).

Murat Türkeş 4.
Drought and Desertification in Turkey Drought and desertification, together with human-induced climate change, are among the most significant global and regional environmental issues facing humanity today, whose consequences must be taken seriously. In the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification in Countries Experiencing Serious Drought and/or Desertification, Particularly in Africa (UNCCD, 1995), desertification is defined as “land degradation in arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid areas resulting from complex interactions among physical, biological, political, social, cultural, and economic factors, including climatic variations and human activities” (Türkeş, 2012ab). Accordingly, land degradation that can occur in almost every region of the world is considered desertification if it takes place in arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid lands. Desertification occurs more frequently and has a greater impact in environments characterized by low total precipitation, insufficient water resources, extended dry seasons, recurrent drought events, loose surface material, thin soil layers, and sparse as well as fragile vegetation. Factors associated with climate change and variability, such as prolonged and severe drought events, can increase the vulnerability of land to desertification and accelerate desertification processes (Türkeş, 2012ab). Today, arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid areas—including true deserts, semi-deserts (desert-like steppes), and areas prone to desertification—cover approximately 41.3% of the Earth’s land surface (see.
Table 1; Figure 11).
In Turkey, semi-arid and dry sub-humid lands prone to desertification cover approximately 35% of the country’s territory; together with areas under sub-humid climate conditions, this proportion reaches about 60% (Türkeş, 1998b, 1999, 2012ab) (see.
Table 2; Figure 12).
Desertification is not the transformation of a region into a desert, but rather the loss of productivity of soil, which is a non-renewable resource. Land degradation refers to the loss of soil due to water and wind erosion, the deterioration of the physical, chemical, and biological properties of soils, and consequently, the economic losses and long-term loss of vegetation cover resulting from reduced soil fertility. Soil degradation is a process that occurs not only in agricultural lands but also in areas such as steppes, pastures, forests, or maquis/shrublands. Human factors such as deforestation, overgrazing of pastures beyond their capacity, inappropriate and improper land use, excessive production in agricultural areas and the corresponding overuse of fertilizers and pesticides, burning of stubble, excessive and unconscious irrigation, and industrial waste, as well as natural events such as floods, drought, and inundations, lead to negative outcomes such as the loss of soil organic matter, soil sealing, soil degradation, and soil pollution.
The fact that approximately 2 billion people in the world live in arid regions, that these people lack access to clean drinking water and live below the poverty line, and that 41.3% of the world’s land surface and 44% of agricultural land are located in arid areas increases the importance of combating desertification. On average, each year, 12 million hectares of land—on which 20 million tons of grain could be produced—are degraded (EMG, 2011). Therefore, reducing or preventing ecosystem and land degradation in arid regions, rehabilitating degraded lands, and ensuring the sustainable use of soils, among other measures, are of great importance.
Special importance and priority should be given to these issues (Safriel and Adeel, 2005; Türkeş, 2012ab, etc.).
On the other hand, although the relationship between climate, climate change, arid conditions, and desertification is so clear, work conducted at both national and international levels within the scope of Turkey’s obligations under the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification (UNCCD) often considers the issue primarily from the perspective of land degradation caused by various human activities and natural factors, including erosion, in publications, national reports, and action plans.
However, the relationship and dimension of the issue with respect to climate change/variability and drought are being overlooked. Therefore, in this section of the article, the aim is to provide a detailed analysis of the fundamental concepts and definitions related to drought and desertification, as well as an in-depth examination of desertification in Turkey in terms of climate, climate change/variability, and drought.
Observed and Projected Climate Change, Drought, and Desertification in Turkey 4.1.
The Problem of Drought and Desertification: Scientific/Technical Definitions and Assessment Today, around 110 countries worldwide that possess arid lands face the potential threat of desertification (Lean, 1995 and 2008). The United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) has estimated the global cost of desertification at 42 billion US dollars per year. According to projections by the Worldwatch Institute, continents lose 24 billion tons of fertile topsoil annually. Furthermore, approximately 30% of the world’s land area is affected by desertification due to severe degradation (land deterioration and loss) in arid areas where natural vegetation is sparse.
Various climate models indicate that, under warmer future climate conditions, evaporation and transpiration (evapotranspiration) will increase, along with a rise in global mean precipitation amounts and in the frequency of intense precipitation events (Altınsoy et al., 2011, 2012; Öztürk et al., 2012, 2013; Türkeş, 2011a, 2012a; Türkeş et al., 2011; etc.). Conversely, while precipitation is projected to increase in some areas, decreases are expected in others; moreover, even in terrestrial areas where precipitation increases, rising evaporation may result in reductions in runoff and soil moisture. In addition, significant seasonal changes and spatial shifts in precipitation regimes are also projected. Generally, precipitation is expected to increase at higher latitudes in both summer and winter. Precipitation is projected to rise in winter at mid-latitudes, tropical Africa, and Antarctica, and in summer in southern and eastern Asia. In this context, a persistent decrease (aridification) in winter precipitation is expected in Australia, Central America, and southern Africa. Furthermore, river flows are projected to increase at high latitudes and in Southeast Asia, but decrease in Central Asia, around the Mediterranean Basin, southern Africa, and Australia. According to projections from various climate models run with different greenhouse gas emission scenarios, especially for much of North Africa and the Mediterranean Basin, as well as for Turkey and the Middle East, substantial declines in precipitation, water resources, and river flows are expected in the next century, while significant increases in surface air temperatures, evapotranspiration, and extreme weather and climate events are projected (Altınsoy et al., 2011, 2012; Krichak et al., 2007; Giorgi and Lionelle, 2008; Sen et al., 2012; Ozturk et al., 2012, 2013; Tatlı and Türkeş, 2008, 2011ab; etc.).
For this reason, climate change will intensify the magnitude and frequency of drought events in some regions where drought is already a recurring feature of natural climate variability, while raising the vulnerability of developing countries—many of which are located in arid and semi-arid areas—to climate change to even higher levels (Tatlı and Türkeş, 2011ab; Türkeş, 2010, 2011a; Türkeş, 2012ab; etc.).
In order to make an accurate assessment and synthesis regarding arid and semi-arid areas or regions and desertification, it is useful first to define the concepts of aridity (climatological drought), arid (dry) region, drought, desert, and desertification, and to distinguish these concepts from each other based on scientific criteria: Aridity can be defined as "the persistent conditions of precipitation and moisture deficit or climatological drought, resulting from the prevailing physical geography controls and long-term atmospheric circulation patterns in any part of the world" (Türkeş, 1998b, 2008a, 2012ab). Areas where these conditions prevail throughout the year or for most of the year are referred to as arid or dry regions.
In this definition, the likelihood of climatic fluctuations and changes is disregarded.
In the UNCCD, arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid lands are defined as “areas, other than polar and sub-polar regions, where the ratio of annual precipitation to potential evapotranspiration falls within the range of 0.05 to 0.65” (UNCCD, 1995).
Without distinguishing between hydrological, agricultural, and meteorological drought, drought (Türkeş, 2012ab), generally defined as "the water deficit that occurs when the natural water resources used by various systems on Earth fall below the long-term average or normal for a given period and at a regional scale," is fundamentally a three-dimensional natural phenomenon characterized by its intensity, duration, and geographical extent.
Prolonged drought events have very negative impacts on agriculture, forestry and livestock, underground and surface water resources, access to adequate and quality drinking water, energy production, and especially on mountain and terrestrial aquatic ecosystems (Türkeş, 2010, 2012ab). The consequences of drought events, particularly in underdeveloped societies whose lifestyles and traditional production systems directly depend on precipitation or groundwater, often result in severe adversities. On the other hand, activities related to water use and management, insufficient precipitation, or changes in the type and intensity of precipitation (e.g., decreased snowfall, rapid snowmelt, and increased frequency of brief heavy showers or intense rainfall), make it necessary to monitor and plan for drought depending on the duration of the impact. Due to uncertainties regarding the onset and end of droughts, the increase in total impact, the simultaneous effect on multiple resources, the high economic repercussions, and the complex nature of drought phenomenon, identifying and monitoring drought events is not easy (Türkeş, 2010, 2012ab). As a result of meteorological drought, it is inevitable that significant problems will arise, such as serious issues in the irrigation of agricultural lands; from a hydrological perspective, the inability to store sufficient water in reservoirs, the inadequacy of drinking water sources, and the negative impacts on the environment, social life, and socioeconomic systems.
Drought is defined in the UNCCD as a “naturally occurring phenomenon that arises when precipitation is significantly below normal levels, causing serious hydrological imbalances and adversely affecting land resource production systems” (Türkeş, 2012ab).
Table 1.
The classification of extremely arid, arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid lands on Earth according to the UNCCD Aridity Index (AI) and the areas they cover is presented below: Characteristic Aridity Index Area Share Biome/ecosystem Classification (AI = P / PE) (million km²) (%) True desert Extremely arid AI < 0.05 9.8 6.6 Semi-desert, desert-like steppe Arid 0.05 < AI < 0.20 15.7 10.6 Steppe, grassland, shrub Semi-arid 0.20 < AI < 0.50 22.6 15.2 Shrubland, savannah, dry forest Dry sub-humid 0.50 < AI < 0.65 12.8 8.7 Total 60.9 41.3 From the perspective of physical geography, a desert can be defined as "an extremely arid land or biome characterized by high diurnal temperature variations, very low precipitation amounts with significant variability throughout the year and between years (annual average precipitation below 250 mm), and sparse vegetation" (Türkeş, 2012bc). The desert biome is also an ecosystem distinguished by its unique flora and fauna adapted to the conditions of desert life. Because desert air is very dry (with a very low, nearly zero, mixing ratio or water vapor content), both incoming short-wave solar radiation and outgoing long-wave thermal (infrared) radiation are very intense. In deserts, there are high daily temperature fluctuations that can reach approximately 30°C, and potential evapotranspiration is high. The high spatial and temporal variability in the desert biota and habitats is mainly controlled by the presence of water.
Although there are many definitions of deserts, what is important here is that water is the main factor controlling ecosystem processes and human life. According to a widely accepted definition, desert ecosystems are classified as extremely arid when the annual average precipitation is less than 100 mm (Noble and Gitay, 1996). A “true” or “climatic” desert, based on the Aridity Index (AI = P/PET) used in the UNCCD, is climatically associated with extremely arid conditions where AI < 0.05. According to this classification, there are no “true climatic deserts” in Turkey (see.
Figure 12).
According to the UNCCD (1995), land degradation is defined as the reduction or loss of biological or economic productivity of rainfed agricultural lands, grasslands and pastures, woodlands, and forests in arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid areas as a result of a combination of various processes originating from different land uses, human activities, and settlement patterns (Türkeş, 2012ab).
Land degradation largely results from various land use change processes, driven significantly by numerous closely interrelated social factors, and leads to changes in land cover. As it is primarily based on and defined in the UNCCD, under the prevailing adverse biophysical and physical geographical factors and conditions—such as those dominant in the arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid climatic zones of the Mediterranean region—this phenomenon often causes a gradual reduction in the land’s productive capacity over time. Biophysical and physical geographical factors and processes, in order of relative importance, include climate (temperature and precipitation regime and variability, wind activity and drought, severe weather events, etc.), soil, surface and groundwater, topography, geomorphology, and geology. In general, physical processes encompass soil erosion by water and/or wind, the degradation of physical, chemical, biological, and economic properties of soils (e.g., salinization and nitrogen enrichment), and the long-term loss of vegetation (plant cover). Considering these factors, it is expected that in the future, drought and global climate change will expand the current geography of lands vulnerable to desertification (those with high susceptibility).
Desertification is defined as “the process of ecological degradation resulting from a decline in the productivity of economically and biologically productive land” (Türkeş, 2011a, 2012ab). In advanced stages of desertification, physical degradation or new geomorphological formations (e.g., sand dune areas, deserts, etc.) may occur (Türkeş, 2011b, 2012bc). According to the UNCCD (2005), desertification is defined as “land degradation in arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid areas resulting from the complex interactions among physical, biological, political, social, cultural, and economic factors, including climatic variations and human activities” (Türkeş, 2012ab). Accordingly, the loss of land, which can occur in nearly every region of the world except for polar and sub-polar geographic zones, is considered desertification when it takes place in arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid lands. Desertification occurs more frequently and has greater impacts in environments characterized by low total precipitation, insufficient water resources, prolonged dry seasons, recurrent drought events, loose surface materials, thin soil layers, and sparse as well as sensitive vegetation cover. Climate-related factors such as prolonged and severe drought events can increase both the vulnerability of land to desertification and accelerate the processes of desertification.
Figure 11.
According to the UNCCD drought index and classification (see.
Table 1) The geographical distribution of climate types on the Earth's landmasses (Türkeş, 2010, 2012ab).
Today, areas that include true deserts, desert-like steppes, and lands prone to desertification—namely, extremely arid, arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid lands—cover approximately 41.3% of the Earth's landmasses (Figure 11; Table 1).
Examples of true deserts on Earth include the Great Sahara, Gobi, Taklamakan, Sonoran and Navajo, Atacama, Namib, Kalahari, Arabian, Syrian, Red Sand, and Kara Kum deserts, as well as the deserts of Australia (Türkeş, 2010). In arid and semi-arid regions, water deficiency is very pronounced and precipitation is irregular. In addition to low precipitation, these regions are characterized by high daily and annual temperature differences, low atmospheric moisture content (water vapor mixing ratio), and high evapotranspiration (Türkeş, 2010, 2012bc). Furthermore, soils in these areas have low organic matter content. Even if the soil is deep, the amount of available water stored in the soil is small due to insufficient rainfall. In arid and semi-arid regions, the climax flora and fauna are mostly comprised of species adapted to the ecological conditions described above. In these areas, the biomes and ecosystems that generally cover large areas include, respectively, steppe (prairie) and meadow (prairie), shrub (maquis or chaparral), savanna, and dry forest (Türkeş, 2012c).
Geographically, in the context of the UNCCD and with regard to countries affected by desertification, the countries included in the Regional Annex for the Northern Mediterranean—of which Turkey is a part (briefly, the Northern Mediterranean Annex)—are generally under the influence and control of the subtropical Mediterranean climate, characterized by dry and hot summers and mild, rainy winters. Within this framework, the societal drivers or regulators of desertification in the Northern Mediterranean region include population and socioeconomic structure, connectivity, interactions and dynamics, rural population movements, culture (value systems, lifestyles, and habits), cultural and technological changes, legal, institutional, administrative, and constitutional organizations and the level of organization, as well as national policies and measures. The human factors and pressures listed here constitute the primary sources of events, just like the final activities that directly lead to land degradation.
These include intensive (technological, widespread, and labor-intensive) agricultural practices, the abandonment of traditional practices such as monocultures and terracing, the presence or inadequacy of rural markets, traditional overgrazing and rural animal husbandry, deforestation, forest fires, excessive use and biochemical pollution of surface and groundwater, drainage of wetlands, and large-scale construction and investments such as housing, infrastructure, and tourism (MEDACTION, 2004ab; Türkeş, 2012ab; Briassoulis, 2005; Wilson and Juntti, 2005). The main land use practices associated with these activities are developments in agriculture, cattle breeding, forestry, housing, and tourism sectors, as well as, secondarily, transportation, industry, and mining. The unique combinations of these determinants at various spatial and temporal scales make desertification a complex geographical, social, economic, and environmental issue (Reynolds and Stafford-Smith, 2002; Prince, 2002; Türkeş, 2012ab).
All types of public policies, whether directly or indirectly, and whether their effects are positive or negative, influence the determinants of desertification by offering various possibilities and opportunities or by creating pressures and negative behavior patterns that affect human actions. In addition, the policies implemented by governments have the power to influence individual and collective decisions on critical questions or issues such as “which resources should be used, when, how, and to what extent to meet the needs of diverse economic activities?”

The geographical distribution of annual drought index values across Turkey (Türkeş, 2010, 2012ab). On the map, all areas shown with (i) solid fill (semi-arid), (ii) cross-hatching (dry sub-humid), and (iii) square-hatching (sub-humid) correspond climatologically to regions in Turkey with an annual water deficit, making them prone to drought and desertification. In areas on the map without any hatching, regions where index values fall between 1 and 2 (iv) are humid, while those with values above 2 (v) indicate very humid regions.
4.2.
Drought and Desertification in Turkey In this section of the study, in order to objectively describe the arid lands in Turkey and the country's susceptibility to desertification, the Aridity Index (AI)—which serves as a basis for the implementation of the UNCCD—was used (Table 1). The AI is calculated using the simple equation AI = P / PET, where P is the annual total precipitation (mm) and PET is the annual total adjusted potential evapotranspiration (mm). For the identification of arid and humid areas in Turkey, the classification from Türkeş (1999) was used as the basis. On the map showing the geographical distribution of AI values (Figure 12), values where AI<1.0 indicate areas in Turkey with an annual water deficit. Semiarid, dry sub-humid, and sub-humid climate conditions with AI<0.80, and semiarid and dry sub-humid conditions with AI<0.65, predominate at approximately 59% and 34% of the 90 stations used in the study, respectively. Dry sub-humid climate conditions are widespread across much of the continental Central Anatolia and Southeastern Anatolia regions, as well as in parts of Central and Eastern Mediterranean, and in eastern and western parts of Eastern Anatolia. Semiarid and dry sub-humid conditions cover 75% and 62.5% of the stations in Central Anatolia and Southeastern Anatolia, respectively. Lands with semiarid climate conditions are predominant in the Konya Plain and the Iğdır region (Figure 12). The driest station is Iğdır, with an AI value of 0.3, while the most humid station is Rize, with an AI value of 3.0.
5.
Future Climate Projections for Turkey and the Mediterranean Basin Studies on observed climate change and variability in Turkey and its surrounding regions (generally including the Balkans and the Middle East region that make up the Eastern Mediterranean Basin), as well as simulations and projections from global and regional climate models, indicate that significant climatic changes are taking place in Turkey. Together with many countries in the Mediterranean Basin, these findings suggest that Turkey will also be adversely affected by climate change in the future (Altınsoy et al., 2011; Demir et al.).
2008; IPCC, 2001, 2007; Trigo et al., 2006; Türkeş, 1996, 1998, 1999, 2003ab, 2008bc; Türkeş and Sümer, 2004; Türkeş and Tatlı, 2008, 2009; Türkeş et al., 2002; Türkeş et al., 2009ab; Türkeş et al., 2011, etc.).
For all these reasons, predicting Turkey’s future climate is of vital importance in order to prevent or at least mitigate the impacts of climate change and to facilitate adaptation.
Demir et al. (2008), Tatlı and Türkeş (2008, 2011a), Altınsoy et al. (2011, 2012), Öztürk et al. (2012, 2013), Türkeş et al.
(2011) and Sen et al.
(2012) can be given as an example of limited regional climate model studies aimed at projecting Turkey’s future climate and climatic variability.
In this section, Altınsoy et al.
The study titled "Estimation of Future Air Temperature and Precipitation Changes in the Mediterranean Basin Using a Global Climate Model,” conducted by (2011) using outputs from two global climate models, was utilized. Within this framework, potential changes in average air temperatures (°C) and total precipitation amounts (mm) in the Mediterranean basin and its surrounding regions in the future, as well as changes in the interannual variability (coefficient of variation (%), CV) of these same climate variables, were examined. In the study, the differences in annual and seasonal air temperatures and total precipitation projected for the 2071-2100 period compared to the 1971-2000 period were investigated, taking into consideration a very broad region covering the Mediterranean basin and stretching from the North Atlantic to Syria and from North Africa to southern Europe, also including Turkey and the Black Sea basin.
In the study from which we draw our results, the World Climate Research Programme's CMIP3 project's multi-model ensemble of 14 datasets (Meehl et al., 2007) was used as a basis. The global datasets have a resolution of 0.5°. The observational data cover the period from 1950 to 1999 (Adam and Lettenmaier, 2003). In the modeling part of the study, the 14 global data sets were compared with the CRU data set, and the two global model datasets that most closely resembled the actual observational CRU dataset were selected for further analysis. The selection of these two global models was based on a comparison of area, season, and annual mean temperature and precipitation values between the observational and model outputs for the period 1971–2000, and the difference between datasets was tested for significance at the 0.05 level using the Student’s t-test. As a result, it was determined that the output of the CCCMA-CGCM3.1, developed by the Canadian Centre for Climate Modelling and Analysis, and the CNRM-CM3.1, developed by the French National Meteorological Centre, yielded the best results (Altınsoy et al., 2011).
In future climate projections, the outputs of these models, based on the broadly most pessimistic (A2) and most optimistic (B1) emission scenarios of the IPCC SRES (IPCC, 2000), were used to examine the nature and magnitude of potential future temperature and precipitation changes in the Mediterranean Basin under the assumptions of these scenarios. In order to objectively demonstrate future changes in interannual temperature and precipitation variability, changes in the seasonal coefficient of variation (CV, %) ratios projected for the 2071–2100 period relative to the 1971–2000 normal were also analyzed, according to the outputs of the A2 and B1 scenarios of the two global climate models used in the study.
However, in this article, only the outputs of the A2 scenario are presented.

5.1.
Changes in Temperature and Precipitation Climatologies According to the outputs of the French National Meteorological Centre’s global climate model CNRM-CM3.1 under the SRES A2 emission scenario, increases in average air temperatures during the winter season in the future period 2071–2100 compared to the historical period 1971–2000 are projected to range from 2.5 to 5 °C (Figure 13a). The temperature increase in Turkey will be about 2.5 °C. When the region as a whole is considered, the south will warm by approximately 1 °C more than the northern regions. A similar pattern of increase is observed for the spring and autumn seasons, though the projected changes are relatively higher (Figures 13b and 13d). In the summer season, however, unlike the outputs of the other global climate model for the same scenario, it is projected that warming will be stronger in the northern regions (southern Europe) (Figure 13c). In this region, temperature increases may reach up to 7 °C. According to the SRES A2 emission scenario outputs of the French National Meteorological Centre’s global model, a decrease in total precipitation amounts on the order of 0.5–2 mm/day is expected during winter across the entire Mediterranean and Black Sea basins, with a more pronounced reduction in Turkey (Figure 14a). Although a general decrease in precipitation amounts is anticipated in spring, summer, and autumn, especially for Turkey and the northern Mediterranean regions (southern Europe), declines reaching up to 1.75 mm/day are projected (Figures 14b, 14c, and 14d). The reduction in total precipitation amount is evident in all seasons; in summer, however, it intensifies and reaches up to 2 mm/day particularly in France, Switzerland, Germany, and the Alpine region (Figure 14c).

5.2.
Changes in Temperature and Precipitation Variability As expected, according to the outputs of the SRES A2 emission scenario from the French National Meteorological Centre’s global model (Figure 15), the regions where the greatest increase in temperature is projected for the future also correspond to those where variability is expected to increase the most. This result not only indicates that air temperatures will rise in the future, but also carries important climatological and climate change signal information—specifically, that the frequency and extent of extremely high temperatures (extremes) and heatwaves may increase. When we examine the SRES A2 emission scenario winter season outputs of the CNRM-CM3.1 global climate model, we see that precipitation variability is projected to change most in North Africa and the Middle East during the period 2071–2100 (Figure 16). These regions already correspond, under present climate conditions, to arid and semi-arid climate zones with very high interannual precipitation variability, and which are prone to drought and desertification (Türkeş and Tatlı, 2010, 2011ab; Türkeş, 2010, 2012ab). In these regions, both in terms of spatial distribution and annual and seasonal precipitation, there is a prevailing significant annual and seasonal water deficit and randomness (i.e., an irregular precipitation regime). In other regions, although the increase in variability is comparatively less, it is still present. From these findings, it can be inferred that the frequency of severe drought events in these regions will increase in the future.
6.
Conclusion and Discussion Climate Change is currently one of the most emphasized, most scientifically researched, and most debated global topics at the intergovernmental level. Projected climate changes have both positive and negative effects on water resources, agriculture, natural ecosystems, and human health. As changes in the climate become more pronounced, the dominance of negative impacts also increases.
Socioeconomic sectors (e.g. agriculture, forestry, fisheries, water resources, and human settlements, etc.)
), human health—which is vital for terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems as well as for human development and well-being—is highly sensitive not only to changes in climate variability, such as weather and climate extremes and disasters, but also to the magnitude and rate of climate change.
Taking into account climatic factors and vegetation, the arid lands in Turkey that are prone to desertification include significant portions of the interior continental and eastern regions, as well as the Southeastern Anatolia Region. (Murat Türkeş) A large part of the Mediterranean and Aegean regions should also be considered as dry sub-humid areas that may be more affected by desertification processes in the future, considering factors such as highly variable and fragmented topography; the unsustainable and non-agricultural use of agricultural lands over approximately the past 40 years; efforts and legal regulations to remove agricultural and forest lands with high urban and tourism value from agricultural and forestry regimes; and other well-known natural and human-induced factors such as industry, tourism, and forest fires. In addition to prolonged and severe summer droughts and high air temperatures, the observed trends towards arid conditions in precipitation and drought index series, as well as the increasing desertification force of climatic factors in the Mediterranean and Aegean regions, further support this view. In the implementation of drought management and mitigation actions, as well as in the control of desertification and in the preparation and execution of action plans to combat desertification, contemporary, objective, testable, and multivariate approaches and methods must be used that are suitable for the countries’ or regions’ geographic conditions and socio-economic development levels—without disregarding traditional and cultural experiences, habits, and practices.
Drought, which is one of the consequences of climate variability, is considered an unusual weather and climate event or a natural disaster. Since the impact of drought on agriculture results from a combination of highly complex factors, it is not always possible to separate them from one another.
Similarly, in the case of desertification—which arises from the convergence of highly complex factors and processes in arid, semi-arid, and dry sub-humid lands, particularly land degradation and climate change—it is necessary to distinguish and identify the functions, importance, and priority of the natural (physical geographical, hydrological, biological, ecological, and soil-related) and human-induced (such as inappropriate land use, land use change, deforestation, excessive agricultural production, grazing, water use and irrigation, use of agricultural chemicals and fertilizers, etc.) factors that contribute to desertification.
For this purpose, various "decision-making" methods such as the Analytic Hierarchy Process (AHP), Topsis, and Electre can be applied under a certain probabilistic error or risk level (Saaty, 1980; Hwang and Yoon, 1995; etc.).
Furthermore, taking into account scientific studies and related model projections concerning Turkey’s physical geography, characteristics and trends of climate change and variability, drought, and desertification, as well as the country’s vulnerability to these phenomena, the following assessments and recommendations can be made regarding adaptation to climate change and countermeasures for Turkey (Altan et al., 2011; Türkeş, 2011a, 2012ab; Türkeş and Altan, 2012ab; Türkeş and Tatlı, 2008, 2009, 2010, 2011a; Türkeş et al., 2011): (1) Due to the prevailing climate and especially precipitation climatology, Turkey is not a water-rich country. Therefore, considering the possibility that the ongoing aridification trend in the central and eastern Mediterranean basins and in Turkey since the early 1970s may intensify, it is imperative to formulate and urgently implement realistic water policies supported by legislation to prevent the serious water shortages likely to be faced in the future. (2) In agriculture, the use of pressure-closed pipe irrigation methods (including sprinkler and drip irrigation), which minimize water loss through seepage and evaporation, should be encouraged instead of traditional irrigation practices, and appropriate financial support should be provided to producers willing to adopt these methods. (3) One of the most significant consequences of human-induced climate change (i.e., global climate change associated with an intensified greenhouse effect) is the projected increases in the severity, duration, and affected areas of forest fires in Turkey (manifestations of which are already observed in many parts of the subtropical climate belt, in countries bordering the Mediterranean basin to the west and north, and within Turkey itself). Taking into account the observed decreasing trends in precipitation since the early 1970s, the ever-present risk and danger of forest fires in Turkey may, as in the hot and dry summers of 2007 and 2008, become an even greater problem in many regions.
(4) In addition to prevailing climatic conditions and observed climatic changes, the possible impacts of global climate change—which is attributed to the intensified greenhouse effect and other human activities such as deforestation—on water resources, drought, and desertification in Turkey must be continuously monitored and assessed. (5) Options regarding the potential effects of climate change on water resources and desertification should include the more effective, rational, and economical management of existing water and land resources, the protection of forests, the monitoring of key indicators of desertification such as changes in soil, biological productivity and diversity, and vegetation cover, as well as drought early warning systems. (6) Problems arising from the overuse and mismanagement of groundwater resources should be scientifically identified and monitored, and solutions should be implemented and controlled through feasible and stringent legal regulations.
In this context, groundwater reservoirs should be preserved and reserved for “bad” days; as is the practice in developed countries, unless absolutely necessary, surface waters fed by precipitation (such as major rivers with regular regimes, artificial ponds, and dams, etc.) should be used instead of groundwater for irrigation, energy, and industry.
It should be used.
In addition, especially for urban drinking water supply, water structures (such as ponds, dams, etc.) constructed solely for drinking water purposes and, provided that it is sustainable and environmentally conscious, natural spring waters should be utilized.
(7) Taking into account the prospect of hotter and drier conditions in the future, agricultural crop varieties suitable for more drought-tolerant and warmer climates should be identified; research projects, especially those focused on adaptation, should be developed and supported. (8) Principles of sustainable agriculture and forestry should be implemented; the improper use of agricultural and forest lands should be prevented; and, considering that forests serve as carbon sinks, sufficient budgetary resources should be allocated for afforestation, reforestation, erosion control, and the improvement of pastures and meadows.
(9) Turkey must implement and enforce a “Drought Management Plan” (DMP) that is seriously prepared at national, regional, and basin or local scales, and that is legally supported, in order to avoid being caught unprepared and without water during significant droughts, regardless of whether these are related to global climate change. The DMP should include relevant and authorized institutions that can explain drought and, when necessary, provide drought alerts, as well as institutions and universities that work on and generate information regarding drought (disaster) analysis and monitoring, assessment of the effects of drought (meteorological, agricultural, hydrological, socioeconomic), and the identification of geographic areas and socioeconomic sectors that are vulnerable to the effects of drought.
(10) Applicable to all affected countries, Turkey's program to combat desertification and reduce the impacts of drought events must include an effective, functional, and multidisciplinary/multi-sectoral “National Action Program,” which should also encompass a contemporary Drought Management Plan. Since most of the commonly used drought indices—an essential component of such a Drought Management Plan for drought (disaster) analysis and monitoring—are sector- and system-based, it is necessary to utilize multivariate and multi-purpose indices to analyze, characterize, and monitor drought. Furthermore, the information contained and explained by drought indices, as well as the observed impacts of drought events on various systems and sectors, drought risk levels, and various data on the vulnerability of different geographical units and crop varieties to drought, should be examined and validated together.
