Ankara University, Journal of Faculty of Educational Sciences, year: 2004, vol: 37, no: 2, 61-82 Globalization, Knowledge Society, and Education Refik BALAY* ABSTRACT: The aim of this study is to analyze the previously presented views on globalization, the knowledge society, and education, and to examine the changes experienced in education in light of recent developments. In this context, the study focuses on globalization, the positive and negative aspects of globalization, the knowledge society, changes in the educational process, curricula in the process of change, the school, school administrators, teachers, and students, and discusses how these are reflected in education.
Key Words: Globalization, information society, and education Introduction The most significant phenomenon of the world we live in today is globalization.
It is necessary to continually re-examine the process that emerges as individuals and societies become increasingly aware of what is happening in the world, are influenced by each other’s actions and experiences, and share and disseminate these.
Although globalization is a relatively new concept and much has been written about it to date, it remains an ongoing process. As the experiences arising from this process spread and are shared, it will become possible to better understand its causes and consequences, and the potential future trends that may emerge will also be more clearly grasped. Some of the significant changes brought about by globalization include developments in social structure, population diversification, economic transformation, and shifts in family forms and lifestyles. All of these appear to be closely related to the sharing and dissemination of knowledge at a global level.
Rather than examining the social, political, economic, and cultural domains of change brought about by globalization, this study aims to provide information on the issues of the information society and changes in education as reflective processes of globalization, to analyze previously expressed views on this subject, and to discuss new trends emerging in this area.
The Phenomenon of Globalization The term “Küreselleşme,” used as the Turkish equivalent of globalization, initially emerged as a concept with economic content (Tezcan, 1996, 187). Although the term contains some ambiguity in this sense, the relationships and new lifestyles that have appeared on a global scale in recent years provide certain insights into the nature of globalization. According to Toulmin (1999, 906), individuals and societies have increasingly engaged in activities that overlap and even transcend national borders. Travel, communication, finance, trade, sports competitions, professions, and even popular music can no longer be confined within the borders of a single country. Many similar relationships and activities have acquired an international character. From this perspective, globalization, as a historical phenomenon and process, can be said to have further enriched relations among individuals and societies. People from different countries come together, exchange goods, services, and ideas, and benefit from one another’s experiences. All these developments demonstrate that people are transitioning from national patterns of thought and relationships to new patterns of interaction and thinking on an international scale.
Accordingly, globalization refers to worldwide economic, political, and cultural integration; the global use of ideas, views, practices, and technologies; the universalization of capital circulation; the emergence of new forms of relationships and interactions that transcend nation-state boundaries; the shrinking of distances; the world becoming smaller; unlimited competition; free movement; the expansion of markets on a global scale; and, in short, the transformation of the world into a single market (Kaçmazoğlu, 2002, 49). On the other hand, globalization is also closely associated with the concept of “competitiveness.” This concept, which has emerged alongside globalization, refers to a country's ability to sustain its “national policy” in a way that can meet the requirements of the global market more effectively than its competitors (Toulmin, 1999, 906). Globalization has diminished the economic roles of national governments and accelerated a process in which global competition is increasingly taking place not between countries, but between international corporations. Today, global competition is less between England and Germany, Japan and the United States, or Europe and the United States, and more between Fuji and Kodak, Boeing and Airbus, or Compaq and Toshiba. This process also signals an increasing conflict of objectives between the business world and nation-states. The fact that international companies already demand the cheapest labor, the lowest taxes, and the least stringent environmental protection laws demonstrates that this process will intensify conflicts of interest and objectives (Toulmin, 1999, 905). Regardless of the perspective, technological developments and economic and ideological factors can be counted among the main drivers of globalization. The decreasing cost and widespread availability of information technologies have accelerated the flow of information, changed perceptions of time, space, and distance, and, by accelerating the process of culturalization, created a foundation for the emergence of global values (Yurdabakan, 2002, 63). As this ongoing process concerns all societies on earth, it is a phenomenon that deserves close attention.
Positive and Negative Approaches to Globalization Kurul Tural (2004, 62-63) states that there are both positive and negative perspectives regarding globalization in various dimensions. According to the first positive discourse, the source of inequalities in societies is not structural but individual. The responsibility for an individual's unemployment lies not with economic processes, but with the individual who cannot adapt to change. The second positive discourse draws attention to the concept of justice. It argues that as long as the "welfare rights" of individuals who cannot adapt to the globalizing world—rights they have not actually earned—are defended, economic entrepreneurship is hindered. This leads to the perpetuation of a problematic concept of justice. The third positive discourse focuses more on the state's role in regulating social and economic life. According to this view, the more the state reduces its function of regulating social and economic life and evaluates this function within a moral framework based largely on equal individual opportunity, the more democratic it can be. Thus, a democratic state—by having more of the characteristics required by individualism and the understanding of freedom, and less state intervention—can provide greater benefits for individuals.
Refik BALAY On the other hand, there are also discourses that take a negative view of globalization. The first of these discourses evaluates globalization as an "ideological construct." According to this perspective, globalization is an ideological tool that serves to reinforce the dominance of global capital and the free market over the world. In this sense, globalization emerges as a process that should be resisted.
The second negative discourse emphasizes the cultural dimension of globalization. According to this view, globalization is evaluated negatively because it is seen as a process that creates a single world dominated by Western values and lifestyles, thereby leading to cultural uniformity on a global scale.
The third negative discourse on globalization is based on the view that it is an independent economic process with its own distinctive laws of operation. According to this discourse, regarding globalization as “an inevitable and irresistible process” means not resisting global capital movements and aligning oneself with the minimalist state approach.
The positive and negative discourses on globalization can be examined more closely in the context of the positive and negative effects created by this process. Indeed, every change and transformation brings with it both positive and negative effects. The positive and negative effects of globalization can be summarized as follows (Bilhan, 1996, 179-180, 183; Tezcan, 1996, 192-194): Positive Effects • With globalization, the boundaries of time and space have gradually expanded over the centuries up to the present, and everything that happens in the world has come to be owned by all of humanity. • Common values of humanity have begun to form, and humans and their creations, that is, culture and civilization, have been reinterpreted. • As concepts such as human rights, freedom, justice, and equality have become widespread, people have started to gain a new sense of self, to believe in themselves, and to feel confident. Individuals who know themselves and gradually grow in self-confidence have discovered their own worth and have come to understand that they should not be anyone's or any institution's servant or slave. • It has been realized that it is not a solution for a country to educate only a select elite, and that for development, it is necessary to have a qualified population that has been raised en masse through an education system reaching the grassroots of society. • In the field of health, many diseases have been overcome through the cooperation of countries, infant and child mortality rates have decreased, life expectancy has increased, and opportunities for a healthy life have expanded. • The free movement of a diversified workforce between countries has been ensured, competition in production and consumption has increased, and people everywhere in the world have started to benefit from each other's production and to consume each other's products. Globalization, the Information Society, and Education • New and common forms of life have begun to emerge among people, and through common educational policies, common perceptions and understandings have developed among citizens of different countries.
Negative Impacts • In a globalizing world, delayed nation-states that have been compelled to integrate with powerful states have been subject to the overt influence of major powers in economic, political, and cultural terms. As a result, a kind of dependency has emerged, national borders have been disregarded, concepts such as national sovereignty and independence have been hollowed out, and imperialist objectives have been legitimized under the guise of globalization. • Globalization has begun to pull societies in two different, and even opposing, directions. On one hand, societies are becoming increasingly closer and more integrated, while on the other, nationalism and ethnic nationalism are fostering processes of fragmentation. These two opposing situations have created a dilemma for member countries: on the one hand, not being left out of the process of globalization, and on the other, preserving national unity. • The production power and consumption opportunities of countries around the world are not the same. In this regard, countries that have completed industrialization are in a more advantageous position.
Globalization has accelerated not only the distribution of wealth and prosperity but also the spread of poverty and misery.
• The number of people participating in the educational process worldwide is steadily increasing. Educational institutions at all levels are using technological opportunities to raise people's levels of knowledge and skills.
In contrast, it has been argued that the number of those not benefiting from education has not decreased, but rather increased.
As can be seen, globalization encompasses both positive and negative aspects in the social, political, economic, and cultural spheres. One of the positive effects of globalization is that it has initiated a process characterized by intensive accumulation of knowledge. This increase in knowledge has spread diversity and richness of information to all areas of life, and as this knowledge has been made available for the benefit of humanity, a noticeable sense of relief has emerged. While people are learning to instantly communicate and share all kinds of information with individuals on the other side of the world, to be informed about their products, and to engage in trade, this process also reveals concerns that rich countries, which have already completed their development, will further weaken poorer nations. On the one hand, globalization eliminates a portion of existing job fields and negatively impacts employment, while on the other hand, it creates new job opportunities for people and teaches different ways of performing work. Refik BALAY As human beings explore worlds beyond themselves, they acquire more knowledge about other people and societies; by utilizing their knowledge and experience, they not only reconstruct themselves but also the societies in which they live. While this situation increases the chance to benefit from other individuals’ and societies’ learning and experiences, it also makes them more open to external influences. As can be understood from the summary above, globalization encompasses both many positive and negative effects at the same time. Individuals and societies should, on the one hand, reinforce and disseminate the positive aspects of this process, and on the other hand, minimize its negative effects to achieve the greatest benefit.
Here, education, as a regulatory institution, has a significant role to play. Education can make important contributions to this ongoing process by retraining people and making individuals and societies compatible with new conditions.
Information Society The concept of information comes from the Latin root “informato” and is used in the sense of “giving form,” “shaping,” and “notifying.” In general terms, information is defined as a “mental product” or “something learned” obtained through thinking, judging, reasoning, reading, observation, and experience. In this sense, information refers to data that has passed through a certain process, has been processed, is meaningful for its possessor, and is assumed or actually holds strategic importance in managerial decision-making (Öğüt, 2003, p. 9). Although there is not yet complete consensus on how to define the level of information reached by contemporary civilization, considering the information explosion caused by the dizzying advancements in science and technology over the past 20 years and the opportunities offered by information technologies for social and economic development, the phase that Toffler describes as the “third wave” has come to be referred to as the “information age,” and the society envisaged in this period as the “information society” (Özden, 2002, p. 15). The informatical century or the information age refers to a new period of social and economic organization in which information is the fundamental resource for production, the production and transmission of information have become widespread, the majority of the workforce is engaged in the production and distribution of information, and change and development have become inevitable through continuous learning and acquisition of knowledge (Öğüt, 2003, p. 5). The information age is described as the most significant development humanity has witnessed since the “Industrial Revolution,” as it has led to the emergence of new educational technologies that make learning possible for everyone (Reddi, 1991, p. 347). Many academic publications argue that the “Information Revolution” will lead to profound changes in the economic, political, social, and cultural spheres as well as in relationships (Kaplan, 1991, p. 5).
Globalization, the Information Society, and Education The changes that began in the mid-20th century and intensified particularly in its last quarter have made transformation necessary not only in economic, social, political, and cultural fields but also in the field of education. In this context, in recent years, many developed and developing countries have implemented numerous innovations to improve their education systems. These innovations include systemic reforms, efforts to establish modern institutions, the provision of modern teaching tools and materials, raising teachers’ levels of professional knowledge and skills, and various policies and practices aimed at enhancing the teaching-learning process through innovations in school administration (Karip, 1996, p. 245).
Change in the Education Process Considering that globalization and the information society are dynamic and ongoing phenomena, it is necessary to revisit and discuss concepts such as education, educated individuals, learning, school, school administrators, teachers, and students. In the context of change in the education process during the global age, the following points should be taken into account (Özden, 2002, p. 17): • Education programs based on knowledge will be implemented. • Environments that foster more thinking, discussion, and research for children will be provided; thus, a societal structure that thinks freely, discusses, researches, and can evaluate its findings will be formed. • Adults will not be excluded from the education process; they will require continuous education to adapt to education and technology. • Instead of loading encyclopedic information in lessons, the focus will be on deep understanding of subjects and events and on critical thinking. • Rather than simply equipping students with the knowledge they need for the future, schools will shift to teaching how to learn, based on the perspective that the knowledge provided in school will not suffice for a lifetime. • In education, rather than only developing verbal and numerical intelligence, a multidimensional mental development that includes visual, kinesthetic, rhythmic, and self-development will be targeted. In the new era, raising the skill level in education, self-education and development of individuals, and utilizing their individual abilities to the fullest will become prominent. Individuals are expected to acquire skills such as learning how to live a knowledge-based life, analytical thinking, synthesizing information, problem solving, and effective communication. In the face of rapidly multiplying knowledge, the need will arise for people who, rather than knowing everything, are able to identify what information they need, where and how to find it, act selectively—in other words, people who know how to learn (Numanoğlu, 1999, p. 333).
Refik BALAY Almost every individual knows how to learn in one way or another.
However, the main issue here is how we should learn in the information age and in an information-intensive society. Understanding how to learn means grasping the essence of learning itself. According to Garavan (1997, 24), learning how to learn is possible by developing new modes of thinking and changing behavior. In the traditional sense, people who had access to a certain stock of knowledge were valued, but now, the valuable person will be the one who knows the methods of obtaining knowledge (Özdemir, 2000, 21). A. Toffler’s saying, “The illiterate of the future will not be the person who cannot read, but the person who does not know how to learn,” clearly demonstrates the importance of knowing the methods and ways of learning in the new era (Boydak, 2001). All of these indicate that the most decisive factor in shaping individuals and societies in the future will be knowledge. Again, A. Toffler’s statement that “in ancient times the strong won, in the industrial age the rich won; but in the information age, the knowledgeable will win” shows that in the coming period, the success of individuals, institutions, or societies will depend on their effectiveness in producing and using knowledge (Yıldırım, 2001, 3).
Summary information on the changes experienced in education during the transition from an industrial society to an information society is provided in Table 1.
Table 1.
Changing Education Model Criteria Industrial Society Education Model Information Society Education Model Role of the Teacher Teacher who knows everything, transmitter of knowledge, expert in their field Facilitator, guiding teacher Role of the Student Listener, passive, individual work Active, collaborative team work Role of Administrator Management leader Instructional-management leader Learning Method Learning in the classroom Personal research Form of Learning Learning through individual work Learning through team work Education Programs Standard education programs Flexible education programs Employee Development In-service training Organizational learning Criterion for Success Emphasis on memorized knowledge transfer Ability to define concepts multidimensionally Source: (Aytaç, 1999, p. 75; cited in Nartgün, 2002, p. 132). As seen in Table 1, educational models show significant changes during the transition from an industrial society to an information society. Accordingly, in the transition to the information society, the guiding teacher, students who learn through teamwork, administrators based on instructional-management leadership, learning methods dependent on personal research, flexible education programs, organizational learning, and multidimensional conceptual learning criteria become important.
Globalization, the Information Society, and Education The School in the Information Society In every era, the education system has fulfilled functions such as transmitting the cultural heritage of society through the school, facilitating the socialization of the child, fostering a spirit of unity and solidarity within the society, and cultivating individuals who can drive innovation and change. In order to achieve these objectives, education must carry itself forward with innovation, and the school must serve as a regulating and adapting institution in this process (Tezcan, 1992, pp. 48-51).
The functions of the school are not limited to these alone. As in previous periods, the economic function continues to stand out as one of the important roles of the school today. Economic needs require the effective use of human resources and the preparation of all young people for working life. In a knowledge- and technology-intensive society, obtaining better employment opportunities will become one of the most significant goals for young people in the new era. The responsibility of providing these opportunities to an educated young population has been assigned to schools. Developing a school identity that is sensitive to environmental changes, able to make necessary decisions quickly and accurately, and capable of reflecting the requirements of change into its programs as promptly as possible has become a fundamental necessity (Doğan, 1997, pp. 7, 13-14). As the qualifications sought in employment increasingly depend on more specialized knowledge and skills, the responsibilities of the school have both diversified and increased, making it essential for school-based education to be conducted in a more systematic, transparent, and continuous manner in line with evolving and changing technologies (Tan and others, 2002, p. 8). In the information society, the need for schools has relatively decreased, learning has moved beyond the boundaries of the school, and it has become faster and more enjoyable; nevertheless, this has not diminished the importance of schools in knowledge production—on the contrary, the importance of schools in this regard has increased. This is because, in the information society, knowledge has become not only denser but also more complex in nature. The density and complexity of knowledge impose limitations on individuals who are expected to acquire and use it. In this process, the school can broaden individuals’ horizons by guiding them to become more conscious and selective, enabling them to discover methods and strategies for learning, and systematizing the ways to access information. Innovations necessitate change in the structure of schools and learning environments. As educational institutions, schools must be continuously open to innovation; they must be environments where knowledge is produced, used, and developed, where teamwork fosters trust among individuals, where resources are accessible at any time, where new information needs of society are met, and where the aim is to cultivate original and creative thinking in students—in other words, they must become multi-functional structures. In the new school culture, the most distinctive characteristic is working and learning in teams.
For individuals to be in a constant state of desire for learning, to work with high motivation and high performance, are prerequisites for learning and achieving success as a team (Numanoğlu, 1999, p. 345).
Today, it is increasingly argued that many schools are unable to generate the type of learning required by new reforms. The inability to produce the necessary learning stems not so much from schools’ unwillingness, but rather from their lack of knowledge about how to achieve it. Therefore, the need to restructure schools is growing steadily. This means that the school must become a true “learning school” for both students and teachers (Leithwood et al., 1998, pp. 243-244).
The idea of the learning school can be created through a process in which power and authority are not concentrated in a single hand, and the authority to make decisions regarding the school is distributed in a balanced manner among school administrators, teachers, families, students, and members of the community. In this process, rather than standardized conformity, differences are supported and managed effectively (Oswald, 1997, pp. 181-184). On the other hand, Senge (1990) argues that schools, in their current operations, are not learning organizations. According to him, the excessive rule-orientation of schools, the necessity to comply with this rigidity in the teaching process, administrators not being accustomed to collaborative work, the lack of a clear and unifying vision, and the execution of school activities in a fragmented manner all appear to be significant obstacles preventing schools from becoming learning organizations.
According to him, in its current operation, the concept of school evokes the fulfillment of orders rather than a spirit of entrepreneurship and foresight (Morrison 1998, 166).
Education Programs in the Information Society The conception of education in the information society necessitates the implementation of flexible curricula within educational institutions that can accommodate and diversify to meet the differing interests and abilities observed among students. In addition to common compulsory courses, the inclusion of elective courses designed to address students’ varying interests, abilities, and needs requires the creation of separate curricula for each student group (Kuzgun et al., 1997, p. 28). Educational programs should be oriented away from narrowly focused skills toward productive and broadly based knowledge and skills (Doğan, 1997, p. 18).
In its conventional form, teaching in schools is often limited to the transfer of a series of facts to students and their memorization.
The curriculum does not contain content that develops students’ thinking and problem-solving skills. For a student whose thinking ability has not developed, the greatest mental activity will be to recall the stored information, but not to know how and where to use that information (Özden, 2002, pp. 18-19).
However, in the Information Age, being “knowledgeable” means developing one’s learning capacity, being able to use knowledge, acquiring new skills, and turning this into a continuous behavior (Yıldırım, 2001, p. 6). The skills acquired must meet the needs of both individuals and society in every domain.
These skills Globalization, Information Society, and Education should, on the one hand, regulate and harmonize the relationships between the individual and society, and on the other hand, equip the individual with the socio-cultural and psychological competencies necessary to live in harmony with both themselves and, above all, with society and the wider universe.
On the other hand, in order to provide educational services that will equip all students with the skills sought in the professional world, it is also necessary to implement different program arrangements and methods. For this, the limitations of place, time, and methods in teaching need to be eliminated (Doğan, 1997, p. 19).
In this process, Bruner, who considers the student as an active problem solver, also regards the new learning-teaching process as one that helps the student to discover manageable or solvable problems (Wood, 1998, p. 168). This process is not the same for every student.
It is now a widely accepted fact that people use different methods in cognitive activities such as learning, processing information, and problem-solving. According to Kolb (1984), people who examine events from different perspectives and solve problems using different approaches will also demonstrate differences in the way they learn. He asserts that while some people learn through feeling and assimilation, others learn about events and phenomena in an abstract manner, without associating them with themselves, by processing them through a logical filter. Furthermore, a group of people learn by observing objects and phenomena, in contemplation, through a slow process and filtering them from a certain perspective, whereas another group prefers to immediately try out and learn new information by doing (Özden, 2000, p. 76). As can be seen, the view that students with different thinking styles also learn differently has shifted the focus in education toward the student.
The development of democratic attitudes and habits has also played an important role in this change. According to Kepenekçi-Karaman (2003, p. 45), in democratic education, the individual—that is, the student—is at the center of education. Each individual is recognized as a unique personality and is respected as such. In this type of education, the responsibility for learning lies more actively with the student, while the teacher assumes the role of facilitator. On the other hand, the student must evolve from the type who thinks narrowly and in a limited manner, into an “intellectual student” who can reach beyond existing knowledge, discover, and aim to go further than the information at hand. This requires a transition from single-loop learning to double-loop learning. Single-loop learning is the kind of learning a person needs to complete a task and to apply existing methods to work (Bennett, 1999, p. 118). Individuals inclined toward single-loop learning tend to disregard stimulating behaviors. These people respond to environmental changes with a partial perspective, generally reacting to problems in a limited way, at a single level, and through mechanical actions.
Forced conformity, risk avoidance, and routine behaviors hinder approach diversity, experimentation, and risk-taking for these individuals.
Individuals with single-loop learning do learn, but they do so within narrowly defined parameters. This type of learning can be sustained as long as the environment remains relatively stable. However, when the environment becomes dynamic and changeable, transitioning to double-loop learning becomes inevitable (McGill et al., 1992, p. 6). In double-loop learning, individuals learn how to alter the assumptions and conditions that are predominantly present in the process of single-loop learning. This level of learning leads to a transformational change in which the current state is fundamentally altered (Argyris & Schön, 1996, p. 21).
In this type of learning, which Senge refers to as “creative learning,” the individual continually identifies and resolves problems based on ongoing experience and feedback from research (Bennett, 1999, p. 118).
School Administrators in the Information Society The changes experienced in education within the information society have also altered the expectations from school administrators. This transformation is a result of the multifaceted changes occurring on a global scale.
John Naisbitt used the concept of “megatrend” to describe broad social, political, economic, and technological changes. According to him, the major transformation that began in the 1980s has carried people and societies into markedly different phases. This change is summarized in Table 2. Accordingly, the major transformation, Table 2.
Megatrends and Administrative Roles • From an industrial society • To an information society • From coercive technology • To human-oriented high technology • From a national economy • To participation in the world economy • From short-termism • To an interest in long-term perspectives • From centralization • To decentralization • From institutional assistance • To self-help • From representative democracy • To participatory democracy • From hierarchical communication and control • To networks • From the interest and efforts of developed countries • To the interest and efforts of developing countries • From “either-or” thinking • To multiple choice options
Source: Balcı (2001, 114).
As seen in Table 2, the major transformation of the past 25 years has brought people and societies into a period that is more flexible, more diverse, broader in perspective, more open to the world, and has greater freedom of movement.
Within the framework of the megatrends summarized above—Globalization, the Information Society, and Education—the following key roles are expected from school administrators. Accordingly, the school administrator (Balcı, 2001, p. 114): • Must possess a high level of strategic planning capacity and be able to see the big picture behind events and relationships. • Should have strong marketing skills. • Needs to create new marketing interests for schools and provide restructured sources of public funding. • Should ensure that schools can offer programs and services that meet the needs of a broad student body and community. • Must ensure that schools become a part of lifelong learning. • Should make schools the central hub for distributing school support services. • Must be aware that national program frameworks do not always diminish the autonomous structure of the school. • Should ensure that the community participates more actively in school decision-making processes than in the past. • Needs to recognize that there will be greater emphasis on the aims and outcomes of schools and remember that providing the best for students will become the central discourse and interest in a highly educated society. Increasing demands for improved quality and expanded services have brought the new role of school administrators to the forefront. School administrators must now act as school leaders and assume greater initiative. The system should provide them with opportunities for professional development and training to gain these competencies and should, to some extent, tolerate learning from mistakes.
School administrators need to be able to understand and manage change. In order to achieve effective change, they must also adapt to the active role of transformational leadership.
The school administrator should motivate employees, create a supportive culture, develop a shared group vision, design effective educational programs, establish a positive learning environment, encourage high performance, and, as the person primarily responsible for outcomes, be able to utilize human resources in the most effective way (Smith & Piele, 1997, pp. 3–4). In a multicultural setting, rather than hierarchical decision-making, school administrators are expected to consult with various individuals and groups to reach a consensus. The capacity for strategic planning and preparing an agenda for personal change has become increasingly important.
In an environment characterized by such a rapid pace of change, school administration is both a difficult and complex endeavor (Balcı, 2001, p. 115).
Refik BALAY The school administrator should facilitate the changes and innovations required by the circumstances by recognizing the opportunities that come with the new situation, rather than resisting change.
Having skills in managing knowledge, using intuition, persuading, and engaging in continuous learning are among other important qualities of a school administrator (Numanoğlu, 1999, p. 345).
Since the 1980s, the image of the school administrator has shifted from that of a "gatekeeper" to that of an "instructional leader" (Balcı, 2001, p. 116). Administration is now a profession characterized more by disciplined work and taking on responsibility than by the exercise of authority and power (Özden, 2002, pp. 8-9).
The point reached in education today has made us realize the importance of acting intuitively rather than reactively. It has become important to anticipate a problem's emergence from various signs before it fully manifests itself and displays all its consequences, rather than waiting for the problem to arise and reveal its outcomes.
The school administrator must stand out as someone who can control objectives in a dynamic environment characterized by rapid change, and who possesses strong intuition (Biçer, 1999, pp. 94-95). In the new process, school administrators are expected to mobilize their employees toward specific goals.
One of the prerequisites of leadership is to bring people together around a common and shared vision that will inspire them, and to develop strategies to bring this vision to life (Limerick et al., 1994, p. 38).
It is now evident that the role of the school administrator has shifted from merely influencing the implementation of specific innovations in a narrow sense to leading change within the school. This, in a sense, constitutes the distinction between management and leadership.
Leadership is focusing on mission, power, and work; whereas management involves designing and implementing plans, getting things done, and working effectively with people.
While leadership involves communicating a vision, achieving shared ownership, and engaging in developmental planning, a manager deals with resource issues and strives for coordinated work (Balcı, 2001, p. 116). During the change process, school leaders will assume the duty and responsibility of developing a vision and sharing it with everyone.
One of the most valuable benefits of a vision is that it acts like a magnet; it attracts people and brings them together (Maxwell, 1999, 164). A vision is an image of a future state that we greatly desire to achieve, a picture of the point we want to reach. Vision is about drawing the picture of the future together and making people feel that this picture is being created collectively. It is a strong sense of the future. While exceeding today’s possibilities, it also serves as an intellectual bridge that connects today to tomorrow—a foundation created not to justify the past, but to look toward the future (Aytaç, 2003, 2).
Marshall Sashkin also argues that visionary leaders possess a comprehensive repertoire of behaviors that make collaboration possible; by focusing on key issues, communicating effectively, adapting to change, fostering respect for themselves and others, and taking reasonable/measured risks, these leaders create an environment in which the school vision can be realized (Lashway, 1997, p. 135).
Globalization, the Information Society, and Education The Teacher in the Information Society In the globalized information society, it has become increasingly important to take precautions against the potential consequences of the process of change and to prepare individuals and societies for the future in a way that will enable them to cope with change. Therefore, preparation for this process should begin with educational institutions and the teachers who work in them. Individuals’ participation in the rapidly changing information society, their ability to secure their deserved place, acquire new knowledge, enrich their skills, engage in lifelong learning, and attain higher levels of quality than before can only be achieved in this way (Yurdabakan, 2002, p. 63). In the school culture created by the information society, it will become essential for teachers to assume new roles and responsibilities. Teachers must continually update their knowledge in the face of rapid knowledge production. As educators in the information society, teachers will be aware of the educational value of knowledge and will provide effective guidance in accessing it (Numanoğlu, 1999, p. 346). Therefore, teachers will need to be trained with different and new competencies in the coming period. It is desirable for teachers to be equipped with the necessary knowledge, skills, and values to educate students from diverse cultures and socially disadvantaged backgrounds, to resolve existing conflicts peacefully, to respect individuals' identities and cultures, and to act as citizens with a sense of social responsibility (Yurdabakan, 2002, p. 64).
The current system views the teacher as the primary source of knowledge. In this context, the teacher mainly assumes the role of delivering and transmitting information, while the student is positioned passively, merely receiving what is conveyed. This approach stifles the entrepreneurial spirit within the student; the student is unable to develop self-directed learning skills and is not given the opportunity to progress in line with their abilities during the educational process (Doğan, 1997, p. 9).
In the information society, the fundamental role of the teacher is to place the student at the center of the learning environment and to teach by creating appropriate conditions that show how to access information and how to benefit from it. Teachers, as experts in selecting information, should be able to equip students with essential knowledge. For the student, this means learning how to learn. However, here the teacher must first know their student well, provide learning opportunities suitable for the student’s level, facilitate the learning process, and guide the student in the right direction (Numanoğlu, 1999, p. 347).
The Student in the Information Society Educational change in the global information society entails preparing students who can adapt to the new era and necessitates modifications to educational programs for this purpose. In this context, even during their school years, Refik BALAY students who think globally are cultivated. The aim is to develop a youth that assumes responsibility and forms attitudes at the global level. This youth demonstrates the most appropriate stance on a global scale while at the same time not neglecting local needs (Tezcan, 2002, p. 57). In global education practices, the goal is to provide all the fundamental strategic elements needed to create a livable future, to foster reconciliation, and to eliminate fatalism and apathy. Moreover, the aim is to cultivate students who are open to the world, self-confident, advocates of equality, value cultural and spiritual richness, share common passions, and are committed to creating a transformative and peaceful world (Doğan, 2002, p. 90).
While processes that fuel inequalities and imbalances both within and between countries show no signs of slowing down, another subject of debate is how individuals and societies will progress toward a better, more humane, and more peaceful future—and how they will achieve this. The concepts of competition and cooperation appear to be the most prominent in this context. Competition, a concept closely linked to economic globalization, has now also entered the realm of universities. Accordingly, efforts by universities in developed countries to attract international students and researchers within the framework of global competition have become increasingly widespread. Many universities in developed countries have adopted internationalization as one of their main missions. According to UNESCO data, in 1995, there were 1.5 million foreign students enrolled in higher education in 50 host countries. More than 800,000 students from developing countries registered in the higher education systems of other countries for the purpose of studying abroad (Kurul Tural, 2004, 95). Among these efforts, student exchange programs implemented through international cooperation to foster common understanding in the field of education are also notable. The European Community-ERASMUS education project, which began relatively recently among European countries and of which our country is now a member, includes student exchanges. According to this project, students can take courses from universities of their choice in one semester without interrupting their studies and, in another semester, choose different countries. In this way, students gain the opportunity to discover different cultures and benefit from a variety of perspectives and ideas. Through this project, millions of students will undergo rotation between countries, thereby laying the foundations for integration among them (Tezcan, 2002, 58).
A good example of how the educational aims and systems are affected by globalization is the United Kingdom. According to data, the flow of students from various countries to the United Kingdom shows a growing trend. In the 1995-1996 academic year, 196,346 students in the United Kingdom were financed by the public administrations of their own countries. In the same year, among the students studying in the United Kingdom, 43% came from European Community countries, 6% from other European countries, 7% from Africa, 29% from Asia, 1% from Australia, 5% from the Middle East, 7% from North America, and 1% from South America (Kurul Tural, 2004, p. 97).
In the new process, the student will move beyond old narrow molds and become an individual who learns how to learn under the guidance of the teacher (Numanoğlu, 1999, p. 347). From the first to the final stage of instruction, students will be introduced to skills such as identifying problems, developing possible solutions, determining and implementing the most reasonable solution among those proposed, and making necessary corrections by comparing the results obtained with the primary objectives.
Young people can only be raised with the ability to understand, interpret, and develop knowledge in this way (Doğan, 1997, p. 18).
In such a system, students will be able to analyze and synthesize ideas, share what they have understood with others both orally and in writing, structure their learning within a solid framework, answer questions by truly comprehending the subject, and relate what they learn to everyday life (Tan et al., 2002, p. 9). Students of the information society will become individuals who, by utilizing the opportunities of technology, can produce projects, work in different project groups, possess teamwork skills, think independently, have a high imagination, make rational decisions, participate actively, seek out knowledge, think, be productive, remain free of prejudice, and respect all kinds of ideas (Numanoğlu, 1999, p. 348).
CONCLUSION The contemporary world has begun to be defined by the rapid and sudden processes of change occurring in the social, political, economic, and cultural spheres. The global dissemination of information has initiated an information-based economic process that, while providing advantages to developed countries, has also created various obstacles for those that have yet to complete their development. Once again, people and societies are experiencing one of the critical turning points in history. Although hardly anyone can predict exactly when or what will happen, it seems that most have understood why these changes are taking place. At the heart of this major transformation lie, on one hand, systematic research and development efforts and, on the other, the increasing share of the information sector in overall employment. For example, today, as a result of computer technology, about 45% of the workforce in the United States is employed in information processing, 25% in the service sector, and another 25% in industry (Webb et al. 1992, p. 569). As can be seen, there is a transition from an economy based on manufacturing industries to one based on services and information technology. It is also important not to overlook the endless potential and creativity of humankind and its intellect in this development. This difficult and tumultuous process, which began with the creative intelligence of humanity, will again be constructed by that very intelligence.
As H. G. Wells stated, "the history of humanity is a race between education and catastrophe" (Slaus et al., 2004, p. 479).
Refik BALAY Education has always held a special significance in every society. On one hand, education involves looking to the past without being bound by it; on the other, it turns to the future, offering human creativity a pleasurable pursuit and exploration. In this sense, education is a tool for reconstruction for both the individual and society. As long as the world turns and human curiosity and the desire to learn persist, education will have a major role in enabling individuals and societies to continually re-create themselves. Humanity must learn to walk steadily on the slippery ground on which it rapidly advances, and in order to succeed, must continually update its ever-changing roadmap. Our world is moving toward an increasingly diverse and differentiated life, filled with different people, various social and cultural needs, new lifestyles, and distinct habits. Consequently, a challenge arises: how to preserve and maintain the integrity of a social environment that is becoming increasingly diverse and prone to fragmentation.
As Balcı (1995, p. 114) notes, the more uncertain and unpredictable the environment, the greater the need for both differentiation and integration. However, integration and differentiation are processes that operate in opposition to one another; in other words, the greater the differentiation, the more difficult it becomes to achieve unity. From a cultural perspective, globalization holds together two opposing phenomena. On one hand, there is an increasingly differentiated world; on the other, efforts toward standardization aim to rein in this tendency toward differentiation or dispersion. While the lifestyles of societies are becoming more and more similar, at the same time, with increasing differentiation, the multiple identities and characteristics of societies come to the fore. In this way, more local elements are being produced within the global, while at the same time, efforts to homogenize these “multiple faces” continue (Kurul Tural, 2004, p. 66). Balancing these two opposing phenomena falls primarily to education, which serves as an adapting institution within society. Educational programs need to be redesigned so that individuals and communities can better know and learn more about one another. As mutual understanding increases, it can be said that the distinction between “us” and “them” will gradually diminish over time.
However, even if physical distances are overcome and people come together in person, the prevailing view is that they will become alienated from one another spiritually.
This situation means that people are connected to each other in an instrumental way.
People may, perhaps, find the minimum conditions for living together with their differences all around the world, and to some extent, efforts may be made to achieve a certain degree of unity.
However, it is predicted that interpersonal relationships will become increasingly mechanical. To mitigate this drawback to some extent, it will be necessary to strengthen people's spiritual, emotional, and self-awareness intelligences. Therefore, giving more emphasis to these areas in the future may become one of the essential requirements of education.
The diversification of the population will especially require those working in education to be knowledgeable and experienced in the education of individuals from multicultural and different socio-economic backgrounds. With a new interdisciplinary approach, world history, cultures, and geography will be taught anew from a global perspective; as the flow of information, free movement, and student exchanges increase, people's desire to learn about and understand other cultures will grow. Education policies will be designed to be sensitive to the problems of students coming from different cultural and religious backgrounds, and school administrators and teachers will become more respectful of individual preferences in education.
The increasing volume of trade between countries will connect societies not only economically but also socially and culturally to a greater extent; however, as in the past, the transfer of culture will continue to occur from the strong to the weak.
Multidimensional relationships and increasing complexity will require the cultivation of individuals with intellectual flexibility who can process information about complex systems and think creatively and abstractly on an interdisciplinary level.
Schools will bear a significant responsibility in preparing the workforce of the future. Since there will be serious expectations not only in terms of work but also regarding social responsibilities from future employees, schools will have to include moral and ethical education in addition to vocational and technical education.
Learning will extend beyond the walls of the school; geographic and cultural boundaries will no longer serve as obstacles to learning; universities will re-examine their teacher education programs; and the ability of individuals to learn and speak multiple languages will become increasingly important. As individuals’ abilities to solve problems alone are expected to weaken, collaborative learning and problem solving will gain importance. Existing problems will be addressed not through a single discipline but through a multidisciplinary approach, by coordinating knowledge, concepts, and skills acquired from different fields. Greater emphasis will be placed on enhancing dialogue between people, and teaching both verbal and non-verbal communication skills will become among the important aims of education.
