TEVHİDE KARGIN FOCUS GROUP IN SPECIAL EDUCATION Ankara University Faculty of Educational Sciences Journal of Special Education 2004, 5 (2) 1-13 RESEARCH ARTICLES Main Article: Mainstreaming: Definition, Development, and Principles Tevhide Kargın∗ Ankara University As a result of developments in the field of special education, today many students with special needs are educated in general education classrooms. In practice, the education of students with special needs together with their peers without special needs is considered mainstreaming; however, it is acknowledged that mainstreaming does not merely refer to physical coexistence. This article attempts to explain what the concept of mainstreaming means, as well as the factors that have led to the emergence of the concept of mainstreaming worldwide and in Turkey. In addition, the support services required for mainstreaming and the principles for successful mainstreaming are outlined.
Keyword: Mainstreaming.
they are individuals with specific needs.
The question of which environments should address the education of these individuals is a topic within the field of special education. Individuals with specific needs differ from their peers in terms of physical, intellectual, emotional, and social characteristics.
Dr., Ankara University Faculty of Educational Sciences, Department of Special Education, Ankara.
E-mail: tkargin@hotmail.com TEVHİDE KARGIN Special Education and Mainstreaming in the World In parallel with the observed developments, special education and mainstreaming have been addressed in different forms worldwide. In the historical process, the first settings where the education of specific needs students was provided were separate residential/day special education schools. However, today, mainstreaming education has gained wider acceptance and adoption both globally and in our country. The concept of mainstreaming, which is defined as the education of specific needs students in general education classrooms on the condition that special education support services are provided to the classroom teacher and/or the student with disabilities (Kırcaali-İftar, 1992), is fundamentally based on: (1) specific needs students attending the same school as their siblings in the family and their peers, (2) being in the same classroom as peers of the same age, and (3) the provision of special education support services to the student and/or the teacher as needed (York and Tundidor, 1995). Accordingly, mainstreaming does not mean that specific needs students are educated together with peers of the same age in the same classroom environment without any special education support. On the contrary, it refers to the continuation of the education of specific needs students in general education classrooms together with special education support. Before explaining how mainstreaming is carried out and what special education support means, it will be useful to examine the events and historical development that have influenced the formation of the idea of mainstreaming in our country and around the world.
Although the first systematic efforts for the education of individuals with specific needs emerged in the 16th century (Gearheart, Weishahn, and Gearheart, 1996), it is known that school enrollment continued rapidly from the 1700s until the mid-1900s (http://www.indiana.edu/intell/ita rd/htm, 2004; http://www.teacherlink.ed.usu.edu/ tlresources/units/champions/cler). The first half of the 1900s was a period in which the number of special education schools for different disability groups increased rapidly. In these years, in the United States and many European countries, services for students with specific needs were provided through special education schools (OECD, 1995). During this period, it was a widespread belief that students with specific needs could not be educated in general education classrooms. It was argued that full-time special education schools, staffed by specially trained teachers and organized with specially designed environments, provided the best settings for students with specific needs. It was thought that students who differed physically, intellectually, or emotionally-socially from their peers would negatively affect other children, and it was decided to place them in separate schools.
An example of this situation is the mainstreaming program initiated in 1913 in the United States for visually impaired students of average intelligence, which was one of the first implementations of mainstreaming during those years. In this program, visually impaired students spent part of the school day in general education classrooms with their peers and the other part attending a special class designed for the visually impaired. Another development in mainstreaming during this period was the emphasis on the collaboration between special education and general education schools, as stipulated by a resolution adopted in England in 1928. Following this, a law passed in 1944 endorsed the view that students who were deemed suitable should continue their education in general education classrooms (Lindsay, 2003). Worldwide, the period from the 1960s to the present is considered the era in which the most rapid and striking developments in mainstreaming have occurred. When the factors leading to the emergence of the idea of mainstreaming during this time are listed, the foremost issues are the problems encountered in practice with education in special education classrooms and the increasing criticisms directed at this form of education.
Until the mid-1900s worldwide, the primary objective of special education classes, which were widely adopted, was to ensure that students with disabilities received education in the same school as their peers, but in separate classrooms with students who had similar types of disabilities. It was believed that, to some extent, these classes enabled the mainstreaming of students with disabilities with their non-disabled peers.
An example of this can be seen in the case of a 13-year-old student with a physical disability who was transferred from a general education school to a special education school. The reasons cited for this decision included that the student’s physical condition and illness were upsetting and disturbing to the teacher and other students, that the student demanded too much of the teacher’s attention and time, distracted other students, and had a negative impact on discipline and progress at the school (Lewis and Doorlag, 1999).
This example is striking in that it reflects the attitudes of society toward individuals who were different at that time.
Until the 1900s, due to the lack of adequate special education services in the United States, many students with specific needs were placed in general education classrooms. Students who were placed in general education classrooms without special education support continued their education with the efforts of classroom teachers.
At the end of the educational process, it was decided to remove students who were deemed unlikely to benefit from instruction at present or in the near future, or who were considered to be harmful to other children, from education (Lewis and Doorlag, 1999; Mastropieri and Scruggs, 2000).
In those years, the concepts of “education” or “care” were discussed more than the debate between “education together” or “separate education.”
Although the prevailing view until the mid-1900s was that students with specific needs should be educated in special education schools, it is noteworthy that, albeit limited, various perspectives and efforts toward mainstreaming also began to emerge during this period.
In 1913, it was aimed to mainstream students with their peers in classes—especially in non-academic subjects such as art and music. Additionally, since special education classrooms were designed for students with disabilities in terms of personnel, equipment, physical facilities, and curriculum, it was expected that students would benefit more from these classes. However, the expectation that special education classrooms, prepared for students with disabilities, would lead to greater benefit for these students did not produce the anticipated results in practice. It was first realized that many students with specific needs in these classes did not require full-day special education; on the contrary, they would benefit more from interaction with their non-disabled peers (Lewis and Doorlag, 1999). Furthermore, since special education classrooms were made up of students who were academically similar to one another, it was observed that students with specific needs in these settings did not have access to more successful role models and tended to keep their own expectations low. Similarly, teachers working in these classrooms were seen to have low expectations for these students as well, attributing their lack of learning to the students' disabilities instead of the methods or programs used (Gearheart et al., 1996).
As a result of criticisms in the 1960s regarding the education of students with specific needs in special education schools/classrooms, and various court cases that were brought to trial, mainstreaming practices began to be included in the laws of many countries starting from the 1970s.
In Italy in 1971, in Norway in 1976, in England in 1974, and in the United States and France in 1975, laws were enacted that made it legally permissible for students with specific needs to receive education together with their peers in general education classrooms (OECD, 1995).
Although special education classrooms were initially regarded as beneficial in many respects, it was observed in practice that they did not provide the anticipated advantages; on the contrary, they even brought about numerous problems. Firstly, it was found that, contrary to initial expectations, education in special education classrooms did not result in mainstreaming, even partially.
On the contrary, it was observed that special education classrooms in general education schools divided students into two groups—disabled and non-disabled—and that these classes were often placed in the least desirable corners of the school (such as the basement or next to the storage room). It was also noted that special education classrooms separated students with disabilities not only during lessons but also during extracurricular activities. In some schools, recess, meal times, and ceremonies were scheduled at different times for students in the special education class and non-disabled students, in order to prevent negative interactions between the two groups (Lewis and Doorlag, 1999).
For these reasons, it was observed that special education classes did not result in mainstreaming as intended.
Second, in terms of personnel, curriculum, and equipment in special education classes— MAINSTREAMING: DEFINITION, DEVELOPMENT, AND PRINCIPLES The Education of All Handicapped Children Act (PL 94-142), enacted in the United States in 1975, is a comprehensive law aimed at ensuring that school-aged children with disabilities benefit from appropriate educational services. Several significant decisions regarding the education of individuals with disabilities were made under this law, which was passed in the US.
Some of these decisions are as follows (Fiscuss and Mandel, 1983; Kırcaali-İftar, 1998; OECD, 1995): Ending Exclusion from Public School Programs and Services: The law stipulates that every child with a disability of school age has the right to free and appropriate education, and that schools are obligated to accept students with disabilities. It was also decided that the lack of appropriate programs, learning disabilities of the child, or financial difficulties would not be accepted as excuses for denying access. Nondiscriminatory Assessment: It was emphasized that assessment materials and processes must be free from any racial, linguistic, religious, or cultural bias. The adoption of this provision was influenced by instances where children from different cultural or racial backgrounds were misassigned to special education schools. Moreover, the law states that information obtained from a single assessment tool cannot be relied upon. Preparation of an Individualized Education Program (IEP): For every child with a disability who is determined to be eligible for special education services, it was decided that an IEP must be prepared to legally guarantee where, for how long, for what purpose, and by whom the education will be provided. Education in the Least Restrictive Environment: The law stipulates that students with specific needs should be educated according to the principle of the least restrictive environment. This principle means that the student should be placed in an environment where they can interact as much as possible with their non-disabled peers, while also meeting their educational needs in the best way possible. Arranged from the most to the least restrictive, separate residential/day special education schools are considered the most restrictive educational environment, whereas general education classrooms are considered the least restrictive. When determining the least restrictive environment, the goal is to ensure that the student is together with their peers as much as possible. The least restrictive environment is the setting in which the student can achieve the highest level of success. Need for Mainstreaming: The law states that students with disabilities require mainstreaming. The placement of a child in a special education school or special education classroom should occur only when, due to the nature or degree of the disability, education in general education classrooms cannot be successfully achieved even with the help of supportive tools and services.
Right to Legal Practice: This right provides families of children with disabilities and professionals access to evaluation, SPECIAL EDUCATION JOURNAL TEVHİDE KARGIN Salend, 2001; Ysseldyke, Algozzine, and Thurlow, 2000).
With this concept, it was emphasized that public spaces commonly used by the general population in Europe and the United States—such as parks, cinemas, markets, and public transportation—should also be arranged to accommodate individuals with disabilities.
Special Education and Mainstreaming in Turkey Today, compared to the past, there have been encouraging developments in the educational opportunities provided for individuals with specific needs in our country.
It is possible to examine these developments, which gained momentum in the 1980s, under the headings of the period up to 1950, the period between 1950 and 1980, the period between 1980 and 1990, and the present period.
They were given the opportunity to express their objections regarding placement or the program.
Following the enactment of the law in 1975 in the United States, the rights of children with disabilities to benefit from free education in the least restrictive environment, regardless of the type of their disability, were further expanded and highlighted in the Individuals with Disabilities Education Act (IDEA PL 101-476) adopted in the United States in 1990, and later in the law enacted in 1997 (PL 105-17) (Stainback and Stainback, 1996). In the act passed in 1997, it was stated that the vast majority of students with specific needs should be educated in general education classrooms and that teachers working in these schools should also be recognized as part of the team.
When we look at the period up to the 1950s, the first example we encounter in the education of children with disabilities in our country is the “Enderun School” practice, which is claimed to have been implemented during the Ottoman Empire in the 16th century. Enderun Schools were schools established at that time for the education of gifted children (Ataman, 1984). Regarding the education of individuals who are highly intelligent and gifted, the Idil Biret-Suna Kan Law was adopted during the Republican era in 1948, and thus, the education of the gifted was legally secured (Ataman, 1997).
Another factor that has influenced the adoption of mainstreaming practices today is the acceptance of the concept of “normalization.” This concept first appeared in Danish legislation in the late 1960s and later in legislation in the United States.
The concept of normalization, which is based on providing individuals with specific needs the opportunity to experience ordinary life, not only advocates for these individuals to be educated in general education classrooms, but also envisions that they will benefit equally from opportunities available to other members of society, such as acquiring a home and job, and participating in recreational activities (Lindsay, 2003; Peterson and Hittie, 2003). With regard to special education, apart from the Enderun practice during the Ottoman Empire, we see that classes for children with hearing and visual impairments were opened in Istanbul in 1889 by Grati Efendi.
These classes later became a separate school (Akçamete and Kaner, 1999; Özyürek, 2004), and students with hearing and visual impairments received education at this school for a period of 30 years until 1912. After this school was closed in 1912, no developments in the field of special education are mentioned in the relevant sources until 1921.
It is known that during the War of Independence in 1921, a school named the “Private İzmir Institute for the Deaf and Blind” was opened in İzmir, and that this school subsequently operated under the Ministry of Health and Social Assistance until 1950 (Akçamete and Kaner, 1999).
The first systematic effort to train personnel in the field of special education during this period was the establishment of the Special Education Branch within Gazi Education Institute in 1952. Forty students were admitted to the branch, who, after graduation, would provide services for the development of special education in various parts of the country and lead the training of the necessary personnel in this field (Enç, Çağlar and Özsoy, 1987).
The branch was closed in 1955 after graduating two classes (SPO, 1992).
With the adoption of the Republic, greater importance was given to education, and views supporting the elimination of gender and class distinctions in education were embraced.
However, the country, which was dealing with problems in many areas at that time, was unable to demonstrate any notable progress in education, and especially in the education of individuals with specific needs.
Until the 1950s, it was believed that children with specific needs were ill and uneducable, and during that period, institutions operating under the Ministry of Health and Social Welfare primarily focused on care (Özyürek, 2004).
Another noteworthy development in the records of 1955 was the establishment of the Psychological Service Center, which laid the foundation for the current Guidance and Research Centers present in all our provinces and which play an important role in the integrated delivery of special education services (Özsoy, 1990). This was followed in 1955 by the opening of special classes for students with intellectual disabilities in two primary schools in Ankara (Yeni Turan and Hıdırlıktepe). These two special classes later became the first examples of the lower special class practice, which is still implemented nationwide today (Özsoy, 1990). We observe that significant developments in the field of special education began to take place starting from the 1950s. One of the most striking developments of this period was the transfer of planning and provision of special education services from the Ministry of Health and Social Welfare to the Ministry of National Education. This transfer of special education services from one ministry to another is important as it indicates a shift in perceiving the issue from being a “health concern” to being considered primarily an “educational matter.” The years 1956 and 1957 are also important as they mark the enactment of two significant laws in the field of special education. With Law No. 6660, passed in 1956, the scope of the 1948 law was expanded, and measures were adopted to ensure that gifted and talented children would be educated by the state. In 1957, the “Law on Children in Need of Protection” (Law No. 6972) was enacted. Article 22 of this law stated that necessary measures should be taken by the Ministry of National Education for children in need of protection who require special education (Şura Report, 1999).
It is regarded as a department. The establishment of the Department of Special Education is considered an important development as it serves as a resource for training the personnel needed in the field (Enç, 1972). Offering undergraduate, master's, and doctoral programs, the Department of Special Education has strived to meet the field's needs for personnel, publications, and research, and has made significant contributions.
With the "Special Education Teaching Certificate Program," which began implementation in 1978, teachers working in various fields—and especially elementary school teachers—were awarded special education teaching certificates, thereby training teachers for every branch of special education (Akçamete and Kaner, 1999). The 1970s stand out as years during which an increasing number of students with disabilities received education at the primary school level in special education schools.
During these years, the number of separate boarding and day schools for children in different disability groups increased.
The most important feature of the 1960s is that the rights of individuals in need of special education were guaranteed by the state in the 1961 Constitution.
Article 48 of the 1961 Constitution
Article 48 states, “everyone has the right to social security,” and Article 50 stipulates, “the state shall take measures to render those in need of special education due to their circumstances beneficial to society.”
Additionally, the 1960s are significant in terms of special education, as they were years in which legal regulations continued and personnel training was carried out within universities.
During this period, Article 12 of the Primary Education and Education Law No. 222, published in 1961, stated that “children who are mentally, physically, emotionally, or socially disabled, even though they are within the age range for compulsory primary education, shall be provided with special education and instruction.” With this provision, the education of children with disabilities within the age of compulsory primary education was legally recognized (Gökçe, Kartal, Rıdvanoğlu, Erezkan, and Alışcı, 2002).
Another significant aspect of the 1970s in the field of special education is the "Basic Law of National Education" No. 1739, which was enacted in 1973.
In Article 8 of this law, we see the statement, “special measures shall be taken to educate children in need of special education and protection.” This statement is important in that it demonstrates that special education services are included within the general education system (Gökçe et al., 2002).
In summary, when we look at the period from the 1950s to the 1980s, after the Gazi Education Institute, the second significant beginning in the treatment of special education at the university level in Turkey was the establishment of the Department of Special Education within the Faculty of Education at Ankara University in 1965. The Special Education Teaching Program was launched at the Faculty of Education and produced its first graduates in 1987 (Çağlar, 1990). Following the program opened at Anadolu University, in 1986, Teacher Training Programs for the Visually Impaired and the Intellectually Disabled were initiated at the Department of Special Education within the Faculty of Education at Gazi University. Despite these notable developments, it is evident that these advances primarily concerned the provision of education for individuals with specific needs by the state, with most education taking place in special education schools and classrooms and with no systematic effort toward mainstreaming. For this reason, these years stand out as a period in which the number of special education schools and classrooms increased rapidly. The 1980s, however, were years in which significant developments emerged in the field of special education.
Initially, between 1950 and 1980, special education services were administered by a separate branch directorate under the General Directorate of Primary Education; in 1980, these services were incorporated into the general directorate. With this restructuring, the management of special education services was ensured within the Ministry organization by the general directorate and, at the provincial level, by the Guidance and Research Centers (Akçamete and Kaner, 1999; Şahin, 2003). Thus, a nationwide structure was established for the planning and implementation of special education services.
The 1980s were significant not only in terms of personnel training, but also as years during which numerous laws related to special education were enacted and important advances emerged. With the enactment of Law No. 2916 on “Children in Need of Special Education” in 1983, the first steps toward a comprehensive law specific to individuals with specific needs were taken. This law included sections on definitions, principles, institutions and their responsibilities concerning special education, as well as the identification, placement, and monitoring of children in need of special education; and various regulations were issued in accordance with this law (Şura Report, 1999). Law No. 2916 is important because it was the first to address the education of children with specific needs through mainstreaming. In the Fundamental Principles of Special Education stated in Article 4 of the law, it is noted that “necessary measures shall be taken to ensure that children in need of special education, whose situations and characteristics are appropriate, are educated among their non-disabled peers in schools and educational institutions established for the education of non-disabled children.”
Again, in the same section, it is stated that "starting special education early is essential" and that "special education services should be based on the needs and characteristics of children." Until 1981, the training of university-level personnel in the field of special education was limited to Ankara University; however, in 1981, a "Special Education Certificate Program" was launched at the Eskişehir Academy of Economic and Administrative Sciences—which later became Anadolu University—and efforts to train personnel on a nationwide scale were initiated.
Later, in 1983, Anadolu University decided that the education of students should be planned individually, delivered and monitored by experts in the field (Şura Report, 1999). Although the Council held in 1990 emphasized the need to broaden the scope of mainstreaming, it was also observed during these years that the number of separate boarding and day schools increased. While 75 special education schools were opened between 1950 and 1992, an additional 75 schools were opened between 1992 and 1995, bringing the total number to 150 (Şura Report, 1999).
It is stated as “planned in such a way that, taking the child’s characteristics into account, it will be provided as close to the child as possible” (MoNE, 2000).
As seen in the relevant articles, although the integration of students with specific needs is emphasized in Law No. 2916, the phrase "children in need of special education whose circumstances and characteristics are suitable" indicates that mainstreaming is only considered appropriate for some children and remains ambiguous regarding which children are deemed suitable. Furthermore, the absence of a definition of mainstreaming within Law No. 2916 also brings uncertainty as to how this process should be implemented in practice.
One of the most significant developments of the 1990s was the enactment of Decree Law No. 573 in 1997. This decree constitutes the most comprehensive legal regulation concerning children with specific needs in our country. The Decree addresses the principles related to the identification, assessment, and education of children with specific needs, starting from their initial identification. Under the titles of "Identification-Assessment-Placement," "Early Childhood Education," "Preschool Education," "Primary Education," "Secondary Education," "Higher Education," and "Non-Formal Education," the stages of education are defined in the first section of the second part of the Decree.
“Special Education Support,” “Assessment,” and “Educational Programs” are also addressed and defined in the same section (MoNE, 2000).
In the second section of the second part of the Decree Law, under the heading "Educational Environments," mainstreaming is defined as the education of individuals with special needs, conducted in accordance with individualized education plans that have been prepared. The 1990s were years in which developments in the field of special education and mainstreaming increased significantly. During this period, both the number of personnel and the volume of research and publications grew.
XIII was held in 1990.
At the National Education Council, a decision was made to take measures to enable individuals with special needs to benefit more from non-formal education opportunities (Şura Report, 1999).
In line with the decisions of the Council, the 1st Special Education Council was convened in 1991. Commissions were established within this council for different disability groups, and each commission made decisions regarding its own field. One of the important decisions taken was to emphasize day schooling and to expand mainstreaming programs.
In the 1st Special Education Council, mainstreaming was defined as follows: “The education of students with disabilities who are included in mainstreaming programs is carried out together with their peers at all types and levels of schools and institutions, using appropriate methods and techniques” (MoNE, 2000). In this Decree Law, unlike Law No. 2916, the concepts of mainstreaming, individualized education plan, and special education support were addressed. Although the Decree Law is more comprehensive than Law No. 2916, it does not clearly specify where, how, and by whom the addressed concepts will be implemented.
The Decree bears many similarities to the Education of All Handicapped Children Act, adopted in the United States in 1975. Although mainstreaming practices have been adopted, separate residential/day schools still continue to exist.
According to data from the Ministry of National Education in our country, there are 16 primary schools for students with visual impairments, 47 for those with hearing impairments, and 4 for those with orthopedic impairments; additionally, there are 14 vocational high schools for students with hearing impairments and 2 for those with orthopedic impairments. Moreover, there are 51 vocational training centers for educable children, 77 for trainable children, and 2 for adults with intellectual disabilities.
Accordingly, there are currently 213 separate residential/day special education schools for individuals with disabilities in our country (MoNE, 2004). While the education of many students with disabilities is provided in separate schools, according to information obtained from the Ministry of National Education, the number of students with specific needs included in mainstreaming in 2004 was approximately 49,000.
These figures indicate that although the number of students receiving education in general education classrooms in our country is lower than in other developed countries, there has nonetheless been an increase. Furthermore, considering that there are students with special needs in general education classrooms who have not been identified or formally assessed, it is possible to say that this number is actually even higher.
In the Special Education Services Regulation, which came into effect in 2000, special education services to be provided for individuals with specific needs were detailed, starting from the assessment stage and including placement and evaluation services. Mainstreaming was addressed in a separate section, as the seventh part, and its principles and criteria were outlined. In the regulation, mainstreaming is defined as “special education practices based on the principle that individuals with special needs continue their education together with their non-disabled peers in public and private preschool, primary, secondary, and non-formal education institutions, with the provision of support education services.” The regulation states that special education services should be planned according to the educational needs of individuals rather than their disabilities (MoNE, 2000).
Although the number of students participating in mainstreaming in our country is steadily increasing, the fundamental issue is that no special education support is provided to students with special needs and to teachers in general education classrooms. With such placement, the student with specific needs and the classroom teacher are left to face each other, and the classroom teacher is unable to overcome the problems encountered on their own.
Indeed, findings from research conducted in the field have shown that although teachers support mainstreaming, they require special education support (Batu, 1998; Baykoç-Dönmez, Avcı and Aslan, 1997). Therefore, it would be useful here to specify what mainstreaming support services entail.
In this arrangement, the classroom teacher is primarily responsible for instruction. The special education consultant provides suggestions to the teacher to enhance the success of mainstreaming, and the teacher implements these suggestions (Lewis and Doorlag, 1999). This support service helps improve the teaching knowledge and skills of the classroom teacher. Additionally, it develops the teacher’s ability to independently solve problems should they encounter similar issues in the future (Kırcaali-İftar and Uysal, 1999).
Models of Supportive Education in Mainstreaming Supportive education services within mainstreaming are divided into two categories: in-class and out-of-class support. In-class support education services refer to services provided without removing the student from the classroom environment. In-class supportive education services can be categorized as general education classrooms with consultant assistance, general education classrooms with the assistance of a special education teacher, and general education classrooms with a co-teacher. General Education Classroom with the Assistance of a Special Education Teacher: In this arrangement, the student with special needs continues his or her education in the general education classroom and receives special education support from a teacher. The special education teacher assists the classroom teacher in matters such as facilitating the adaptation of the student with special needs to the classroom, helping the teacher work with the student, carrying out various activities with non-disabled students, providing direct instruction in the classroom, and fostering positive interactions among students (Lewis and Doorlag, 1999). The amount of time the special education teacher spends in the general education classroom may vary depending on the needs of the student. The advantage of this support service is that the student with special needs does not have to leave the classroom, and the special education teacher provides direct support to the classroom teacher during instruction. General Education Classroom with Consultant Assistance: In the consultant-assisted general education classroom model, the student with special needs spends the entire school day in the general education classroom and does not receive direct special education support (Lewis and Doorlag, 1999). In this model, the special education consultant provides guidance to the teacher on adapting instruction, managing problem behaviors, and increasing the student’s acceptance in the classroom. Additionally, the consultant advises the teacher on how to adapt or prepare materials according to the student's needs and how to utilize various resources and materials in the classroom.
General Education Classroom with a Co-Teacher: The general education teacher and the special education teacher work together in the classroom.
They share joint responsibility for the education of students in the classroom, and the special education teacher provides instruction tailored to the needs of students with special needs in the classroom (Lewis and Doorlag, 1999).
This support system is considered advantageous in terms of the continuous collaboration of two teachers in the general education classroom, and it differs from the type of support provided with the assistance of a special education teacher in terms of continuity and shared responsibility.
Hittie, 2003).
In addition, in order for education in the resource room to achieve its objectives, close communication and cooperation between the general classroom teacher and the resource room teacher is essential. When this cooperation cannot be established, inconsistencies may arise between the education provided in the general classroom and that in the resource room. A student who has the opportunity to work more closely with the teacher in the resource room may begin to expect similar closeness in the general education classroom as well.
This leads to the mainstreamed student experiencing difficulties in the general classroom (Kırcaali-İftar, 1998). The types of support services summarized up to this point are services provided to the teacher within the classroom, without removing the student with special needs from the general education classroom. The main difference between these services lies in whether the support is provided to the student or to the teacher, as well as the continuity of the support.
The second type of support service is the one provided outside the classroom, such as support from a resource room or an itinerant special education teacher. An itinerant special education teacher provides support special education services to students by traveling from one school to another.
A student with special needs spends most of the school day in the general education classroom but leaves the class at certain times during the day to receive special education support from an itinerant teacher.
This type of support service is also criticized for the same reasons as the resource room (Lewis and Doorlag, 1999).
In the resource room and itinerant special education teacher models, special education support is provided directly to the student with special needs outside the classroom.
Resource Room or Itinerant Special Education Teacher and Mainstreaming: The resource room is a place where a student with special needs spends at least 21% but no more than 60% of the school day outside the general education classroom to receive special education services (Ysseldyke et al., 2000). In this setting, the student with special needs receives instruction individually or in small groups from a special education teacher, and efforts are generally made to provide instruction that parallels what the student receives in the general education classroom.
Resource room education has been criticized due to concerns that it separates the student with special needs from the general education classroom, prevents the student from participating in certain classes within the general education setting, and leads to the stigmatization of students who receive instruction in the resource room (Peterson and...). When we look at all types of supportive education services for mainstreaming in general, each type of service has its own advantages and limitations.
The type of support service to be implemented should be determined based on the needs of the student and the teacher, as well as the overall structure of the classroom and the school. However, in order for the support service to be beneficial, the provider of the support service and the classroom teacher must work in cooperation and should jointly decide where, for how long, and in which areas the student will be supported. When assisting the teacher, the classroom teacher should be willing to receive this support, believe in the necessity of cooperation, and not feel uncomfortable about having another educator present in the classroom.
In our country, there are no support services available in general education schools to assist classroom teachers with a) identifying students with special needs, b) assessment, c) preparing individualized education programs when necessary, and d) planning and implementing instruction. This situation negatively affects mainstreaming practices. The issue of which supportive education services for mainstreaming should be implemented under which circumstances is also seen as related to the country's conditions.
Although the number of students included in mainstreaming education in our country has increased especially after the enactment of Decree Law No. 573 in 1997, supportive education services for mainstreaming have not been established. While attempts have been made to provide these support services through guidance counselors working in school counseling services, it is not possible for guidance counselors who have not taken a sufficient number of courses in the field of special education and mainstreaming during their university education to provide the expected services. In addition, there is a need for research to determine which support services can be implemented in which schools and for which children across the country.
Although there are not major differences between general and special education in terms of planning and implementing educational programs, many teachers who will work in general education schools graduate with insufficient preparation regarding special education during their professional training. Despite the fact that mainstreaming is accepted both legally and in practice in our country, an examination of teacher training program curricula reveals that courses related to special education and mainstreaming are lacking. This situation causes difficulties in practice, and the knowledge gaps of teachers who have students with special needs in their classrooms are attempted to be addressed through in-service training programs. In order to eliminate these practical difficulties, all teachers should take both theoretical and practical courses during their professional training regarding individuals with special needs and their education within mainstreaming settings. Indeed, studies conducted in the field show that teachers working in general education classrooms feel unprepared for students with special needs and In cases where necessary, it is required to provide support services to the teacher and/or the student with special needs. As previously stated, if the teacher and/or student are not provided with the support services they need, it does not appear possible to benefit from mainstreaming.
However, they have also stated that they have very little time for cooperation (Baker and Zigmond, 1995; Manset and Semmel, 1997; Pivik, McComas and LaFlamme, 2002; Scruggs and Mastropieri, 1996).
What are the principles of successful mainstreaming?
As previously mentioned, mainstreaming does not simply mean placing a student with special needs in a general education classroom. It also requires making certain arrangements/adaptations, fulfilling general principles, and meeting key criteria.
Accordingly, for successful mainstreaming; cooperation emerges as the second most important element for its success.
Teachers are individuals responsible for the education of children. They have duties such as planning and implementing an educational program, as well as ensuring the development and success of their students.
However, in order for teachers to fulfill these responsibilities, they must first possess positive attitudes toward their students (Chubon, 1982; Combs and Harper, 1967). Teachers should first accept students as individuals, provide opportunities for them to express themselves and to demonstrate their creativity, and create environments that will enable and support students’ progress. Every student in the classroom should be made to feel that they are important and valued. In addition, teachers should serve as role models for their students through their own behavior and respect individual differences among students (Horne, 1985). When teachers display an accepting and supportive attitude in the classroom, this fosters a sense of security in children, and only in such an environment will the desire to learn emerge (Brophy, Good, and Nedler, 1979). Furthermore, the acceptance that the teacher demonstrates toward students with disabilities is also seen as necessary for creating a “general atmosphere of acceptance” within the classroom environment (Jamieson, 1986).
1.
First and foremost, all school staff—especially the principal—should demonstrate accepting and supportive attitudes toward students with special needs. From the moment of enrollment, children with special needs and their families interact with the principal, vice principal, guidance counselor, other teachers, clerical staff, and other school personnel. The sense of belonging that the student and their family feel toward the school will be influenced not only by the teacher but also by the behavior of these individuals listed above. In addition, the school principal should assist teachers in acquiring new knowledge, providing necessary materials, and fostering cooperation with relevant individuals both within and outside the school, and should work toward establishing an overall mainstreaming policy at the school.
2.
The attitudes of classroom teachers are crucial for mainstreaming.
General education classrooms should be arranged in a way that meets the needs of all students and facilitates their learning.
The classroom environment should be arranged not only to facilitate the learning of students with typical development, but also to support the learning and increase the participation of students with special needs in the classroom and activities. For this reason, the classroom teacher should possess the necessary knowledge and skills not only in terms of having a positive attitude, but also in adapting the physical environment of the classroom, the curriculum, and teaching methods according to different needs, as well as using effective classroom management techniques. If any adaptation is to be made for a student with special needs, the other students should also be informed about this matter. Adaptations may involve arranging the physical environment (such as seating in the front rows), as well as curriculum modifications and assessment adjustments. By informing the students in advance about such adaptations, the teacher will help facilitate the acceptance of their classmate with special needs.
4.
All students in general education classrooms should have opportunities to learn, play, and participate in educational and social activities together. A learning environment should be created in the classroom that allows each student to progress according to their individual development. Furthermore, the aim should be not only for the students to develop academically but also to foster their social and personal growth (Salend, 2001).
6.
In order for inclusive education to be implemented successfully, both the student with special needs and the teacher in the general education classroom must be provided with supportive special education services tailored to their needs. As previously explained, supportive special education services are divided into two categories: those provided within the classroom and those provided outside the classroom. Resource rooms, itinerant teaching, and special education consultation are among these services.
5.
Other students in the inclusive classroom should be informed about the student with special needs. They should be given information regarding the emotional, behavioral, and cognitive characteristics of the student with special needs. If the student’s disability has also led to a difference in physical appearance, the teacher should explain the reason for this to the other students. If the student with special needs uses a hearing aid or any kind of prosthesis, the function of this assistive device should be explained to the other students. The explanations given to the students should be accurate and appropriate to their developmental level.
Within the classroom, a student with special needs
Cooperation must be established with the parents of all students in the classroom.
In order for inclusive education to provide the expected benefits for students with special needs as well as other students, all parents in the class must cooperate with the classroom teacher. Parents of students with special needs should contribute to their children's development by supporting the skills they acquire at school at home, while other parents should serve as role models for their own children in accepting students with special needs and ensuring peer support in the classroom. The classroom teacher should help to establish and maintain cooperation and positive relationships with parents, and create suitable environments to support this process.
In a classroom where the teacher complains that the student with special needs takes up too much of their time and tires them out, it will be difficult to achieve the desired level of parental solidarity.
The concept of inclusion that has been defined emerged as a result of numerous events that have taken place both around the world and in our country.
Inclusion that meets the basic requirements and principles offers benefits both to students with special needs and to other individuals. Therefore, efforts should focus on studies that examine the extent to which these requirements are met in practice.
In summary, on the condition that support special education services are provided to the classroom teacher and/or the student with disabilities, students with special needs are educated in general education classrooms. SPECIAL EDUCATION JOURNAL TEVHİDE KARGIN REFERENCES SPO (1992).

The Opinions of Teachers about Compulsory Distance Education due to Coronavirus (Covid-19) Fatih BALAMAN Dr. Lecturer
Member, Hatay Mustafa Kemal University, Faculty of Education Asst.

(2021).
Teachers’ Opinions on Compulsory Distance Education Implemented Due to Coronavirus (Covid-19).
Journal of Human and Social Sciences Research, 10 (1), 52-84.
