Fatih BALAMAN & Sevil HANBAY TİRYAKİ Teachers’ Opinions on Mandatory Distance Education Implemented Due to Coronavirus (Covid-19)* This study investigates teachers’ opinions on the mandatory distance education conducted during the COVID-19 process. In this context, the focus is placed on Zoom, the live lesson software actively used during the process, the Education Informatics Network (EBA) platform used for offline education purposes, and EBA TV, which is utilized for one-way communication. The aim is to reveal the effects of these systems on learning, the general impact of distance education on learning, students, and teachers, as well as teachers’ assessments of distance education and the aforementioned systems. The research, in which qualitative data were obtained using an interview form, is designed as a phenomenological model. The study involved 12 teachers—5 women and 7 men—working at 7 different public schools in the city center of Hatay in the 2019-2020 academic year. All interviewed teachers actively participated in the process of mandatory distance education implemented due to COVID-19.
In the study, which employed the Content Analysis method, the data were also analyzed using NVivo 10 software. This software was particularly utilized for coding, grouping codes to form themes, modeling the frequencies of themes and subthemes, identifying frequently occurring words, and conducting queries such as generating word trees and word clouds. As a result of the Content Analysis, themes were identified and the frequency values of the items related to these themes were provided. At the end of the analysis, the following themes were revealed: “Situations encountered specifically during the COVID-19 process,” “Conveniences provided by distance education,” “Disadvantages encountered in distance education,” and “Needs felt throughout the process.” Many findings were obtained within the context of these themes; among the notable ones are the perception that the resources provided by the ministry are adequate, the belief that our education system was prepared for this process, the need for a domestic and secure live lesson software, and teachers’ perception that distance education is not as effective as face-to-face education. Additionally, the problem of assessment and evaluation in distance education, students not being on equal footing in terms of technical infrastructure and resources, the need for in-service training, and students’ attendance in lessons are also among the important findings.
Based on the results, after addressing the identified shortcomings and fulfilling the necessary requirements regarding distance education following COVID-19, face-to-face education and distance education can be blended. In this way, the advantages of both distance and face-to-face education can be utilized.
Keywords: COVID-19, distance education, Ministry of National Education (MEB), EBA, distance education practices * An Ethics Committee Approval was obtained with the decision dated 07.01.2021, No. 13, of the Hatay Mustafa Kemal University Rectorate Social and Human Sciences Scientific Research and Publication Ethics Committee Presidency.
10th Year Journal of the Human and Social Sciences Researches [itobiad] The Opinions of Teachers about Compulsory Distance Education due to Coronavirus (Covid-19) Abstract With this study, the opinions of teachers about compulsory distance education have been investigated during the period of COVID-19.
Keywords: COVID-19, Distance education, Ministry of National Education, EBA, Distance education applications Volume: 10, “Journal of the Human and Social Sciences Researches” Issue: 1, [itobiad / 2147-1185] Fatih BALAMAN & Sevil HANBAY TİRYAKİ Introduction During this period when the entire world is struggling with COVID-19, according to data from the United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization (UNESCO), the closure of educational institutions has affected a total of 1 billion 646 million students in 172 countries throughout the process (UNESCO, 2020). In this period, the distance education system has been used for conducting educational activities, and distance education has attracted more attention in terms of the continuity of education.
On December 31, 2019, a cluster of pneumonia cases attracted attention in Wuhan City, Hubei Province, China, and on January 7, 2020, it was stated that the cause of this situation was a new coronavirus not previously identified in humans, which was named COVID-19.
The first COVID-19 case in Turkey was announced on March 11, 2020 (Ministry of Health, 2020). On the same date, the World Health Organization (WHO) declared this infectious disease a pandemic worldwide (WHO, 2020a). COVID-19 spreads from person to person very quickly and easily.
(Republic of Turkey
(Ministry of Health, 2020). For this reason, methods that minimize contact between people—such as social isolation, the use of personal protective equipment like masks and gloves, and maintaining social distance—have become highly important in preventing transmission (WHO, 2020b).
Close contact between people, high mobility, public transportation, and shared spaces are factors that cause infections to spread rapidly (Hang, 2020). Studies conducted in China, where the initial cases emerged, determined that human movement within communal living areas needed to be reorganized. Within this scope, schools were closed, people were advised to stay at home, and the use of communal spaces was restricted. These measures proved effective in bringing the number of COVID-19 cases under control (Gu, Jiang, Zhao, & Zheng, 2020; Tian et al., 2020; as cited in Deniz & Evci Kiraz, 2020, p. 148). In Turkey as well, numerous measures were implemented to combat COVID-19, one of which was the suspension of classes in schools. Explaining the measures taken, Minister of National Education Ziya Selçuk announced that weekly lesson schedules would be restructured and that required remedial education support would be provided online through the Education Informatics Network (EBA) and on television through the Turkish Radio and Television Corporation (TRT) (Ministry of National Education [MNE], 2020). Accordingly, conducting classes via distance education during the COVID-19 process became one of the measures taken, and the distance education process began for millions of students and teachers.
Distance education, which provides educational opportunities to large groups of individuals with different physical, social, and economic conditions, can be conducted through various means. The continuously advancing technology has also developed and transformed the tools used in distance education.
Starting with printed texts and letters, and extending to programmed texts and textbooks, printed materials have also been delivered through different media as technology has developed.
Distance education can also be conducted using multimedia such as tape recorders, radio, television, films, and presentations, as well as through computer and internet technologies (Alakoç & Bozbıyık, 2003, p. 1195; Hızal, 1983, p. 69; Koçdar, 2006, p. 31; Unusan & Abia, 2007, p. 129). While the use and implementation methods of distance education vary according to available resources, conditions, and needs, in Turkey during the pandemic period, distance education was carried out using television and internet technologies (Ministry of National Education, 2020).
The EBA platform is used effectively, especially for offline education, while EBA TV and the Zoom live lesson software are utilized for online education.
Distance education, in its most general and simplest definition, is a formal educational process in which students and teachers are not physically in the same place (National Center for Education Statistics [NCES], 2008, p. 1). In the distance education process, students and teachers, while in physically separate environments, connect with instructional resources and with each other through interactive communication technologies (Simonson, Smaldino, Albright, and Zvacek, 2012, p. 10).
Conceptually, distance education aims to create new educational opportunities, integrate work and learning, ensure equal opportunity in education, promote lifelong learning, integrate educational technology into the process, provide opportunities for both individual and mass education, and make education effective, efficient, and cost-effective (Alkan, 1998, p. 193; Cavanaugh, 2001, p. 75). Additionally, distance education is an educational method that offers learning opportunities through printed, audiovisual, and electronic materials to learners at times when educational opportunities in formal education institutions are inaccessible or unavailable due to various reasons such as age, illness, geographical distance, family situations, or constraints of time and money (Demiray, 1999, p. 85).
Each teaching method has different characteristics of use, and it cannot be claimed that any one method is always suitable, practical, or successful for all students under all conditions.
When selecting a method, there are variables such as conditions and students that affect the choice of method. While all methods have their own specific advantages and disadvantages, distance education also has many advantages and disadvantages. Especially when compared to face-to-face education, the significant advantages of distance education become evident. Distance education provides access to information for large groups without limitations of time and place. By adding richness and flexibility to educational processes, it allows learners to learn continuously and independently at their own pace. Distance education enables instant and easier access to information through information and communication technologies, reduces educational costs, and offers flexible and objective assessment and evaluation (Elitaş, 2017, p. 94; İşman, 2008, p. 18; Uşun, 2006, p. 19). Of course, as technology is reflected in education and used in conjunction with it, distance education conducted in more advanced forms also brings some disadvantages. Elitaş (2017, p. 95) explained the important disadvantages of distance education as follows: The lack of a group work discipline and the lack of interaction may lead to loss of motivation and cause the learner to become antisocial. It may be inadequate for teaching courses or topics that require practical application. There may be a risk of technology dependence and problems with the reliability of assessment and evaluation.
The process of distance education, which began with correspondence courses and extends to the advanced software and hardware technologies used today, dates back to the 1700s globally and to the 1900s in our country. In particular, the announcement in the Boston Gazette in 1728 that shorthand lessons would be delivered via distance education is recorded as a significant development, and the publication in 1833 of an advertisement stating that learning would be conducted using letters marked another important step in the field of distance education (Çoban, 2013, p. 2). In Turkey, the initial steps of distance education were taken in 1927 when correspondence education was considered a solution during a period when educational institutions faced physical deficiencies and solutions were sought for general educational problems (Arar, 1999, p. 26). In the 1950s, distance education was encouraged in vocational and foreign language training, and with the establishment of TRT in 1968, efforts were made to utilize television technology in distance education. In 1982, the Open Education Faculty of Anadolu University officially began its services, and in the 1990s, web-based distance education initiatives gained momentum in universities (Çukadar & Çelik, 2003, p. 33; Fiş Erümit, Gedik & Göktaş, 2016, p. 666). In recent years, as Kırık (2016, p. 58) has also noted, distance education has become highly developed in Turkey, and during the COVID-19 period, education was conducted remotely through the EBA platform and EBA TV. Subsequently, in the 2020-2021 academic year, the Ministry of National Education allowed the use of free applications such as Zoom to further support distance education.
Importance and Purpose of the Study In order to slow the spread of the COVID-19 pandemic, governments at times implemented various restrictions such as curfews and travel bans, and the closure of educational institutions was also among the measures taken. During this period, the educational process was restructured through the distance education system to ensure the continuity of learning for students and to keep the countries’ educational systems active. In the distance education system, where students are at the center, the role of the teacher in creating suitable learning environments for students has become even more significant, especially during this pandemic, which affected the entire world. Teacher roles in facilitating learning processes and guiding students in distance education, as well as their perspectives on distance education and their experiences teaching through this method, are factors that affect the efficiency of the educational process (Harris & Krousgill, 2008, p. 918; Yurdakul, 2015).
In Turkey, during the pandemic period, distance education was implemented as of 16/03/2020, and many teachers had to conduct the distance education process, with which they were not as familiar as face-to-face education (Ministry of National Education, 2020).
At this point, identifying teachers’ perspectives on distance education and developing recommendations based on the findings are important for conducting the distance education process effectively and efficiently. Additionally, the study group and method of this research, as well as its evaluation of educational activities conducted during the unique global circumstances we are experiencing, are considered original.
Indeed, the literature shows that, just like in the pre-pandemic period, distance education studies conducted during the COVID-19 process have mostly been based on quantitative research and experimental designs, while qualitative or mixed-method studies are less common.
In this study, the interview technique, one of the qualitative data collection methods, was used in order to obtain more in-depth data.
In addition, although studies have been conducted in the literature to determine the views or attitudes of students, teachers, and faculty members toward distance education, it is observed that these studies have mainly focused on students at the undergraduate and higher levels (Aktaş, Büyüktaş, Gülle & Yıldız, 2020, p. 3; Alper & Deryakulu, 2008, p. 53; Çakır Balta & Horzum, 2008, pp. 191-192; Horzum, Özkaya, Demirci & Alpaslan, 2013, p. 85; Karadağ & Yücel, 2020, p. 183; Keskin & Özer Kaya, 2020, p. 59; Pınar & Dönel, 2020, p. 462; Türker & Yaylak, 2011, p. 154).
In this study, research was conducted with teachers, who are the practitioners of the educational process, and it is believed that this will contribute to the literature from this perspective as well.
The aim of this study is to determine teachers’ opinions regarding the compulsory implementation of distance education in Turkey due to COVID-19 and, based on these opinions, to offer suggestions for increasing the effectiveness of the distance education process. The research problem is stated as follows: “What are teachers’ opinions on the compulsory distance education implemented in Turkey due to COVID-19?” The following sub-problems are addressed: 1) What are teachers’ opinions regarding the delivery of courses conducted through distance education and their impact on learning processes? 2) What are teachers’ views on assessment processes in distance education? 3) What are teachers’ perspectives on their self-perceived competence (needs for in-service training) in conducting courses via distance education? 4) Compared to face-to-face education, what are the positive and negative aspects of distance education? 5) What are teachers’ suggestions for the effective and efficient implementation of the distance education process?
Evaluating distance education conducted with the available means during this unique period from the perspectives of teachers may provide a basis for improving, conducting more efficiently, and addressing the deficiencies of distance education activities to be carried out in subsequent periods.
Indeed, in the statement made by the Ministry of National Education (MEB), it was indicated that after the pandemic period, face-to-face education would be conducted alongside distance education and that distance education would not be completely abandoned. This approach was implemented in the 2020-2021 academic year as well. Since teachers participating in distance education during the COVID-19 period are the practitioners of this process, their opinions should be valued to make the educational process more effective, and their observations and suggestions should be taken into consideration. It is believed that the results of this study will be beneficial and provide guidance for decision-makers, the Ministry of National Education, and teachers in the organization and restructuring of distance education processes, and that steps taken accordingly will benefit students as well. Furthermore, it is thought that this study will help address the gap in the literature regarding the inability to obtain sufficient and in-depth information from teachers, who are the practitioners of the educational process during the COVID-19 period. The research findings are also expected to guide other researchers on how to address teachers’ opinions on distance education from different perspectives.
Method Research Model The research employs the phenomenological model. In educational research, explaining a phenomenon, thereby attempting to clarify life experiences related to it and evaluating results and variables, is a common practice. Accordingly, qualitative studies are conducted with small groups to obtain in-depth information about people's life situations (Arthur, Waring, Robert, and Hedges, 2017, pp. 10-11). In phenomenological studies, which are defined as the experience of an event or phenomenon by an individual or individuals, it is known that experiencing the phenomenon is of critical importance (Seggie and Bayyurt, 2017, p. 3). In this study, interviews were conducted with teachers who experienced the distance education that was mandatorily implemented during the COVID-19 period, and the situations, deficiencies, advantages, disadvantages, and reflections on students and teachers that occurred during this process were examined. The main purpose here is not to reach generalizations, but rather to describe and explore the situation in question.
Indeed, qualitative research also necessitates this (Sönmez and Alacapınar, 2013, p. 70).
The research is limited to the academic term in which the interviews were conducted (the period of compulsory distance education), the interview questions, the teachers who participated in the interviews, and the branches of these teachers.
Study Group Table 1: Demographic information of the interviewed teachers Gender Male 7 58.33% Female 5 41.66% 10 the Years “Journal of the Human and Social Sciences Researches” [itobiad] Teacher Opinions on Distance Education Compulsorily Conducted Due to Coronavirus (Covid-19) Branch Information Technologies 2 16.66% Science 2 16.66% English 1 8.33% Mathematics 1 8.33% Music 1 8.33% Art 1 8.33% Special Education 1 8.33% Social Studies 1 8.33% Technology and Design 1 8.33% Turkish 1 8.33% Seniority The study group consists of a total of 12 teachers—5 female and 7 male—working in 10 different fields at 7 different public schools in the city center of Hatay in the 2019-2020 academic year.
The teachers interviewed were selected according to the typical case sampling method, which is a form of purposive sampling. In this method, individuals who possess representative characteristics are included in the study group (Sönmez and Alacapınar, 2013, pp. 97-98). The fact that the interviewees were teachers who actively participated in distance education during this process, and that the study group included teachers from both theoretical and applied branches, was considered as a typical case in forming the study group. Prior to the research, the teachers were informed about the study and the interviews, and participation was voluntary. The inclusion of teachers from different branches and the fact that the course content covered both theoretical and practical aspects were considered beneficial for achieving the aims of the study. The teachers actively served during the aforementioned distance education process.
The necessary ethics committee approval for conducting the research was obtained.
Validity and Reliability Validity and reliability in qualitative studies differ significantly from those in quantitative studies. In qualitative research, explaining the procedures from the beginning to the end of the study, reporting the results in detail, and being objective, flexible, and transparent are considered criteria for validity and reliability (Yıldırım & Şimşek, 2008). Kuş (2008) also states that the use of computer-assisted software in qualitative studies contributes to enhancing validity and reliability by increasing transparency and generalizability. Throughout the research, the procedures and stages were explained in detail, and expert opinions were sought during the development of the data collection tool.
The data were subjected to in-depth analysis using qualitative data analysis software, with frequent inclusion of direct quotations in an effort to ensure validity. For internal reliability, inter-coder agreement was calculated. In this calculation, the reliability coefficient formula for qualitative studies proposed by Miles and Huberman (1994, p. 64) was used (reliability = agreement / (agreement + disagreement) x 100). According to Miles and Huberman (1994, p. 64), a minimum reliability of 70% is recommended. In this study, the interview data in written form were coded independently by the researchers, and the codes were reviewed to identify areas of agreement and disagreement. As a result, the inter-coder agreement was calculated to be 83.75%. For external reliability, it is necessary to describe the characteristics of the participants, state the limitations, and discuss the data collection process and analyses (Yıldırım & Şimşek, 2013). These requirements were explained under appropriate headings in the study, and an effort was made to ensure external reliability. Data Collection and Data Collection Tool The data were collected offline in a virtual environment using the interview method—one of the qualitative data collection methods—through a semi-structured interview form. The interviews were conducted in written form.
The current COVID-19 situation was influential in the lack of face-to-face communication, while the interviewees’ preferences played a role in the communication being offline. Indeed, Seggie and Bayyurt (2017, p. 44) also state that interview data can be collected face-to-face, or obtained in written or visual form via telephone or the internet. Before preparing the interview form, a literature review on the subject was conducted, and preparations were made for the development of the interview questions. Prior to the interviews, teachers were assured that the data would be used solely for scientific purposes, and they were informed that official permission had been obtained from the relevant institution.
Collecting data through the semi-structured interview method is the most commonly preferred data collection method in phenomenological research. The data obtained are transformed into narrative form by adhering to the story of the interviewees.
Interpretation is highly significant in phenomenology, and this method is also referred to as interpretive phenomenological research (Seggie and Bayyurt, 2017, p. 72).
The interview questions were initially formulated as 11 questions.
This form was sent to five faculty members from the Departments of Educational Sciences and Computer Education and Instructional Technology at the Faculty of Education to ensure content validity. The faculty members were asked to review the questions in terms of how well they covered the research topic and met the research requirements. Following the feedback, one question was removed and two questions were merged with others, resulting in a final interview form consisting of eight questions.
The final version of the Interview Form was administered to a teacher as a pilot application. The clarity, explicitness, and comprehensibility of the questions, as well as whether the questions served their intended purpose, were asked, along with any additional opinions and suggestions if present. With simple formal adjustments, the Interview Form was finalized as shown below.
Interview questions: 1- How does delivering a course through distance education affect the learning process? 2- What are your views on the feasibility of delivering the practical components of courses through distance education? 3- How should assessment processes be designed in distance education that has been made compulsory during the COVID-19 period?
4- How do you evaluate yourself in terms of your ability to use distance education during this process (such as using technical features, organizing content, etc.)?
5- Do you think teachers have in-service training needs while managing the distance education process (if so, in which areas)?
6- What are your views on the extent to which attitudes toward distance education can influence distance education activities during this process?
7- Can you compare distance education and traditional education in terms of their advantages and disadvantages?
8- What can be done to make teaching lessons through distance education more functional during this process? In forming the interview questions, the focus was on the Zoom live lesson software—which provides two-way communication and was used in compulsory distance education during the COVID-19 period—the EBA platform, which is used for asynchronous education, and EBA TV, which offers one-way communication. The aim was to identify the effects of these systems on the learning process, as well as to reveal teachers’ observations regarding the effects of distance education and these systems on students, teachers, and learning itself. The data were collected in the final weeks of the second semester of the 2019-2020 academic year.
Data Analysis The content analysis method was used to analyze the data. In content analysis, qualitative data are analyzed in depth and coded; based on these codes, themes are generated, and the items related to these themes are reported with their frequency and percentage values (Sönmez and Alacapınar, 2013, p. 116). During the data analysis, the NVivo 10 software, a qualitative data analysis program, was also utilized. NVivo 10 was specifically employed for coding, generating themes by merging codes, identifying frequently occurring words, constructing models, and conducting other queries.
The interviewed teachers were randomly numbered, and when direct quotations from the interviews were presented in the findings, the teachers were referred to as Tchr.1, Tchr.2, and so on.
As a result of the content analysis, themes were developed and presented in a table along with their frequency values. As also recommended by Yıldırım & Şimşek (2008), direct quotations from the interviewed teachers were frequently included.
Seggie and Bayyurt (2017, pp. 253-254) stated that content analysis is utilized in thematizing texts, identifying words, titles, significant topics, and contents, and quantifying these elements.
Findings As a result of the content analysis, the following themes emerged based on the teachers’ responses: “situations encountered specifically during the COVID-19 period,” “conveniences provided by distance education,” “disadvantages encountered in distance education,” and “needs felt throughout the process.” Each theme and its subthemes were modeled and presented in NVivo along with their frequencies.
Figure 1: Situations encountered during the COVID-19 period In the context of the first theme, teachers emphasized the importance of the process, considered EBA educationally sufficient, found our education system prepared for this sudden transition, and evaluated distance education as a good alternative.
Figure 2: Conveniences provided by distance education In the context of the second theme, it was emphasized that distance education removes the limitations of time and place, allows students to use materials as much as they wish, and highlights the practicality of accessing these materials.
Figure 3: Disadvantages encountered in distance education In terms of disadvantages, teachers reported that distance education is ineffective in psychomotor domain courses, assessment and evaluation, conveying emotions, and interaction. They also stated that not all students have the same opportunities and infrastructure, and that they observed a decline in motivation.
Figure 4: Needs felt throughout the process Teachers stated that their current level of knowledge and skills in using distance education systems was insufficient and, accordingly, there was a need for training; that distance education alone was not sufficient to instill the target behaviors; that there were students in need of computers and internet access; that there were problems accessing servers; and that there was a need for a secure live lesson platform.
Findings Related to the Question “How Does Delivering a Course Through Distance Education Affect the Learning Process?” Volume: 10, “Journal of the Human and Social Sciences Researches” Issue: 1, [itobiad / 2147-1185] Fatih BALAMAN & Sevil HANBAY TİRYAKİ The differentiation of interaction in distance education restricts the expression of emotions. Both the systemic characteristics of distance education and the lack of synergy found in the classroom are among the reasons for this. Learning is a communication process, and expressing emotions is important in this process. Through effective communication, the level of learning and social relationships can be strengthened. One of the components of communication is feedback. Although some level of feedback can be provided in distance education, this feedback is not as effective as in face-to-face environments. The fact that students and teachers do not see each other in the virtual environment leaves the control of learning more to the student and reduces external stimuli related to learning. Therefore, students’ motivation towards learning may decrease.
Regarding the limitation of interaction and feedback in distance education and its reflections on students, teachers used the following statements. Teacher 1: “The difficulties children have in expressing themselves and their emotions, the teacher’s limited ability to observe the children, sense of ownership, inability to recognize individual differences, and the inability to immediately resolve learning difficulties are aspects in which distance education is lacking compared to the traditional approach.” Teacher 1 also stated: “At the same time, even if only occasionally, students being able to connect live with their teachers and classmates will have a positive effect on the educational environment.” Teacher 2: “It is not possible for the teacher to convey emotions to students through gestures and facial expressions.”
This situation is negative in terms of learning." Teacher 4: "In practical courses, distance education does not provide much benefit because feedback cannot be given instantly or as needed." Teacher 4: "In distance education, students are in a teaching process where they are far from discipline and unable to fully develop their social and emotional skills." Teacher 5: "Being separated from their peers may lead students to be weaker in social relationships." Teacher 5: "They will be removed from the classroom environment, making it difficult for the teacher to control and motivate the student for the lesson." Teacher 10: "Face-to-face education allows for life-saving moments such as making eye contact with the student, having short or long dialogs during the process, jokes, and students being able to express themselves—unfortunately, these are not possible with distance education." Teacher 10: "The emotional sharing, eye contact, peer learning, spending time with friends at school, the opportunity to chat with teachers, and learning by doing that face-to-face education provides unfortunately cannot be achieved to the desired extent." Teacher 11: "The level of readiness is important for learning new topics."
In the classroom, students’ morale is boosted and they become motivated because they can see and talk to their friends. A disparity of opportunity may arise between students who can obtain computers and other technological devices with computer features and those who cannot.
Considering rural areas in our country where income levels are low and internet service has not yet reached, it is a fact that this situation cannot be ignored.
Indeed, computers and the Internet are indispensable technologies for distance education. Teachers' comments regarding students' need for computer and internet access are as follows. Teacher 1: “Insufficient technical infrastructure, students not having access to the same technology, or differing levels of proficiency in effectively using distance education tools are shortcomings of distance education compared to the traditional method.” Teacher 2: “… In addition to these views, students' adequacy in accessing technology is the most important factor.” Teacher 5: “In general, technical infrastructure has been made accessible in almost every part of the country.
However, there are quite a significant number of students from families with poor financial means and from villages without internet access who are unable to receive this education.
This situation causes inequality of opportunity in education." Teacher 7: "Distance education may fall short because it cannot provide equal opportunities among students. There are many students who do not have an internet connection, tablet, television, or computer at home." Teacher 8: "The fact that internet connection speed can vary from region to region and insufficient infrastructure may be problematic." Teacher 8: "I think the required internet connection fee for distance education may not be affordable for every family's budget." Teacher 10: "I believe activities should be monitored by parents."
Of course, not every household may have internet, a computer, or a tablet.
Another factor is that not every student is able to use the EBA.
"It is important to establish the necessary infrastructure for this." Findings Regarding the Question "What Are Your Opinions on the Feasibility of Delivering the Practical Components of Courses Through Distance Education?" It has been understood that in courses with a heavy emphasis on practical content, such as Science, Music, Physical Education, and Theater, as well as in artistic activities, instruction through distance education may be inadequate.
Although some software partially enables practical applications, these programs are not as effective as face-to-face education.
Teachers have made the following statements regarding the use of distance education in practical courses.
Tchr.2: "The learning process varies depending on the course content."
The inclusion of verbal topics in the course content does not negatively affect the learning process in distance education, but practical course content involving demonstration and hands-on activities is negatively affected. Tchr.3: "It is very difficult to conduct experiments and observations in Science class through distance education."
This situation should be supported with presentations or videos.” Volume: 10, “Journal of the Human and Social Sciences Researches” Issue: 1, [itobiad / 2147-1185] Fatih BALAMAN & Sevil HANBAY TİRYAKİ Tchr.4: “In courses like science, which is my subject and requires practical application, even if the practical part is sometimes carried out with software, it does not reflect reality very well.” Tchr.7: “I believe that practical courses, for example, my own subject of music, will be lacking if delivered through distance education.”
By watching films about art and reviewing the assigned works, a contribution can be made to our course.
However, it is not possible to teach instrument lessons through distance education.
Only instrument repetition can be done." Tchr.10: "Unfortunately, practical courses or the practical components of our course cannot be fully delivered through distance education."
Techniques such as demonstration and observing practical steps can be used only up to a certain point.” Findings Regarding the Question “How Should Assessment Processes Be Designed in Compulsory Distance Education During the COVID-19 Period?” Today’s education system argues that, rather than relying on a single or a few measurements, assessment and evaluation systems should employ methods that continue throughout the process and encompass the entire period, rather than being instantaneous, as these would be more appropriate.
In the interviews, there were teachers who emphasized that the process was more important than the outcome regarding this situation, which highlights the significance of the process.
In distance education, traditional methods of assessment and evaluation have drawbacks in terms of the possibility that students may use resources other than their own knowledge and skills. However, by employing alternative methods, efforts are made to conduct realistic assessments and evaluations.
Among the teachers interviewed, there were those who believed that exams should be conducted centrally, as well as a considerable number of teachers who favored holding exams in virtual environments rather than face-to-face. Additionally, there were also teachers who thought that exams should not be administered at all.
Teacher statements regarding the assessment and evaluation process are as follows.
Teacher 1: “I believe that it cannot be implemented as effectively as in a school environment. However, activities in the teacher’s session should be presented to students in a more flexible way, supported by various visuals, videos, and interactive materials, and then evaluated.” Teacher 2: “There should not be any assessment or evaluation system due to the uncertainty of all students’ access to TV- and internet-based education.”
Exams should not be administered.” Teacher 3: “Students are passive, practical application is very limited, opportunities to ask questions and receive feedback are insufficient, and assessment in the virtual environment is not very reliable.”
The process is important.
In order to reach a conclusion, the process must be evaluated properly." Teacher 3: "Exams should not be conducted online; they should be administered practically."
8.
They should be administered by the Ministry of National Education for classroom teachers, and by subject teachers for grades 5, 6, and 7.” Tchr.5: “It would be a fairer examination system for the Ministry of National Education to administer a central exam to students across Turkey.”
Exams should again be conducted online through e-examinations." 10 the Years "Journal of the Human and Social Sciences Researches" [itobiad] Teachers' Opinions on Distance Education Compulsorily Implemented Due to Coronavirus (Covid-19) Teacher 6: "Exams can be administered centrally through EBA. A specific time can be set, and students can log in at that time to answer the questions."
However, how reliable this would be is debatable because there is no guarantee that students will answer the questions on their own.
Therefore, the entire process should be evaluated." Teacher 7: "Education is a process.
In conclusion, I do not think the evaluation is correct.” Teacher 8: “Exams can be prepared in a timed format with open-ended reasoning questions, similar to university distance education exams. For each course, they can be prepared by a central exam committee and completed within set time frames.
I believe that both the evaluation process and its outcome should be important." Teacher 9: "Exams can still be conducted via distance education. The course instructor should administer the exam.
I think that during the remote education and instruction process, the educational and instructional process itself is more effective than the exams.” Teacher 10: “For example, students should complete the steps they need to take throughout the process.” Teacher 10: “Exams can be administered by spreading them out over the process.”
By imposing time limits on seminars or courses taken through remote education, individuals can reflect what they have learned.
Commissions can be established in the provinces.
“In this commission, which works in coordination with the national education department, qualified teachers can prepare the exams.” Teacher 11: “For years, because we have worked in a results-oriented manner, wasn’t our child who put in the most effort and worked the hardest considered unsuccessful just because some water was spilled on the optical answer sheet?”
“No matter the outcome, what matters is making an effort.” “In this process, how do you rate your ability to use distance education (such as using technical features, organizing content, etc.)?”
Findings Regarding the Question "How Do You Evaluate Yourself?" There were teachers who felt competent in the use of various software and hardware within the scope of distance education, as well as those who felt inadequate and believed they could be better. I think I have the necessary technological knowledge and the ability to solve problems if I encounter any.” Tchr.6: “I consider myself inadequate in using the technical infrastructure of distance education.

“I have sufficient technical knowledge on this subject due to my interest.” Tchr.11: “…Teachers are not sufficiently competent in using web 2 tools.” Findings Related to the Question: “Do You Think Teachers Need In-Service Training While Managing the Distance Education Process (if so, on which subjects)?” The use of systems such as Zoom, EBA TV, and the offline EBA platform by students and especially teachers requires a certain level of computer and internet literacy. If we include content preparation in this usage, the scope will become even broader. Interviews revealed that some struggled with using the systems and that most considered in-service training necessary. While there was general consensus on the need for in-service training, it was also found that the Ministry of National Education actually has educational videos on this topic for system usage. Teacher statements on this subject were as follows. Tchr.1: “I definitely think there is a need for in-service training. First of all, every educator should primarily receive training in basic computer usage and auxiliary programs, Office programs, lesson and content development, video and audio editing, and Web 2.0 tools.” Tchr.3: “If there are going to be new applications (involving technical knowledge), in-service training can be provided.”
Training can be provided on designing experiments in digital environments for science and having students conduct them.” Tchr.5: “Teachers and school administrators definitely need to receive in-service training.
This training is not only a course on teaching via video conference, but they also need to learn programs for preparing materials for activities and conducting these activities online.” Teacher 6: “If a process evaluation is to be carried out, training can be provided on how this should be done.” Teacher 7: “There should be training on the flow of the process and how it will be evaluated.” Teacher 8: “In-service training could have been useful; however, it seems that the Ministry of National Education has taken measures for this during the quarantine period. Topics such as distance education, content creation on EBA, computer usage, and self-improvement in one's field... Findings Regarding the Question: “What Are Your Opinions on the Extent to Which Attitudes Toward Distance Education Can Affect Distance Education Activities During This Process?” 10th Years Journal of the Human and Social Sciences Researches [itobiad] Teachers’ Opinions About Distance Education Mandatorily Conducted Due to the Coronavirus (Covid-19) Pandemic Teachers believed that attitude is effective in learning processes. It was understood that the teaching method during the COVID-19 period has changed students’ attitudes toward distance education either positively or negatively.
Teachers’ opinions on this matter are as follows. Teacher 3 stated, “Since it is understood that education has not been neglected during this process, I think this affects all stakeholders’ attitudes toward distance education positively.” Teacher 7 said, “The attitude towards distance education influences students’ willingness to participate in lessons and their adaptation to the instruction provided.” Teacher 12 remarked, “I observed that some of our students, whom we would describe as ‘good’ in face-to-face education, developed negative opinions about distance education. I also received feedback from some parents in this regard.”
Attitudes affect the process.” Teacher 4: “I believe I do my job professionally and I trust my experience.”
Therefore, regardless of my perspective on distance education, my distance education activities are not affected by this.
This situation may vary depending on other teachers’ personality traits, their approach to the profession, their professional burnout, and whether or not they possess a scientific way of thinking.
“For me, this is the situation.” Findings Regarding the Question: “Can You Compare Distance Education and Traditional Education in Terms of Advantages and Disadvantages?” Teachers generally mentioned both the advantages and disadvantages of distance education when compared to face-to-face teaching. However, during the COVID-19 period, when face-to-face education could not be conducted, the next best alternative that could most closely approximate what could be done in a face-to-face environment and ensure the continuity of teaching was, again, distance education. In this respect, although it does not fully replace face-to-face education, teachers stated that the best alternative or solution to face-to-face instruction could be distance education. One of the most significant advantages of distance education, namely independence from time and place, was also felt during this period. In addition, teachers especially emphasized the opportunity in distance education for students to rewatch offline lessons as many times as they wish, particularly highlighting the pause–resume feature of videos.
Students with different characteristics and backgrounds may require a different number of repetitions to learn. While topic repetition is limited in the classroom, it is unlimited with distance education.
Enriching educational materials with features that appeal to multiple senses has increased their usability and ensured better understanding of the subject. At the same time, advanced computer and internet infrastructure and software have enabled quick and easy access to these materials. In terms of advantages and disadvantages, teachers have made the following remarks.
Tchr.1: “Interaction between teacher and student is facilitated through various materials.” Tchr.2: “With the pause-and-continue option in lesson content, students can learn more efficiently.” Tchr.5: “For students and parents who are aware of their responsibilities, this is the most beneficial educational tool in such a difficult time. It is the best form of education and instruction when face-to-face education cannot be conducted.”
Remote education is beneficial in this respect, as it helps maintain student motivation and prevents them from losing interest in school.” Tchr.5: “Through this form of education, students are provided with the opportunity to access all kinds of information.” Tchr.6: “Students can manage the process by pausing educational videos, allowing them to work at their own pace.” Tchr.7: “The advantage of remote education is that it makes a significant contribution to ensuring that education is not disrupted during periods when schools are closed (such as during an epidemic).” Tchr.8: “The abundance of options for finding and researching content provides rich material support.” Tchr.10: “Since we are going through a mandatory process, remote education seemed like a kind of lifesaver.” Tchr.10: “... Now we need to put these aside.”
We should see remote education as an important option in order to minimize the damage during this process.” Tchr.10: “It allows for independence from time and place.” Tchr.10: “Students can improve themselves by watching their lessons repeatedly during the day.” Tchr.11: “The advantage of remote education is that, in such times, it prevents students from being completely disconnected from the school environment.” Findings Regarding the Question “What Can Be Done to Make Teaching More Functional Through Remote Education During This Process?” In contemporary educational approaches, remote education holds a significant place.
The combination of remote education, which is a special type of Blended Learning, and face-to-face education aims to bring together the advantages of both methods. The positive aspects of remote education are utilized without abandoning face-to-face education. Providing a fast and continuous remote education service requires a robust technical infrastructure, which also depends on the number and intensity of users. One of the most important elements for this is the servers. During the COVID-19 period, there were teachers who mentioned shortcomings originating from servers. After compulsory remote education was implemented during the COVID-19 period, teachers voluntarily used the live lesson software called Zoom with its free version. There was a significant increase in the use of this software not only in our country but also around the world.
However, as announced by the Ministry of National Education, the public and teachers were informed about the software’s security vulnerabilities.
An investigation has been launched by the United States government into the software company headquartered in the United States (US).
In order to make the process more functional, the teachers' views are as follows.
Teacher 1: “It is a fact that an educational transformation will take place. In future educational planning, it is inevitable that opportunities, activities, and interaction environments related to distance education will be integrated in parallel with formal education.”
“It would be beneficial to implement them together.” Teacher 1: “Considering that there are millions of students in the system, efforts should be made to strengthen the servers.” Teacher 2: “Although there are various shortcomings and problems in the compulsory distance education, it has been seen that distance education can be carried out.”
However, I believe that without a certain amount of face-to-face education, the effectiveness of distance education will decrease.” Teacher 2: “With the improvement of internet and server infrastructure, a more efficient process can be managed.” Teacher 6: “I observe that EBA is very slow due to the heavy traffic, despite the precautions taken.” Teacher 11: “Domestic software should be used.”
“As long as domestic software is not used, we remain constantly vulnerable to attacks.” Tchr.1: “It is inevitable that addressing the security vulnerabilities of the programs and applications to be used is also among the fundamental requirements in this process.” During the compulsory distance education process imposed by COVID-19, the ministry provided the EBA platform for offline education and EBA TV for online education, in order to bring teachers and students together and to conduct educational activities.
In addition to teachers who considered these systems sufficient for education and teaching, there were also teachers who claimed that there were shortcomings.

Student 2: "EBA is especially sufficient for 11th and 12th grades."
4.
The lesson content in key subjects is sufficient for the range from grade to 10th grade. If supported by assessment and evaluation tools, it can address all student groups.
Preschool, 1st and 2nd.
There are shortcomings at the grade level.” Student 4: “I think EBA is adequate both in terms of course content and technical features, such as being able to send assignments to students, track them, and, recently, conduct live lessons.” Student 5: “The content and scope have been prepared in accordance with the annual curriculum, in a simple and comprehensible manner.”
Its aspects that support students, do not alienate them from the lessons, and are motivating are its positive sides.” Teacher 8: “Given the thousands of materials and existing potential that EBA offers, I believe it is sufficient to facilitate Distance Education.” Volume: 10, “Journal of the Human and Social Sciences Researches” Issue: 1, [itobiad / 2147-1185] Fatih BALAMAN & Sevil HANBAY TİRYAKİ It was unforeseeable that the unexpectedly developing COVID-19 process would affect human life and daily activities to this extent. Many activities in human life either stopped entirely or were carried on through alternative methods in safer ways. The education–teaching activity, which holds vital importance in our lives, was also continued through distance education since in-person instruction was no longer possible. It was a significant advantage that, prior to this process, our Ministry had already undertaken important work related to distance education and had made practices actively available for use by students and teachers. The transition from face-to-face education to distance learning in teaching brought about many adjustments for both teachers and students. It is understood that the Ministry, making considerable efforts to ensure efficiency in education and teaching, implemented important practices during this period. On this subject, teachers shared the following views.
Teacher 1: “The rapid delivery of EBA login passwords, time coordination, the reassuring and encouraging effects of public statements made according to the prevailing situation on students and parents, and the prompt launch of TV channels are the results of the efforts made by our ministry.” Teacher 3: “Of course, since it was an unprepared situation, it is not sufficient.”
Improvement efforts are underway.
The scope of implementation can be expanded.” Teacher 4: “The fact that the Ministry was able to make decisions quickly and carry out the necessary preparations in a short time shows, in my opinion, that it was sufficiently prepared.” Teacher 6: “I think the Ministry of National Education managed the process well in a short period of time.”
Although it may not be functional, it is promising that efforts are being made.
As for us teachers, I think we were caught completely unprepared.” Teacher 8: “If we consider that both the Ministry of National Education and the teachers contributed to this process with only a one-week delay and that uninterrupted education was able to continue, I think the process was managed successfully.” Teacher 10: “In a short period, lessons started not only through EBA but also via television.”
A plan and program were prepared, and assistance was requested from both families and teachers to ensure our children's adaptation to the process.
It is actually a comprehensive struggle.
Is it enough then? No, it isn’t! Can we do better? Of course we can! I believe that efforts are being made for better each day, learning lessons from every day.
Both the Ministry of National Education and we as teachers will continue to do our best for our students.” Some teachers expressing that they were caught unprepared suggests that the EBA platform, which existed prior to COVID-19, was not actively used by some teachers. Teacher 4 stated, “The situation developed suddenly, but initially, not much time was given to teachers.”
I think that especially parents have developed very high expectations of teachers, but nevertheless, teachers have also managed the process well.” Teacher 6: “It is promising that efforts are being made by the Ministry.

The Ministry of National Education made great efforts.
Everything happened very suddenly, but everyone managed to adapt in a short time.” Word Cloud and Word Tree Generated with NVivo 10 A word cloud can be generated with NVivo to identify the most frequently mentioned words and to extract the concepts that are heavily emphasized. It is known that as the font size increases in word clouds, the frequency of the mentioned word in the interview data also rises. In this way, prominent concepts and highlighted expressions can be visualized.
Figure 5: Word Cloud According to the word cloud obtained in the study, words such as distance education, course, students, and EBA were frequently mentioned in the interviews.
With the extraction of preliminary statements related to qualitative data from the word cloud, in order to obtain information such as the locations of these statements within written texts and their sequences with other concepts, and to examine their relationships, a word tree was created. It was found that the most frequently occurring concept was "distance education." According to the word tree generated around this concept, word groups commonly used together with distance education were visualized. To enhance the readability of the visual, the word tree was presented in two sections. Figure 2: Distance Education Word Tree -1 Some of the findings obtained from this section of the word tree include the perception of distance education as a savior, the responsibilities assigned to families during this process, the limited activeness of students in distance education, and the Ministry's rapid action during the virus period.
Figure 3: Distance Education Word Tree -2 10 the Years “Journal of the Human and Social Sciences Researches” [itobiad] Teachers’ Opinions on Compulsory Distance Education Implemented Due to the Coronavirus (Covid-19) Some of the findings obtained from the word tree for this section are that distance education was considered the best alternative during this compulsory period, the method was insufficient for practice-based courses such as music, teachers received in-service training to use distance education, and the adequacy of the infrastructure for distance education.
Discussion and Conclusion The compulsory conduct of courses in digital environments has had various repercussions from different perspectives. Although the dominant opinion among teachers is that distance education cannot replace face-to-face education conducted in classroom settings, it is acknowledged that in this extraordinary period where in-person education is not possible, the best solution remains distance education. Sindiani (2020) also stated in his study that distance education was the best option during this process.
While all stakeholders were affected by the process in different ways, studies have shown that during the COVID-19 period, when students were separated from face-to-face education, they experienced significant levels of depression and feelings of loneliness (Lee, Ward, Chang, and Downing, 2020), as well as decreased levels of happiness and physical activity (Munasinghe et al., 2020). For this reason, in order to increase the usability and effectiveness of distance education, students’ motivation and satisfaction levels should be maximized, and their needs should be met as much as possible.
Indeed, Patricia (2020) states that motivation, self-efficacy, and the use of technology play a significant role in students' academic performance.
Teachers perceive distance education not as an adequate and complete instructional method, but rather as a tool for various reasons such as preventing students from falling behind in their lessons, keeping them engaged with the educational environment, and avoiding a holiday mindset.
Kürtüncü and Kurt (2020, p. 71) also found that students were dissatisfied with not being able to conduct instructional activities face-to-face.
Patricia (2020) and Sindiani (2020) also stated in their studies that during the COVID-19 period, students preferred face-to-face education over distance education.
In contrast, Qazi et al.
In contrast, (2020) reported that students who were able to participate in remote education expressed satisfaction with the method.
In order to reach all students through remote education and ensure that all stakeholders benefit, it is necessary to provide adequate technical infrastructure even in rural areas and to make accessibility possible in terms of both software and hardware.
Morgan (2020) and Carrillo and Flores (2020) also found that students without a computer or internet access at home experienced a decline compared to face-to-face education.
The provision of internet services and the socio-economic status of families play a role in access to these resources. Differences in access among students lead to unequal opportunities (Bennett, Uink, and Cross, 2020). Indeed, it has been determined that the number of students in our country who were unable to benefit from distance education services during the pandemic was at significant levels, and that this inequality of opportunity reduced the quality of education (Yılmaz, Güner, Mutlu, Doğanay, and Yılmaz, 2020, p. 25).
In contrast to these findings, Kang and Zhang (2020) found that online education during this period increased learning levels and motivation.
It has been found that teachers' approaches to distance education vary according to their subject area.
While teachers of theoretically oriented courses generally find the method sufficient in instruction, teachers of practical courses may find it inadequate.
However, according to the study by Koçoğlu, Ulu Kalın, Tekdal, and Yiğen (2020, p. 2961), teachers found the remote education activities conducted to be sufficient regardless of the subject or educational level.
There have been those who argue that exams should be conducted remotely, those who argue that they should be held face-to-face, and those who argue that there should be no exams at all.
However, while traditional face-to-face education tends to emphasize outcomes, during distance education, the activities carried out throughout the process have been highlighted as more important for learning, thus emphasizing the significance of the process itself. It has also been understood that students do not find remote exams reliable or realistic; instead, it has been suggested that assignments to be given throughout the process would be more appropriate than online exams (Kürtüncü & Kurt, 2020, p. 66).
Teachers occasionally used the Zoom software for online education; however, following the Ministry’s recommendation due to various security vulnerabilities of Zoom, they stopped using it. Most of those who had used it continued their teaching activities only through the EBA platform and EBA TV.
This situation indicates the need for a reliable live lesson software.
Television-based lessons may not meet the needs for online classes. As is well known, live lesson software enables two-way communication and allows for significantly greater interaction.
It has been recognized that there is a need for in-service training regarding the use of distance education systems such as EBA platforms and the teaching of practical lessons through distance education.
Kırmızıgül (2020, p. 288) also emphasized the need for in-service training for teachers in his study on this particular process.
Not all teachers took an active role in the very large, student-oriented organization that included approximately 18 million students in primary, secondary, and high schools (Can, 2020, p. 43).
Indeed, in distance education, a teacher delivering online lessons to a large number of students does not significantly impact the quality of instruction as it does in face-to-face education. Therefore, in-service training can be provided to teachers gradually, and those who have received such training can be assigned to distance education. It is important that teachers who will be involved in distance education are competent in this field and in areas such as the use of information technologies (Can, 2020, p. 30). The proficiency of teachers in using technologies for distance teaching, students’ access to information tools, and teachers’ in-service training are among the most important factors for the success of the distance education process (König, Jäger-Biela, and Glutsch, 2020).
According to Nuland, Mandzuk, Petrick, and Cooper (2020), teacher training is necessary and important for the use of distance education software and for managing the process successfully.
While the monitoring of educational tasks assigned to students can be effectively carried out thanks to the strong interaction of face-to-face education, in distance education, the completion of these tasks and extracurricular activities by students is open to abuse. It is difficult to conduct and monitor these activities effectively. Cooperation with parents is of great importance for the effective execution and achievement of these activities, and indeed the entire distance education process.
At this point, the importance of discipline in education and instruction comes to the forefront.
Teachers have mentioned various shortcomings regarding the use of distance education in its different aspects.
Tang, Abuhmaid, Olaimat, Oudat, Aldhaeebi, and Bamanger (2020) also reported that the use of distance education alone was generally not satisfactory in terms of class participation, assessment and evaluation, and students’ learning levels.
Distance education is necessary and important to be used not only in special situations such as during a pandemic, but at every stage of the educational process. Indeed, while face-to-face education continues, students, teachers, and parents—either intentionally or unintentionally—make use of distance education, whether through social media platforms or by using other tools.
Even outside the pandemic process, although distance education is used effectively, the continued reliance on face-to-face education has brought blended learning—the combination of these two methods—to the forefront. The Ministry of National Education announced that following the return to face-to-face education after COVID-19, distance education would also continue, and that face-to-face and distance education would be integrated and used together. This decision indicates a desire to take advantage of the benefits offered by distance education. This significant development shows that the situations identified during distance education will remain relevant in subsequent periods of the educational process.
Yılmaz, Güner, Mutlu, Doğanay, and Yılmaz (2020, p. 39) also concluded in their study that rather than using distance education exclusively for teaching and learning, it would be more appropriate to use it as a supplementary tool. Based on the research findings, the following recommendations were developed.
Internet infrastructure can be developed to provide internet service to rural areas as much as possible.
- Student participation can be increased by providing more students with access to computers and the internet. - To obtain more realistic results, the assessment and evaluation process can be extended over time. - Online in-service training seminars can be organized on the use of systems employed in distance education. - The effectiveness of distance education can be enhanced by supporting EBA materials with interactive materials and videos to be prepared for applied courses.
Measures to increase internet bandwidth can be taken to enable faster use of online and offline environments. In order to provide a safer distance education process, domestic and secure live lesson software can be developed.
