Turkish Adaptation of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form: A Validity and Reliability Study.
Abant İzzet Baysal University Journal of Faculty of Education, 18 (4), 2502-2517.
Date of Submission: 09/08/2018 Date of Acceptance: 14/11/2018 TURKISH ADAPTATION OF THE SELF-COMPASSION SCALE SHORT FORM: VALIDITY AND RELIABILITY STUDY* Musa YILDIRIM** Tuğba SARI*** ABSTRACT The aim of this study was to examine the psychometric properties of the Turkish version of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form among adolescents. For this purpose, data were collected from 800 adolescents—400 middle school students and 400 high school students. In order to examine the psychometric properties of the scale, construct validity, criterion-related validity, internal consistency coefficient, and test-retest reliability were addressed. According to the findings, item 10 in the original form was removed from the scale due to its factor loading being below .30. The results of Exploratory Factor Analysis indicated that the scale demonstrated a single-factor structure, and this factor consisted of two subcomponents. Confirmatory Factor Analysis also confirmed the factor structure of the scale. In addition, a moderate positive correlation was found between adolescents’ self-compassion scores and subjective well-being scores, while a moderate negative correlation was found between self-compassion scores and fear of self-compassion scores. The internal consistency coefficient of the scale was calculated as .75. Finally, a high level of correlation was observed between the test-retest measurements administered with a 17-day interval.
In conclusion, the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form, consisting of 11 items and a single dimension, can be considered a valid and reliable instrument for measuring the self-compassion levels of Turkish adolescents.
Keywords: Self-Compassion Scale Short Form, Turkish adaptation, middle school students, high school students ADAPTATION OF THE SHORT FORM OF SELF-COMPASSION SCALE INTO TURKISH: A VALIDITY AND RELIABILITY STUDY ABSTRACT In this study, it was aimed to examine the psychometric properties of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form in Turkish adolescents.
Keywords: Self-Compassion Scale Short Form, adaptation study, high school students * This study is derived from a part of the thesis completed by the first author under the supervision of the second author at the Institute of Educational Sciences, Bolu Abant Izzet Baysal University.
** Ministry of National Education, musayildirim78@gmail.com *** Bolu Abant Izzet Baysal University, Faculty of Education, Department of Educational Sciences, Guidance and Psychological Counseling Division, saritugba75@gmail.com Musa YILDIRIM, Tuğba SARI 1. INTRODUCTION The word self-compassion has been translated into Turkish from the Latin-rooted word “Compassion.”
Compassion derives from the combination of the words com (together) + pati (to suffer) (Strauss et al., 2016), and is defined as the ability to respond to the suffering of others in a sensitive, patient, compassionate, and empathetic manner, as well as to recognize that such suffering is an experience that anyone can have (Condon and Feldman Barrett, 2013). Self-compassion, on the other hand, refers to directing one's compassionate attitudes and behaviors toward oneself.
Neff (2003a) defines self-compassion as being warm and kind to oneself during difficult times when experiencing pain, distress, failure, and inadequacy, recognizing and accepting one's negative emotions, and understanding that all kinds of challenging experiences are a part of being human.
Self-compassion is one of the fundamental concepts of Buddhist teaching, which seeks ways to live wisely and is based on love, compassion, empathy, and composure/equanimity in doing so (Wallace and Shapiro, 2006).
According to Buddhist philosophy, suffering, failures, and difficult experiences are part of human life; almost everyone, by virtue of being human, endures painful experiences, and this is inevitable (Rubin, 2013). The important thing is to be able to cope appropriately with painful and challenging experiences. Living wisely is only possible by coping effectively with suffering and challenging situations (Thurman, 2006). Self-compassion is also considered one of the healthy ways to cope with suffering and difficult experiences (Neff, 2003a). Self-compassion is a construct consisting of three components, each with both a positive and negative dimension. The first of these is self-kindness versus self-judgment. Self-kindness versus self-judgment is concerned with the response one gives to emotional suffering and challenging situations (Neff et al., in press). This subdimension describes the attitude a person has toward themselves in the face of suffering and difficulties as a position between self-kindness and self-judgment. Self-kindness means adopting an attitude toward oneself that is gentle, kind, and understanding in the face of suffering, failures, and inadequacies, as well as being able to provide oneself with the necessary care and attention (Neff, 2003b). Conversely, self-judgment can be seen as taking a critical, punitive, and self-deprecating attitude toward oneself in response to suffering, failures, and inadequacies. The second component is common humanity versus isolation. This sub-factor relates to the awareness that painful and challenging experiences are part of being human. It refers to cognitive processing about a painful or challenging experience (Neff et al., in press). Being aware of common humanity means recognizing that difficult and painful experiences are, in fact, experienced by many other people as well, and that experiencing pain is a natural part of being human. A person who is aware of common humanity takes the attitude, “The challenging experience I am going through relates to my humanity; this could happen to most people, and nearly everyone can encounter difficult experiences in their life” (Neff, 2003a). On the other hand, isolation is when a person sees their challenges, suffering, failures, and inadequacies as experiences unique only to themselves. The final subcomponent is mindfulness versus over-identification. Mindfulness versus over-identification involves seeing and understanding one's suffering and failures, and witnessing the resulting negative emotions without judgment (Neff et al., in press). Mindfulness means allowing emotions and experiences to be present without labeling them as good-bad or beautiful-ugly, staying present in the moment, directing one's attention to the present, and maintaining balance in thoughts and emotions (Brown and Ryan, 2003; Neff, 2003a). In contrast, over-identification refers to defining oneself by one’s emotions (e.g., “I am a hopeless person”), excessively identifying with situations, and believing that difficult and painful experiences will persist forever rather than pass. As a result, self-compassion has been defined as a construct composed of three factors, each comprising a positive (self-kindness, common humanity, and mindfulness) and a negative (self-judgment, isolation, and over-identification) structure (Costa et al., 2016). Thus, self-compassion can be considered as a whole consisting of six subdimensions (Neff, 2003b); these six subdimensions can also be examined under two themes, positive and negative (Bengtsson, Söderström, and Terjestam, 2016; Costa et al., 2016). This is because the subdimensions of self-kindness, common humanity, and mindfulness reflect a positive, self-compassionate attitude, while self-judgment, isolation, and over-identification reflect a negative, self-critical attitude (Costa et al., 2016).
Studies conducted abroad have reported a positive relationship between self-compassion and life satisfaction (Bluth and Blanton, 2012), happiness, optimism (Neff, Rude, and Kirkpatrick, 2007), need satisfaction, self-esteem, self-acceptance (Neff, 2003b), and psychological resilience (Neff and McGehee, 2009).
Similarly, studies conducted in our country have shown that self-compassion is positively associated with concepts that predict positive mental health, such as life satisfaction (Tel and Sarı, 2016), self-enhancing humor and communication skills (Akyol, 2011), social support (Akkaya, 2011), mindfulness and resilience (Aydın Sünbül, 2016), as well as positive perceptions of mother, father, and self (Andiç, 2013).
On the other hand, studies conducted in our country have found a negative relationship between self-compassion and depression, anxiety, worry, anger, stress (Andıç, 2013; Temel, 2015), problematic internet use (Yiğit, 2015), difficulties in emotion regulation (Aydın Sünbül, 2016), loneliness (Akın, 2010b), and cognitive distortions related to relationships (Akın, 2010a).
Similarly, studies conducted abroad have also found that self-compassion is negatively associated with depression (Reas, 2010), anxiety (Pauley and McPherson, 2010), post-traumatic stress disorder (Thompson and Waltz, 2008), and eating disorders (Ferreira, Pinto-Gouveia, Duarte, 2013).
Based on this, it can be said that self-compassion is a variable that determines positive mental health (Neff, 2003a). This concept, introduced to the psychology literature by Neff (2003a), was measured only by the “Self-Compassion Scale” until 2011.
In the literature of our country, the “Self-Compassion Scale” has appeared as the Self-Understanding Scale (Deniz, Kesici, and Sümer, 2008) and the Self-Sensitivity Scale (Akın, Akın, and Abacı, 2007).
The Self-Compassion Scale Short Form was developed by Raes, Pommier, Neff, and Van Gucht (2011) as a more practical alternative to the long form in terms of time. It is recommended that this short form of the scale be used especially in studies where the total score will be used (Raes, Pommier, Neff, and Van Gucht, 2011). The Self-Compassion Scale Short Form consists of items taken from the 26-item Self-Compassion Scale (Neff, 2003b). In order to create the short form, two items for each subcomponent of self-compassion were included in the scale.
The positive subcomponents are Self-Kindness, Common Humanity, and Mindfulness, while the negative ones are Self-Judgment, Isolation, and Over-Identification. Thus, the 12-item Self-Compassion Scale Short Form (SCS-SF), consisting of both positive and negative components, was developed.
It is observed that this scale has recently started to be used in Western literature, particularly due to its ease of use (Neff et al., in press).
The aim of this study by Musa Yıldırım and Tuğba Sarı is to adapt the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form into Turkish and to examine its psychometric properties among adolescents. It is considered that introducing the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form to Turkish will offer convenience in terms of time during the implementation phase of research conducted with adolescents.
2.1.
Research Model The aim of this study is to adapt the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form, developed in Western literature, into Turkish and to examine its psychometric properties. In this context, the study encompasses the stages of scale adaptation.
2.2.
Study Group This research was conducted in middle and high schools in Sakarya Province during the 2016-2017 academic year. Data were collected from 800 students within the scope of the study. The gender, school type, and grade level characteristics of the study group are presented in Table 1.
Table 1.
Characteristics of the Study Group Examining the Psychometric Properties of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form Gender School Type Grade Level Middle School 400 (50%) 5th grade 100 (12.5%) 6th grade 100 (12.5%) Anatolian High School 174 (21.8%) 7th grade 100 (12.5%) 8th grade 100 (12.5%) Male 400 (50%) Science High School 100 (12.5%) 9th grade 100 (12.5%) Imam Hatip High School 74 (9.3%) 10th grade 100 (12.5%) Vocational and Technical Anatolian High School 52 (6.5%) 11th grade 100 (12.5%) 12th grade 100 (12.5%) Total 800 (100%) Total 800 (100%) Total 800 (100%) When Table 1 is examined, it is seen that the sample includes 400 (50%) female and 400 (50%) male students.
Of the students, 400 (50%) are attending middle school, 174 (21.8%) are at Anatolian High School, 100 (12.5%) are at Science High School, 74 (9.3%) are at Imam Hatip High School, and 52 (6.5%) are at Vocational and Technical Anatolian High School.
When considering the grade level characteristics of the sample, it is seen that 100 students (12.5%) from each grade level from 5th to 12th grade participated in the sample. In addition, in order to examine the test-retest reliability of the scale, data were collected twice, 17 days apart, from another sample group of 154 students (68 female, 86 male) attending a middle school, apart from the main sample of 800 students.
2.3.
Procedure To conduct the validity and reliability study of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form (SCS-SF), Kristin Neff was first contacted via email and the necessary permissions were obtained. Subsequently, the English version of the scale was sent to five faculty members working in the field of guidance and psychological counseling, and they were asked to translate the scale items.
The translated items received from the five faculty members were reviewed by the researchers, resulting in the formation of the Turkish version of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form.
Before the created Turkish form was administered to the sample group, it was reviewed by the researchers with two groups of 10 students each, comprised of middle school and high school students, to assess whether the scale items were understood. It was found that the scale items were sufficiently understood by both middle and high school students, and the scale was subsequently administered to the sample group.
The data obtained were analyzed using the SPSS 22.0 and Lisrel 8.72 software packages.
2.4.
Measurement Tools In this section, the Adolescent Subjective Well-Being Scale and the Fear of Compassion Scale, which were used to examine the criterion-related validity of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form, are introduced.
2.4.1.
Adolescent Subjective Well-Being Scale The Adolescent Subjective Well-Being Scale is a measurement tool developed by Eryılmaz (2009) for use in happiness research conducted with adolescents. The scale is a Likert-type instrument consisting of 15 items and 4 factors. Due to their structure, these factors are named “Satisfaction in Family Relationships,” “Satisfaction in Relationships with Significant Others,” “Life Satisfaction,” and “Positive Emotions.” Each item on the scale is scored from 1 (strongly disagree) to 4 (strongly agree). Higher scores obtained from the scale indicate a greater presence of the measured characteristic. This tool can be assessed based on the properties of its subscales or using the total score. There are no items on the scale that should be reverse scored. As a result of the reliability analysis, the Cronbach’s alpha coefficient was calculated as 0.83 for the Family Relationships subfactor, 0.73 for the Satisfaction in Relationships with Significant Others subfactor, 0.81 for the Life Satisfaction subfactor, and 0.66 for the Positive Emotions subfactor. In the current study, the Cronbach’s alpha coefficient for the entire scale was calculated as .894.
2.4.2.
Fear of Compassion Scale The Fear of Compassion Scale was developed by Gilbert, McEwan, Matos, and Rivis (2011) and adapted into Turkish by Necef and Deniz (2018). The scale measures fear of compassion in three subdimensions: “Fear of Expressing Compassion and Understanding to Others,” “Fear of Responding to Compassion from Others,” and “Fear of Expressing Compassion and Understanding to Oneself.” The scale is a Likert-type measurement tool consisting of a total of 35 items. Each subscale is scored separately. In this study, only the items in the subdimension “Fear of Expressing Compassion and Understanding to Oneself” were used. The construct validity of the scale was examined via Confirmatory Factor Analysis, and three items were removed from the scale because their factor loadings were below 0.30. Therefore, while the original scale contains 38 items, the Turkish version consists of 35 items.
As a result, the fit indices of the CFA are as follows: χ2 (552, N = 654) = 1631.68, χ2/df = 2.96, CFI = .90, IFI = .90, SRMR = .058, RMSEA = .055, 90% CI.
(.052 – .058).
The Cronbach’s alpha value was examined for the internal consistency coefficient of the scale, and accordingly, an alpha value of 0.83 was calculated for the Fear of Responding to Compassion from Others subscale, 0.93 for the Fear of Showing Understanding and Compassion to Oneself subscale, 0.83 for the Fear of Showing Understanding and Compassion to Others subscale, and 0.92 for the entire Fear of Compassion Scale.
For the criterion-related validity of the scale, the relationship between the scores obtained from the Fear of Compassion Scale and the scores obtained from the Self-Compassion Scale and the Life Satisfaction Scale was examined, and it was found that there was a significant relationship between these measures. In the present study, the internal consistency coefficient of the Fear of Showing Understanding and Compassion to Oneself subscale was calculated as 0.91. In addition, to test the validity of the scale in an adolescent sample, the factor structure of the scale was examined using Confirmatory Factor Analysis. The analysis showed that the chi-square value was statistically significant (χ2=494.49, N=800, p=0.00). When the fit indices of the CFA analysis were examined, the RMSEA was 0.075, RMSR was 0.070, GFI was 0.92, NFI was 0.97, CFI was 0.97, and NNFI was 0.97. Accordingly, it can be said that the RMSEA, RMSR, and GFI values are at an acceptable level, while the NFI, CFI, and NNFI values indicate an excellent fit. It was observed that the single factor found in both the original version of the Fear of Compassion Scale’s Fear of Showing Compassion to Oneself subscale (Gilbert, McEwan, Matos, and Rivis, 2011) and in the adaptation studies in Turkey (Necef and Deniz, 2018) was confirmed, and the factor loadings of the scale items ranged between 0.43 and 0.76. Thus, the Fear of Showing Compassion to Oneself subscale of the Fear of Compassion Scale can be considered a valid and reliable measurement tool for use with adolescents.
3.
FINDINGS 3.1.
Findings Regarding Exploratory Factor Analysis Factor analysis is a multivariate statistical method that aims to discover and identify a small number of conceptually meaningful and uncorrelated new variables by grouping together p related variables (Büyüköztürk, 2004). In Exploratory Factor Analysis, procedures are carried out with the aim of identifying factors based on the relationships among variables. In the present study, EFA was utilized to examine the factor structure of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form (SCS-SF). Before conducting EFA, the Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin (KMO) coefficient and Bartlett’s Test of Sphericity were used to assess the suitability of the dataset for factor analysis. Accordingly, it was found that the dataset had a KMO coefficient of .81 and a Bartlett’s Test of Sphericity χ2 value of 937.456 (p<0.05). Since the KMO coefficient was greater than .50 and the Bartlett’s Test of Sphericity was statistically significant, the dataset was considered suitable for EFA (Çokluk, Şekercioğlu, and Büyüköztürk, 2012). Thus, EFA was conducted, and due to the existence of relationships among the identified factors in the analysis, the oblimin (direct oblimin) method, which is among the oblique rotation methods, was employed (Tabachnick and Fidell, 2015). In the analysis, it was observed that the factor loading of Item 10 was below 0.30. As is known, the factor loading value is a coefficient that explains the relationship of the items with the factors, and it is expected to be greater than 0.30 (Büyüköztürk, 2004: 118; Floyd and Widaman, 1995). Based on this, Item 10 was excluded from the scale due to its low factor loading value, and the EFA was rerun. The findings obtained from this second analysis are presented in Table 2.
Turkish Adaptation of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form: Validity... Table 2.
Factor Structure of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form (SCS-SF) and Item Factor Loadings Two-Factor Structure N=461 Single-Factor Structure Items First Factor Second Factor Item 8 .577 .727 Item 1 .644 .726 Item 9 .551 .686 Item 11 .416 .669 Item 12 .571 .518 Item 4 .528 .480 Item 7 .555 .737 Item 3 .498 .717 Item 6 .611 .643 Item 2 .529 .637 Item 5 .499 .610 Explained Variance 29.88% 14.99% Total Explained Variance 44.87% When Table 2 is examined, it is seen that the positive items of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form are grouped under one factor, while the negative items are grouped under another, indicating that the SCS-SF has a two-factor structure. At the same time, it is also understood that all scale items can be gathered under a single factor.
Based on this, it can be said that the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form (SCS-SF) has a general factor, and this general factor consists of two subcomponents (Büyüköztürk, 2004: 127). The first factor explains 29.88% of the total variance, while the second factor explains 14.99% of the total variance. Thus, together, the two factors account for 44.87% of the total variance. When the factor loadings of the scale items are examined, it is observed that the factor loadings of the items loaded onto the first factor range from .480 to .727, while those loaded onto the second factor range from .610 to .737.
3.2.
Findings Regarding the Confirmatory Factor Analysis Confirmatory Factor Analysis is a method used to verify the extent to which data collected with a measurement tool fit the theoretical structure underlying the instrument (Aytaç and Ongen, 2012). In the present study, it was aimed to confirm the compatibility of the factor structure of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form (SCS-SF) with both theoretical foundations and the findings obtained from the Exploratory Factor Analysis. In this context, CFA was conducted on a data set of 339 individuals. The analysis showed that the chi-square value was statistically significant (χ2=90.05, N=339, p=0.00). When the fit indices of the CFA were examined, RMSEA=0.06, RMSR=0.095, GFI=0.96, NFI=0.91, CFI=0.95, and NNFI=0.94 were calculated. According to these values, the RMSR, GFI, NFI, and NNFI values for the data set indicated an excellent fit (Bentler and Bonett, 1980; Schermelleh-Engel and Moosbrugger, 2003; Marsh, Hau, Artelt, Baumert, and Peschar, 2006), while the RMSEA value was found to be at an acceptable level of fit (Byrne and Campbell, 1999). The factor loadings for the two-dimensional model obtained from the CFA are shown in Figure 1.
Musa YILDIRIM, Tuğba SARI Figure 1.
When Figure 1 is examined, it can be seen that the two-factor structure of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form (SCS-SF) is confirmed according to the results of the Confirmatory Factor Analysis.
It was found that the model derived from the Confirmatory Factor Analysis (CFA) was consistent with the model identified in the current study’s Exploratory Factor Analysis (EFA), with positive items grouped under one subscale and negative items under another subscale. Similarly, this finding is also consistent with the theoretical foundation of self-compassion (Bengtsson, Söderström & Terjestam, 2016; Costa et al., 2016; Neff et al., in press).
Thus, it has been confirmed that the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form (SCS-SF) demonstrates a two-component structure.
When the factor loadings obtained from the CFA are examined, it is seen that the factor loadings of the positive items range between .49 and .65, while the factor loadings of the negative items range between .50 and .73.
3.3.
Findings Related to Criterion-Related Validity In order to examine criterion-related validity, the relationship between the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form, the Subjective Well-Being Scale, and the Fear of Compassion Scale was investigated. To determine the appropriate method for analyzing the relationships between variables, the data set was tested for normality (Kilmen, 2015). The normality of the data set was examined using the Kolmogorov-Smirnov Test. The findings obtained are presented in Table 3.
Turkish Adaptation of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form: Validity... Table 3.

Therefore, it can be stated that the data obtained for all variables differ from normal distribution, in other words, the data are not normally distributed.
Therefore, Spearman Correlation Analysis, one of the nonparametric tests, was used to examine the relationships among the variables (Kilmen, 2015: 99). The results of the Spearman Correlation Analysis are presented in Table 4.

Spearman Correlation Analysis Results Regarding the Relationship Between Self-Compassion, Subjective Well-Being, and Fear of Compassion (N = 800) Variables 1. Self-Compassion 2. Subjective Well-Being 3. Fear of Compassion 1. Self-Compassion 1 .432* -.411* 2. Subjective Well-Being 1 -.292* 3. Fear of Compassion 1 According to Table 4, there is a moderate positive correlation between self-compassion and subjective well-being (r = .432, p < 0.01), and a moderate negative correlation between self-compassion and fear of compassion (r = -.411, p < 0.01).
3.4.
Findings Related to Internal Consistency To examine the internal consistency level of the scale, the Cronbach’s alpha value was assessed. As a result of the statistical analysis, the Cronbach’s alpha coefficient for the first subfactor of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form was calculated as 0.73, while the Cronbach’s alpha coefficient for the second subfactor was calculated as 0.71. The Cronbach’s alpha coefficient for the entire scale was calculated as 0.75.
3.5.
Findings Regarding Test-Retest Reliability In order to calculate the test-retest reliability of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form (SCS-SF), data were collected twice from a sample group with a 17-day interval, and the relationship between these two measurements was examined. Statistical analysis revealed a high level of correlation between the two measurements (r= .840, p<0.01).
Musa YILDIRIM, Tuğba SARI 4.
DISCUSSION AND CONCLUSION In this study, which examined the psychometric properties of the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form (SCS-SF) among Turkish adolescents, the structural validity, criterion-related validity, internal consistency coefficient, and test-retest reliability coefficient of the scale were analyzed. As a result of the Exploratory Factor Analysis (EFA) conducted to examine structural validity, it was observed that the factor loading of Item 10 was below 0.30, and thus this item was removed from the scale. According to the EFA, it was also determined that the SCS-SF has a general factor, and this general factor consists of two subcomponents (Büyüköztürk, 2004: 127). The negative items explain 29.88% of the total variance, while the second factor explains 14.99% of the total variance. Thus, together, the two factors explain 44.87% of the total variance. In the subsequent Confirmatory Factor Analysis (CFA), it was found that the original SCS-SF is represented by a model consistent with its theoretical foundation (Bengtsson, Söderström, and Terjestam, 2016; Costa et al., 2016; Neff et al., in press), in which the positive items load on one subdimension and the negative items on another. The results of the factor analyses confirmed that the SCS-SF has a structure consisting of a single factor made up of two complementary components (positive component and negative component). When examining the criterion-related validity of the scale, a negative and moderate relationship was found between the Self-Compassion Scale Short Form and the Fear of Compassion Scale’s “fear of showing compassion to oneself” subdimension, and a positive and moderate relationship was found with the Adolescent Subjective Well-Being Scale. Studies exploring the relationship between self-compassion and fear of compassion (Necef and Deniz, 2018) and subjective well-being (Zessin, Dickhäuser, and Garbade, 2015) show that the current findings are consistent with previously reported results. Additionally, data were collected from the same group 17 days apart with the SCS-SF, and a high level of correlation was found between the two measurements. This indicates that the SCS-SF measures a stable construct. With these results, the SCS-SF has become an 11-item, five-point Likert-type, self-report scale. Due to its brevity, ease of application and evaluation, it is considered that the scale will meet an important need in studies aimed at measuring self-compassion among adolescents in Turkish society. In conclusion, (1) the SCS-SF is a valid and reliable measurement tool for assessing self-compassion among middle and high school students in the Turkish cultural context, (2) the SCS-SF exhibits meaningful correlations with subjective well-being and fear of showing compassion to oneself, and (3) the SCS-SF has a unidimensional structure. It is recommended to use the total score of the SCS-SF in studies aiming to measure self-compassion.

Necatibey Faculty of Education, Electronic Journal of Science and Mathematics Education 790 Examining the Turkish Universities’ Distance Education Systems During the COVID-19 Pandemic Examining the Turkish Universities’ Distance Education Systems During the COVID-19 Pandemic Gürhan DURAK 1, Serkan ÇANKAYA 2, Serkan İzmirli 3, 1 Balıkesir University, Balıkesir, Turkey, gurhandurak@balikesir.edu.tr, http://orcid.org/0000-0003-2944-3713 2 İzmir Democracy University, İzmir, Turkey, serkan.cankaya@idu.edu.tr, http://orcid.org/0000-0002-3951-9809 3 Çanakkale Onsekiz Mart University, Çanakkale, Turkey, sizmirli@gmail.com, http://orcid.org/0000-0003-4507-6124 Submission Date: 27.05.2020 Acceptance Date: 07.06.2020 Doi: 10.17522/balikesirnef.743080 Abstract – The aim of this study is to examine the actions taken by universities in Turkey during the transition to emergency distance education in the Covid-19 pandemic period. The survey model was used in the research. The research population consists of all universities in Turkey (208 universities). Data related to each university were obtained from those responsible for the emergency distance education process (such as staff in distance education centers and information technology departments). In the study, data could be collected from 33 participants (universities) on a voluntary basis.
The data for the study were collected through an online survey form (Google Form). According to the findings obtained from the research, the most frequently used learning management systems are Moodle and ALMS. It was observed that the most commonly used live lecture software by universities were Big Blue Button and Perculus.
Despite the Higher Education Council's recommendation to conduct courses synchronously, only six universities have been able to run all their courses synchronously.
Most universities tried to manage the process through previously established learning management systems (f=29) and live lecture software (f=24). Approximately half of the universities tracked student attendance in classes.
Participants stated that training instructors in preparation for distance education was the most challenging aspect.
Keywords: COVID-19, distance education, emergency distance education, learning management system, live lesson software.
Corresponding author: Serkan Çankaya, Izmir Democracy University, Izmir, Turkey, serkan.cankaya@idu.edu.tr Introduction It is difficult to make long-term plans and implement them during times of crisis. The Covid-19 pandemic, which was effective worldwide at the beginning of 2020, is one such crisis. Due to the Covid-19 pandemic, educational institutions were closed in almost all parts of the world during the spring semester of the 2019-2020 academic year, and various levels of quarantine measures were implemented (Daniel, 2020).
In this context, various efforts have been made worldwide NEF-EFMED Volume 14, Issue 1, June 2020/ NFE-EJMSE Vol.
14, No.
Schools at all levels initiated the distance education process to prevent students from falling behind in their education. Consequently, every educator and learner, whether or not they had prior experience with distance education, suddenly found themselves having to teach or take classes via the distance education method.
Educational institutions and educators began seeking ways to conduct classes via distance education and how to use various online tools (Karip, 2020). In this context, it can be stated that, in terms of distance education, the world will no longer be the same as before. Most instructors had never used distance education tools prior to this (Çetinkaya Aydın, 2020). Considering that these instructors have limited knowledge and skills in distance education and in preparing distance education materials, it cannot be said that everything would proceed perfectly during distance education classes. Distance education is a scientific field with theoretical foundations, and successful distance education practices require planning that takes these foundations into account. Ideally, all instructors should take a course on the theoretical foundations of distance education, delivered through distance education tools themselves (Shattuck, Dubins, and Zilberman, 2011). In this way, instructors will both learn the theoretical foundations of distance education and gain experience as students in a distance education setting, which will enable them to anticipate what students might experience during this process.
As previously mentioned, it was not possible to conduct such preliminary work during this crisis period. Instead, educational institutions were only able to provide limited training for their instructors. These trainings generally focused on how to use distance education tools.
On the other hand, it can be anticipated that some instructors who had an unsuccessful experience with distance education during this period will undergo a negative shift in their attitudes (Gaeth, Levin, Sood, Juang, and Castellucci, 1997). In particular, instructors who struggle with using technological tools can be considered in this category.
Incorrect choices made by educational institutions (such as the selection of learning management systems and live lesson software) may also cause instructors to experience difficulties during this process.
Within the scope of this study, the distance education practices of universities in Turkey during the pandemic process were examined.
Universities in Turkey are affiliated with the Higher Education Council (YÖK). YÖK initially announced that universities would be closed for three weeks as of March 16, 2020. Subsequently, it instructed universities to provide courses via distance education and specified that face-to-face education would not be conducted in the spring semester of the 2019-2020 academic year. According to the decisions taken by YÖK, it was stated that universities needed to commence distance education to the extent of their capacities as of March 23, 2020. A portion of the press statement made by the President of YÖK regarding this matter is provided below (Saraç, 2020): Necatibey Faculty of Education, Electronic Journal of Science and Mathematics Education 792 Examining the Turkish Universities’ Distance Education Systems During the COVID-19 Pandemic • “On Monday, March 23, the distance education process will begin in all our universities that have digital facilities for distance education. • For universities that do not yet have this capacity, on the same day, March 23, an open course materials repository that we have prepared in a short period will be made available to all our universities. • In theoretically based courses within practice-oriented programs, digital facilities and distance teaching methods will be used; practical courses will be offered at the most appropriate time as determined by the universities themselves, including extending the calendar when necessary. • This approach and implementation at the associate and undergraduate level will also be available for the graduate level; provided that it is auditable, the use of distance education and digital facilities will ensure there is no disruption in these processes.” In his statement, YÖK President Saraç (2020) also noted that universities could use synchronous (real-time) or asynchronous (non-real-time) distance education methods. Given the difficulties of synchronous implementation in the existing environment, it was anticipated that universities, particularly in associate and undergraduate programs, would predominantly adopt asynchronous practices. However, the press release also stressed that more interactive synchronous methods would be encouraged by YÖK. In line with these decisions, all universities began efforts to ensure the continuation of all courses through distance education methods. Many universities completed these preparations within a week, and courses commenced via distance education as of March 23, 2020. Universities, depending on their infrastructure, employed “synchronous,” “asynchronous,” or “both synchronous and asynchronous” distance education methods. In fact, it can be anticipated that universities with insufficient infrastructure were required to make significant investments in hardware and software. At this point, it was also necessary to urgently train both faculty members and students who would use the system. As far as we observed, web pages introducing the distance education system, documents, and instructional videos were prepared and communicated to instructors and students. The preparation of a distance education system to serve the entire university and the provision of training related to the use of the system had to be carried out within just a few days. In the execution of all these processes, significant responsibilities fell primarily to the Distance Education Application and Research Centers (UZEM) and to the Information Technology Departments at the universities. UZEM units are organizations established to manage the distance education processes of universities.
In these units, distance education is generally provided at the associate, undergraduate, and non-thesis master's levels, and NEF-EFMED Volume 14, Issue 1, June 2020 / NFE-EJMSE Vol.
14, No.
Technical support, organization, and activities such as the preparation of distance education content are carried out in the implementation of various distance education certificate programs.
Purpose and Significance There is no research in the literature addressing the measures taken during the transition to emergency distance education in Turkey amid the Covid-19 process. In this context, the purpose of this study is to examine the activities undertaken by universities in Turkey during their transition to emergency distance education in the Covid-19 pandemic period. Collecting data on what and how universities implemented emergency distance education, as well as identifying which practices were effective and which were unsuccessful, is of great importance. Additionally, determining how prepared universities were for this process is also significant. It is believed that the findings of this study will reveal results that may help universities to be better prepared during such times of crisis.
Additionally, in order to emphasize the importance of the field of distance education, the graph showing the yearly changes in the number of studies on "distance education" included in the Scopus database can be examined (Figure 1).
Figure 1.
Distribution of Studies on Distance Education by Year As shown in Figure 1, the number of scientific studies on distance education has increased significantly over the years. This can be considered an indication that the importance of distance education is gradually increasing and that it will become an indispensable part of education. The pandemic period has further heightened the significance of distance education. During this pandemic, it can be stated that summarizing and presenting the efforts made by higher education institutions in Turkey in response to emergency distance education would contribute to the literature. Relevant Literature During the emergency distance education period, educational institutions used various learning management systems and live lesson software (synchronous virtual classroom software). There are numerous studies in the literature that examine live lesson software (Herand & Hatipoğlu, 2014; Işık, Karacı, Özkaraca & Biroğul, 2010; İzmirli & Akyüz, 2017; Lavolette, Venable, Gose & Huang, 2010; Schullo, Hilbelink, Venable & Barron, 2007; Yıldırım et al., 2011). In these studies, various synchronous tools such as Adobe Connect, Big Blue Button, Dimdim (no longer available), Blackboard Collaborate, Elluminate Live (now Blackboard Collaborate), Electa Live, GoToTraining, Microsoft Live Meetings (no longer available), Openmeetings, Perculus, VMukti, and WizIQ have been compared. Some of these tools have become outdated or have continued under different names. These studies comparing synchronous virtual classroom tools have identified the strengths and weaknesses of the tools relative to each other. It is emphasized that the selection of live lesson software should be based on specific requirements. For example, some free software may restrict the number of participants who can join the live lesson. It is also noted that the institution’s budget can influence the choice of live lesson software. Due to the sudden transition to distance education during the COVID-19 period, the importance of live lesson software has become evident.
In the literature, while no studies have been found regarding the preparedness of universities and other educational levels in Turkey for emergency distance education during the Covid-19 period, a limited number of studies have been identified in some other countries around the world. It is particularly noteworthy that, since the pandemic began in China, there are more studies on distance education conducted in China.
Zhou, Li, Wu, and Zhou (2020) examined the background, foundations, and impact of the online education implemented in China during the Covid-19 period through the nationwide campaign “School’s Out, But Class’s On.” Approximately 270 million primary, secondary, high school, and undergraduate students in China transitioned to online education. Many schools delivered online live lessons, on-demand teaching, and video-based instruction on TV. The ability to conduct large-scale emergency distance teaching in China demonstrates that the Internet infrastructure is fully established. In addition, a student-centered teaching model has been developed. However, it was also observed that some instructors transferred their face-to-face course materials to the online environment without adding any interactivity.
NEF-EFMED Volume 14, Issue 1, June 2020 / NFE-EJMSE Vol.
14, No.
Some studies conducted in China have specifically examined the practices implemented in certain middle schools during the Covid-19 pandemic period. Upon review of these practices, it has been observed that teachers sent home study plans to students and shared lesson materials with their students through various platforms. It has been determined that teachers, students, and parents connected and communicated with each other via online platforms such as QQ, WeChat, and DingTalk.
It can be stated that classes were generally asynchronous, while the communication process was synchronous (Cai and Wang, 2020; Dai and Lin, 2020; Xia, 2020; Xie and Yang, 2020).
(2020) examined the effects of the compulsory transition to distance education at universities in the United States from various perspectives. They presented the initial findings from survey results obtained from 967 university students and 111 faculty members as a technical report. They stated that during the pandemic, the vast majority of the 19.9 million students and 1.5 million faculty members at colleges and universities in the U.S. were forced to conduct classes via distance education. According to the initial findings of their research, students may experience problems with their internet connections. They advised faculty members to avoid conducting only live lectures, and if they do hold live lectures, to also share the recorded sessions with their students.
Method Research Model In this study, the distance education systems of universities in Turkey during the Covid-19 period were examined. For this purpose, the survey model was used to obtain the necessary information.
The study was reviewed by the Balıkesir University Social and Human Sciences Ethics Committee at its meeting dated 02/07/2020 and numbered 2020/7, and was found to be ethically appropriate (Document Date and Number: 03/07/2020-E.26016).
Data Collection Tool An online survey form (Google Form) was prepared for data collection. This survey form included questions regarding the participants' demographic information and the distance education systems of their respective universities. The survey form was prepared in consideration of the literature review and the statements issued by the Higher Education Council (YÖK).
Necatibey Faculty of Education, Electronic Journal of Science and Mathematics Education 796 Examining the Turkish Universities’ Distance Education Systems During the COVID-19 Pandemic Participants The study population consists of all universities in Turkey (208 universities).
The data for each university were obtained from individuals responsible during the emergency distance education process (such as staff in the Distance Education Center and the Information Technology Department).
Participants were contacted via WhatsApp, email, and personal networks.
There is a WhatsApp group in Turkey where universities affiliated with the Higher Education Council (YÖK) can exchange opinions regarding their distance education systems. Since one of the authors of this study is a director of the Distance Education Center (UZEM) at a university, they are a member of this WhatsApp group. Efforts were made to reach participants through this group. Initially, a message emphasizing the significance of the study, along with the survey participation form, was sent individually to participants. One week later, for those who had not yet participated in the survey, the survey participation link was resent together with another message underlining the importance of the topic. Additionally, the email addresses of the universities’ distance education units and the units managing distance education were obtained, and the survey participation link was sent to them via email in a similar manner.
As a result, feedback was received from 33 different universities.

Data Analysis The responses provided by the participants to the online survey were analyzed using descriptive statistics. The findings obtained are presented together with frequency values. Universities were matched with the data that generally stood out. In addition, efforts were made to determine which distance education software was preferred by the universities that were satisfied with and recommended their distance education systems.
Validity and Reliability Participants were informed that their names would be kept confidential and that the data collected would be used solely for academic purposes. Thus, it is believed that the participants were able to express their sincere opinions about the situation more comfortably. Reasons for Preferring Live Lesson Software Reason f The system was already in place before the pandemic 24 Ease of use 20 Being free of charge 12 University decision 10 Note.
Multiple options could be selected. As seen in Table 9, most universities again preferred to proceed with the live lesson software they had previously used. Ease of use and being free of charge are also among the other reasons for preferring live lesson software. It can be said that universities have tended to opt for paid software slightly more in the case of live lesson software. One reason for this is that a university wishing to use a free, open-source software such as Big Blue Button needs to set up a server for approximately 150–200 users.
In this case, the total server purchase costs can reach very high amounts depending on the number of students.
Free live lesson software can be seen as an ideal solution for universities with a low number of students.
Apart from these options, one participant explained their reason for preferring a particular live lesson software as follows: “Because it offered the most reasonable proposal, allows for recording of lessons, and is widely preferred.”
Additionally, one participant specifically stated that they prefer open-source software.

Four participants selected the “Not sure” option, while one participant stated that it should only be used in compulsory situations. In addition to these options, three participants expressed that distance education should be used solely to support face-to-face education. The other two participants indicated that it should become widespread if certain conditions are met.
One participant said, “It should become widespread, provided that adequate education and awareness are ensured and internet infrastructure problems are resolved,” while another participant stated, “Distance education is a system that, in addition to its advantages, also has disadvantages.”
In addition to infrastructure and connectivity issues, it is a practice that requires students to have a high level of self-discipline and self-regulation.
I think it is more appropriate to use it only when necessary under the current conditions, but I support its wider adoption as circumstances improve.

As seen in Table 15, about half of the participants (f=15) thought that students would be able to understand the subjects sufficiently with this system as well, while eight participants stated that they were not sure.
Four participants, however, believe that distance education cannot be the same as traditional education.
Some participants, aside from these options, emphasized the course itself, the instructor, and students’ intrinsic motivation, stating that it is difficult to make generalizations on this matter.
One participant stated, “Students who align more closely with the distance education profile (those who are intrinsically motivated, able to organize their own learning, and have high self-regulation skills) will have a more meaningful learning process.” Another participant expressed the view, “The field and content of the courses will be important, and I think it would be more appropriate to evaluate each course differently.” Another participant’s opinion on this matter was, “As long as course materials are prepared in a planned and timely manner and shared with students, it is more effective; I think alternative assessments have become increasingly important regarding evaluation.”
Although we still have a long way to go in terms of quality, I think that this mandatory process has quickly raised both faculty and student readiness to a higher level.
Participants were asked about the situations they found most challenging during the preparation process for distance education.

As seen in Table 16, one of the situations participants found most challenging (f=23) was the training process of the instructors. This situation can be considered normal, as many instructors were not prepared for this circumstance, and they were expected to teach courses via distance education within a very short period of time.
In addition, technical improvements necessary for the system to be used by a large number of users and the integration of different systems were also among the other significant challenges encountered. Furthermore, one participant stated that a live support unit was established to assist students and instructors due to the problems experienced.
In the survey, participants were asked, "Have faculty members received training on distance education systems?" It was observed that all participants answered "Yes" to this question. Participants were also asked, "Have students received training on the use of distance education systems?" On this question, only 2 participants selected "No," while all other participants (f=31) selected "Yes." Participants were asked whether faculty members were ready for distance education.
This finding is listed in Table 17.
Table 17.
Readiness of Faculty Members for Distance Education Readiness of Faculty Members for Distance Education f Yes 15 Not sure 10 Partially 6 No 2 As seen in Table 17, about half of the participants answered “yes” to this question, while the other half selected “not sure” and “no.” Some participants indicated that they were partially prepared. One participant stated the following on the subject: “Partially, because while some faculty members have prior experience, others do not. Some have a good level of technology use, while others do not. Those with a low level of IT (information technologies) usage experience difficulties.”
Conclusion and Discussion It is difficult to make long-term plans during crisis periods such as pandemics. The Covid-19 pandemic is one such crisis. During this period, universities were forced to hastily initiate distance education processes for all courses. While some universities that were not previously prepared experienced significant difficulties, it can be assumed that universities with the necessary preparations and infrastructure—in other words, those with vision—will emerge from this process even stronger. In this study, the views of participants who are administrators or staff responsible for distance education at universities were sought regarding what universities did during this process and the effects of these efforts on students and faculty members. The vast majority of participants are directors or employees of the Distance Education Application and Research Centers (UZEM) at universities. Since not all universities have a UZEM unit, in some universities this process was managed by other units such as the Department of Information Technologies or by established commissions.
The individuals contacted were involved in the operation of the distance education system.
14, No.
It is believed that the most accurate information regarding universities has been obtained, as the respondents are individuals who hold significant roles.
Herand and Hatipoğlu (2014) and İzmirli and Akyüz (2017) stated that institutions should establish their own priorities and make decisions regarding live class software based on their specific needs. During the pandemic, universities also tried to decide which systems to use according to their circumstances. The most widely used learning management systems in universities during the pandemic were Moodle and ALMS.
The most widely used live class software by universities are Big Blue Button and Perculus. Most participants stated that their universities continued with the existing learning management system and live class software during this process. Additionally, some participants mentioned that the ease of use and the fact that the system/software they use is free influenced their choices. The vast majority of participants indicated that they are satisfied with the learning management system and live class software they use and that they would recommend them. It was observed that in about half of the universities participating in the study, students’ class attendance is monitored.
However, the fact that some students do not have computers and experience problems with their internet connection has also been among the topics shared on social media.
In fact, several articles have been published indicating that the impact of the digital divide has deepened even further during this period.
For such students, monitoring attendance in live lessons, in fact, may lead to their failure. Therefore, it is considered inappropriate to impose a compulsory attendance requirement for live lessons.
The literature states that if live lessons are held, the recordings of these lessons should also be shared with students (Hammond et al., 2020).
The vast majority of the participants in the study stated that large-scale distance education practices implemented during the pandemic should become more widespread.
There are no participants who believe that it should not be more widely implemented.
Only one participant believes that remote education practices should be used only in mandatory situations. Additionally, some participants stated that remote education practices could also be used to support face-to-face education.
In universities in Turkey, remote education programs are generally offered at the associate, undergraduate, and non-thesis graduate levels. In a study conducted by İzmirli, Şahin İzmirli, and Çankaya (2019), it is stated that there are online remote non-thesis graduate programs in Turkey, thesis-based graduate programs are limited in number, and there are no doctoral programs.
In the study, the opinions of distance education administrators regarding online remote graduate education were sought. It was observed that the administrators expressed that thesis-based graduate and doctoral programs could be delivered remotely. In this context, it can be stated that with the experience gained after the Covid-19 process, online remote thesis-based graduate programs will become more widespread and doctoral programs will be introduced.
In this context, it can be stated that the procedures and principles regarding distance education in higher education institutions should be regulated by the Council of Higher Education (YÖK, 2015). In the study, it was observed that during the Covid-19 process, all universities provided training to their faculty members regarding distance education systems (by offering user manuals and/or videos). Similarly, in a study conducted by İzmirli and Kırmacı (2017) before the pandemic, it was found that most universities offering distance education also trained their faculty members on online teaching competencies. There are also online certificate programs on how distance teaching can be conducted (e.g.
Gülbahar and Karataş, 2016).
The literature indicates that, in addition to technical knowledge and skills, education regarding pedagogical knowledge and skills should also be provided within distance education systems (İzmirli and Kırmacı, 2017; Vlachopoulos, 2020). Approximately one-third of the participants reported that academic staff were not prepared for the distance education process or that they were unsure about their preparedness. Since the transition to distance education was made urgently due to the pandemic, a comprehensive in-service training on distance teaching may not have been organized. Furthermore, about one-third of the participants stated that students either did not sufficiently understand the courses delivered through distance education or they were unsure whether the courses were understood.
Recommendations Within the scope of this study, the following recommendations are offered to institutions providing distance education, instructors, and researchers: • It is important for the distance education system used by institutions to be simple, straightforward, and easy to use, in line with the “user-friendly” principle. • The study revealed that there are universities that have not conducted any live (synchronous) classes.
However, within the framework of the equity theory of distance education, it can be stated that in line with the principle that "the more equal learning opportunities are provided to distance learners compared to face-to-face learning experiences, the more equivalent the learning outcomes will be," live classes should also be included. However, it can be recommended that attendance should not be mandatory for live classes.
NEF-EFMED Volume 14, Issue 1, June 2020 / NFE-EJMSE Vol. 14, No.
It will be important for universities to establish a robust infrastructure for distance education support services and to provide students with continuous support opportunities.
In addition, in-service training on distance education can also be provided to academic staff. • It is recommended that the Council of Higher Education (YÖK) develop a national distance education system that can be used by all universities in Turkey. In this way, the large amounts of money paid for such systems will remain within the country and will help ensure equal opportunities in education. • Case studies can be designed to offer a detailed depiction of the emergency distance education process during the pandemic period. • For the evaluation of emergency distance education in universities during the pandemic, both quantitative and qualitative studies can be designed by collecting the views of all stakeholders (academic staff, students, and distance education administrators).

** Atatürk University, Faculty of Education, Department of Biology Education, Erzurum, Turkey; selamiy@atauni.edu.tr *** Alaaddin Keykubat University, Faculty of Education, Department of Primary School Teaching, Alanya, Turkey; cuneyt.capraz@alanya.edu.tr A Roadmap for Content Validity Used in Scale Development Studies * DOI number: 10.17556/erziefd.297741 Selami YEŞİLYURT **, Cüneyt ÇAPRAZ *** Date received: 13.03.2017 Date accepted: 26.01.2018 The aim of this study is to present a roadmap to researchers on how to conduct content validity studies by introducing content validity ratios and the content validity index. The roadmap is provided through an example study, taking into account the technique developed by Lawshe (1975) and the criteria specified by Ayre and Scally (2014). Twenty-six experts participated in the sample study. The "Expert Form," which evaluates 66 items, was used as the data collection tool.
Keywords: Content validity ratio, content validity index, content validity criterion, scale development, expert opinions. *The aim of this study is to provide researchers with a roadmap for conducting content validity studies by introducing content validity ratios and the content validity index.
** Atatürk University, Faculty of Education, Department of Biology Education, Erzurum, Turkey; selamiy@atauni.edu.tr *** Alaaddin Keykubat University Faculty of Education, Department of Primary School Teaching, Alanya, Turkey; cuneyt.capraz@alanya.edu.tr Selami YEŞİLYURT, Cüneyt ÇAPRAZ It is of great importance to accurately identify attitudes and opinions in education and psychology (Özbek, Kahya & Özgen, 2007; Yurdugül, 2005). This highlights the significance of scale development studies. However, the primary issue to be considered in scale development is the validity of the measurement tool (Brains, Willnat, Manheim & Rich, 2011).
Because validity is a crucial criterion for the credibility and generalizability of a scientific study (Basham & Sedlacek, 2009; Yıldırım & Şimşek, 2013). In validity studies, special attention should be paid to the consistency of the relationship between the scale to be developed and the characteristic intended to be measured. Through this type of study, known as content validity, the extent to which each item serves the purpose is revealed. In this way, a developed scale will contain items with strong representational power for the subject under study, rather than unrelated statements irrelevant to the topic (Ayre & Scally, 2014; Basham & Sedlacek, 2009; Brinkman, 2009; Frank-Stromberg & Olsen, 2004; Wilson, Pan, & Schumsky, 2012). In content validity studies, also called logical or rational validity studies (Shuttleworth, 2016), a preliminary study must be conducted in which sufficient expert opinions are obtained to determine the coverage power of the scale item for the feature intended to be measured (Allen & Yen, 2002; Basham & Sedlacek, 2009; Brinkman, 2009; Büyüköztürk, 2005; Ercan & Kan, 2004; Lawshe, 1975; McMillan & Schumacher, 2010; Otrar & Argın, 2015; Rubio et al., 2003). Because, in such preliminary studies, if the number of experts is sufficiently large (between 5-40), the validity of the scale to be prepared will also be high (Ayre & Scally, 2014; Lawshe, 1975; Veneziano & Hooper, 1997; Wilson et al., 2012). With content validity, after determining whether the scale items are a sample of the universe in which the researchers are interested, appropriate items within the scope can be written to form a scale (Cronbach & Meehl, 1951; Rubio et al., 2003). However, content validity studies based on expert opinions are qualitative studies (Shuttleworth, 2016). Therefore, the data obtained should be converted into quantitative data by calculating the content validity ratios (CVR) and content validity index (CVI) (Ayre & Scally, 2014; Büyüköztürk, 2005; Demiralp & Kazu, 2012; McGartland et al., 2003). After this process, referred to as content analysis, a usable scale with sufficient coverage can be prepared that has advantages such as increasing construct validity related to the construct the scale aims to measure, conducting preliminary item analyses, and controlling the number of item factors (Büyüköztürk, 2005; Demiralp & Kazu, 2012). Various techniques have been developed to determine content validity (Cohen, 1960; Davis, 1992; Lawshe, 1975; Lindell & Brandt, 1999; Lindell, Brandt, & Whitney, 1999; James, Demaree, & Wolf, 1993; Tinsley & Weiss, 1975). The most commonly used is the technique developed by Lawshe (1975) (Ayre & Scally, 2014; Veneziano & Hooper, 1997; Wilson et al., 2012). This technique stands out for its simplicity and ease of use compared to alternatives (Wilson et al., 2012). However, as will be discussed in detail in the methods section, Wilson and colleagues (2012) and Ayre and Scally (2014) noted deficiencies regarding the content validity criterion of this technique and revised the “Lawshe technique” accordingly. As a result, in developed scales, it is important to determine, based on expert opinions, whether the items in the scale are sufficient in covering and gathering the factual and/or judgmental data needed. The aim of this study is to present a roadmap for how content validity studies can be conducted by introducing content validity ratios and the content validity index through an exemplary study.
A Path for Content Validity Used in Scale Development Studies… The example study for which content analysis was conducted is a qualitative study through which theoretical processes and theoretical form were obtained. However, the qualitative data obtained were converted into quantitative data in order to test the consistency between expert opinions and to facilitate the analysis. Considering studies conducted on determining content validity (Ayre & Scally, 2014; Cohen, 1960; Davis, 1992; James, Demaree, & Wolf, 1993; Lawshe, 1975; Lindell & Brandt, 1999; Lindell, Brandt, & Whitney, 1999; Tinsley & Weiss, 1975; Veneziano & Hooper, 1997; Wilson et al., 2012), the technique developed by Lawshe (1975) stands out for its simplicity and usability (Ayre & Scally, 2014; Veneziano & Hooper, 1997; Wilson et al., 2012). This was taken into account in the study. In their review on content validity, Veneziano and Hooper (1997) demonstrated, along with their reasons, that the technique introduced by Lawshe (1975) provides objective and definitive results.
Subsequently, Wilson et al. (2012) and Ayre & Scally (2014) confirmed this situation through their studies. However, Wilson et al. (2012) and Ayre & Scally (2014) criticized the critical CVR values published by Lawshe (1975) regarding content validity criteria, and later recalculated and revised these values.
In this study, taking these criticisms into account, a roadmap for the recalculated content validity computations—which can be referred to as the "modified Lawshe technique"—is presented below with an example study.
In the study, the following stages were basically followed: 1. Formation of the expert group. 2. Preparation of the draft scale form and collection of expert opinions. 3. Data analysis.
a. Calculation of content validity ratios (CVR=Content Validity Ratio). b. Calculation of content validity index (CVI=Content Validity Index).
4.
Deciding whether each item should be included in the scale based on the content validity ratio and content validity index criteria (This section is presented in the findings section.).
2.1.
Formation of the Expert Group The quality and number of experts (between 5 and 40) play a significant role in obtaining objective results in the calculations to determine content validity (Ayre & Scally, 2014; Lawshe, 1975; Veneziano & Hooper, 1997; Wilson et al., 2012). This consideration was taken into account in the present study. The experts participating in the study were selected from among faculty members (26 individuals) working at the Faculty of Education, Department of Biology Education, Atatürk University, using a convenience sampling method to facilitate access. The distribution of the participating experts by gender and academic title is presented in Table 1.
Selami YEŞİLYURT, Cüneyt ÇAPRAZ Table 1.
Distribution of the Experts Participating in the Study by Gender and Title Academic Title Prof. Assoc. Prof. Lecturer Gender Member (Dr.) Total Total 5 9 8 2 2 26 2.2.
Preparation of the Draft Scale Form and Collection of Expert Opinions In the Lawshe (1975) technique, expert opinions for each item are rated using three categories: “the item measures the intended construct,” “the item is related to the construct but is unnecessary,” and “the item does not measure the intended construct.” However, in this study, the rating categories for expert opinions found in Lawshe’s (1975) technique were reorganized as “Appropriate,” “Appropriate but Needs Revision,” and “Should be Removed.” Similar rating categorizations are present in the literature (Alisinanoğlu & Şimşek, 2013; Ateş Çobanoğlu, 2013). In the sample study, the expert form to be used as the data collection tool consisted of a total of 69 items. Of these, 66 items were aimed at determining content validity, while the remaining 3 collected demographic characteristics of the experts. The experts were asked to select one of the above rating categories for each of the 66 items on the form. In calculating the content validity ratios for the scale, “Appropriate” was scored as 3, “Appropriate but Needs Revision” as 2, and “Should be Removed” as 1. Additionally, beyond the standard rating in the Lawshe (1975) technique, the researchers requested that for every item an expert marked as needing revision, they answer the question “If your answer is that the item should be revised, what is your suggestion for improvement?”; and for every item marked for removal, “If your answer is that the item should be removed, why?” Thus, experts were asked to provide their opinions in writing for each item as needed.
2.3.
Data Analysis In the study, in order to determine the content validity of the items to be included in the scale, the qualitative data obtained based on expert opinions were converted into quantitative data by calculating CVR and CVI. In this conversion process, CVR was calculated first, followed by CVI. The calculations were performed using Microsoft Excel 2013 (Figure 1).
2.3.a.
Calculation of Content Validity Ratio (CVR) CVR is an item statistic based on content validity regarding whether certain items should be included in the scale or not, and it is calculated using the following formula (Lawshe, 1975): (Equation 1) Here, Nu represents the number of experts who rated the item as “Appropriate,” and N indicates the total number of experts who provided their opinion on the item. The CVR value ranges between -1 (absolute rejection) and +1 (absolute acceptance). If all participants rate any item in the scale as “Appropriate,” the CVR value for that item becomes 1. However, considering the possibility of error or chance, this value is accepted as 0.99.
In this case, if more than half of the participants rate any item on the scale as “Appropriate,” the Content Validity Ratio (CVR) will take a value between 0 and 0.99. According to the CVR equation: if half of the experts give an “Appropriate” rating to an item, CVR=0; if more than half provide an “Appropriate” rating, CVR>0; and if less than half provide an “Appropriate” rating, CVR<0. If the CVR is zero (0) or negative (less than zero), the item is considered to lack content validity. Therefore, such items are directly eliminated from the scale (Ayre & Scally, 2014; Lawshe, 1975; Wilson et al., 2012). According to Lawshe (1975), for each item with a positive value, the content validity criterion (CVRcritical) at the significance level α=0.05 should be checked. CVRcritical is the minimum required CVR value to ensure that the rate of “Appropriate” ratings for each item is not due to chance, and it is therefore used to determine whether an item is truly appropriate for inclusion.
CVR values are used to determine the minimum number of experts who must approve an item in order to decide whether it is necessary or unnecessary. However, the CVR value varies depending on the number of experts.
Lowell Schipper calculated these critical values according to certain criteria, and Lawshe (1975), utilizing Schipper’s calculations, prepared a table of Minimum/Critical CVR values. However, in his study, Lawshe did not provide information on how the values in this table were calculated (Ayre & Scally, 2014; Lawshe, 1975; Veneziano & Hooper, 1997; Wilson et al., 2012). Later, Wilson et al. (2012) identified a calculation error in the table published by Lawshe (1975) and recalculated these critical CVR values (CVRcritical) using different methods. Ayre and Scally (2014) reviewed both the critical CVR value studies conducted by Wilson et al. (2012) and those by Lawshe (1975). In this evaluation, based on the critical value calculations made by Wilson et al. (2012), they argued that it is not possible to decide whether items are necessary or unnecessary using these critical values and that these critical values are not reliable. On the other hand, they noted that the critical values shown in Lawshe’s (1975) article are reliable for making decisions about items, but they are not sufficient. Unlike Lawshe, Ayre and Scally (2014) emphasized that even if the number of experts participating in the study increases or decreases by just one, the critical CVR values would change. They prepared a new table of minimum/critical CVR values (CVRcritical) for different numbers of experts at α=0.05 significance level, using various statistical analyses (Table 2). As a result, considering the arguments put forth in the studies on CVRcritical by Lawshe (1975), Wilson et al. (2012), and Ayre & Scally (2014), it was decided that the CVRcritical values established by Ayre & Scally (2014) would be used as the basis in the sample study. In light of this basis, Table 2 shows that, at a significance level of α=0.05, the CVRcritical value for 26 experts is 0.385.
2.3.b.
Calculation of Content Validity Index (CVI) The Content Validity Ratio (CVR) is a statistical tool used for the acceptance or rejection of specific items. Once items are defined to be included in the scale through the determination of CVR, the CVI is then calculated for the entire test. In this case, the mean of the CVR values of the items deemed appropriate for inclusion on the scale is calculated to obtain the CVI value. It is important to note that the content validity index should not be confused with the correlation coefficient. The CVI represents the degree of overlap observed between the functional capacity of a defined job performance domain and the performance observed in the test under investigation. Functionally, the CVI is the average percentage of overlap between the test items and the job performance domain (Lawshe, 1975).
Selami YEŞİLYURT, Cüneyt ÇAPRAZ Yurdugül (2005) stated that it is important to determine in how many dimensions the characteristic to be measured is gathered, and emphasized that if the characteristic is collected in more than one dimension, a CVI should be obtained for each dimension. Taking this emphasis into account, under the assumption that the sample scale used in the study has only one dimension, the CVI value was calculated as 0.691 for that single dimension (Table 3).
Table 2. Minimum/Critical Values of CVRs at α=0.05 Significance Level (CVR = CVRcritical) (Ayre & Scally, 2014).
